--- Page 1 ---
As a scholarly translator of medieval and Renaissance Latinity, I have reviewed the provided OCR transcription of the 1897 Elliot Stock edition of Boethius. While much of the introductory material consists of English library metadata and a Greek passage from Aristotle, the Latin text appears on Pages 8 and 9. 

Below is the translation and scholarly apparatus for the Latin sections.

***

### PAGE 8

**Latin Inscription (Frontispiece):**
> NARIVS MANLIVS BOETHIVS VIR CLARISSIMVS ET INLVSTRIS EX PRAEFECTVS PRAETORIO PRAEFECTVS VRBIS ET COMES CONSVL ORDINARIVS ET PATRICIVS

**Translation:**
Narius Manlius Boethius, a man most distinguished [*Vir Clarissimus*] and illustrious, former Praetorian Prefect, Prefect of the City, and Count, Ordinary Consul, and Patrician.

**Scholarly Notes:**
*   **NARIVS MANLIVS BOETHIVS:** Proper noun. Historically identifying the father of Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius (the author).
*   **VIR CLARISSIMVS ET INLVSTRIS:** These are formal late-Roman rank designations. *Vir Clarissimus* (v.c.) was the standard title for a member of the senatorial order; *Illustris* was a higher grade of the same.
*   **EX PRAEFECTVS PRAETORIO:** The OCR reads "EX PRAEFECTVS." In classical epigraphy, *ex* denotes a former holder of the office (*ex-prefect*). Note that *praefectus* here retains the nominative case despite the preposition *ex*, a common feature in later Latin titles.
*   **CONSVL ORDINARIVS:** The "Ordinary Consul," referring to those who took office on the Kalends of January and gave their name to the year, as opposed to *suffect* consuls.

***

### PAGE 9

**Latin Epigraphs (Title Page):**

> *Quantumlibet igitur sæviant mali, sapienti tamen corona non decidet, non arescet.*

**Translation:**
To whatever extent the wicked may therefore rage, the crown of the wise man shall nonetheless not fall, nor shall it wither.

**Scholarly Notes:**
*   **Source:** Boethius, *De Consolatione Philosophiae*, Book IV, Prosa 3. 
*   **sæviant:** Present subjunctive, expressing a concession ("though they may rage").

> *Melioribus animum conformaveris, nihil opus est judice præmium deferente, tu te ipse excellentioribus addidisti ; studium ad pejora deflexeris, extra ne quæsieris ultorem, tu te ipse in deteriora trusisti.*

**Translation:**
[If] you have conformed your mind to better things, there is no need for a judge to bestow a reward; you have, by your own hand, joined yourself to more excellent things. [If] you have turned your zeal toward worse things, seek no avenger from without; you have, by your own hand, thrust yourself into lower conditions.

**Scholarly Notes:**
*   **Source:** Boethius, *De Consolatione Philosophiae*, Book IV, Prosa 4.
*   **conformaveris / deflexeris:** Future perfect indicatives (or perfect subjunctives) used here in a conditional sense.
*   **tu te ipse:** A double emphatic construction ("you yourself [have added] your own self"), emphasizing the Stoic and Neoplatonic concept of self-determined moral status.

***

### ADDITIONAL TEXTUAL NOTES

**Page 5 (Greek passage):**
Though not Latin, for the sake of completeness in this scholarly review, the Greek quote from **Aristotle’s *Ethics* (I. xi. 12)** translates as: 
*"Yet even in these [adversities] nobility shines through, when one bears many great misfortunes with gentle temper, not through insensibility, but by being noble and high-minded [*megalopsychos*]."*

**Abbreviations and OCR Anomalies:**
*   **Page 8:** The OCR "INLVSTRIS" uses the 'V' for 'U' (*illustris*), standard in both Roman inscriptions and 19th-century academic printing of Latin.
*   **Page 9:** The use of the ligature **æ** in *sæviant*, *præmium*, and *pejora* (historically *peiora*) reflects the orthographic conventions of the 1897 publication date.

### Scholarly Transcription and Annotated Translation

**Note on Source Material:** The provided text is an English-language Preface and Proem to a late 19th-century edition of Boethius’ *De Consolatione Philosophiae*. While the body text is English, it contains specific Latin terminology and historical references pertinent to the scholarly study of the period.

---

**PAGE 11**

[Header: PREFACE.]

[Decorative Woodcut Initial ‘T’]

THE book called ‘The Consolation of Philosophy’ was throughout the Middle Ages, and down to the beginnings of the modern epoch in the sixteenth century, the scholar’s familiar companion. Few books have exercised a wider influence in their time. It has been translated into every European tongue, and into English nearly a dozen times, from King Alfred’s paraphrase to the translations of Lord Preston, Causton, Ridpath, and Duncan, in the eighteenth century. The belief that what once pleased so widely must still have some charm is my excuse for attempting the present translation. The great work of Boethius, with its alternate prose and verse, skilfully fitted together...

---

**PAGE 12**

[Header: Preface. | Original Page vi]

...like dialogue and chorus in a Greek play, is unique in literature, and has a pathetic interest from the time and circumstances of its composition. It ought not to be forgotten. Those who can go to the original will find their reward. There may be room also for a new translation in English after an interval of close on a hundred years.

Some of the editions contain a reproduction of a bust purporting to represent Boethius. Lord Preston’s translation, for example, has such a portrait, which it refers to an original in marble at Rome. This I have been unable to trace, and suspect that it is apocryphal. The Hope Collection at Oxford contains a completely different portrait in a print, which gives no authority. I have ventured to use as a frontispiece a reproduction from a plaster-cast in the Ashmolean Museum, taken from an ivory diptych preserved in the Bibliotheca Quiriniana at Brescia, which represents Narius [1] Manlius Boethius, the father of the philosopher. Portraiture of this period is so rare that it seemed that, failing a likeness of the author himself, this authentic...

---

**PAGE 13**

[Header: Preface. | Original Page vii]

...representation of his father might have interest, as giving the consular dress and insignia of the time, and also as illustrating the decadence of contemporary art. The consul wears a richly-embroidered cloak; his right hand holds a staff surmounted by the Roman eagle, his left the *mappa circensis* [2], or napkin used for starting the races in the circus; at his feet are palms and bags of money—prizes for the victors in the games. For permission to use this cast my thanks are due to the authorities of the Ashmolean Museum, as also to Mr. T. W. Jackson, Curator of the Hope Collection, who first called my attention to its existence.

I have to thank my brother, Mr. L. James, of Radley College, for much valuable help and for correcting the proof-sheets of the translation. The text used is that of Peiper [3], Leipsic, 1874.

---

**PAGE 15**

[Header: PROEM.]

[Decorative Woodcut Initial ‘A’]

ANICIUS MANLIUS SEVERINUS BOETHIUS lived in the last quarter of the fifth century A.D., and the first quarter of the sixth. He was growing to manhood, when Theodoric, the famous Ostrogoth, crossed the Alps and made himself master of Italy. Boethius belonged to an ancient family, which boasted a connection with the legendary glories of the Republic, and was still among the foremost in wealth and dignity in the days of Rome’s abasement. His parents dying early, he was brought up by Symmachus, whom the age agreed to regard as of almost saintly character, and afterwards became his son-in-law. His varied gifts, aided by an excellent education, won for him the...

---

**PAGE 16**

[Header: Proem. | Original Page x]

...reputation of the most accomplished man of his time. He was orator, poet, musician, philosopher. It is his peculiar distinction to have handed on to the Middle Ages the tradition of Greek philosophy by his Latin translations of the works of Aristotle. Called early to a public career, the highest honours of the State came to him unsought. He was sole Consul in 510 A.D., and was ultimately raised by Theodoric to the dignity of *Magister Officiorum* [4], or head of the whole civil administration. He was no less happy in his domestic life, in the virtues of his wife, Rusticiana, and the fair promise of his two sons, Symmachus and Boethius; happy also in the society of a refined circle of friends. Noble, wealthy, accomplished, universally esteemed for his virtues, high in the favour of the Gothic King, he appeared to all men a signal example of the union of merit and good fortune. His felicity seemed to culminate in the year 522 A.D., when, by special and extraordinary favour, his two sons, young as they were for so exalted an honour, were created joint Consuls and rode to the senate-house...

---

**PAGE 17**

[Header: Proem. | Original Page xi]

...attended by a throng of senators, and the acclamations of the multitude. Boethius himself, amid the general applause, delivered the public speech in the King’s honour usual on such occasions. Within a year he was a solitary prisoner at Pavia, stripped of honours, wealth, and friends, with death hanging over him, and a terror worse than death, in the fear lest those dearest to him should be involved in the worst results of his downfall. It is in this situation that the opening of the ‘Consolation of Philosophy’ brings Boethius before us. He represents himself as seated in his prison distraught with grief, indignant at the injustice of his misfortunes, and seeking relief for his melancholy in writing verses descriptive of his condition. Suddenly there appears to him the Divine figure of Philosophy, in the guise of a woman of superhuman dignity and beauty, who by a succession of discourses convinces him of the vanity of regret for the lost gifts of fortune, raises his mind once more to the contemplation of the true good, and makes clear to him the mystery of the world’s moral government.

---

### Scholarly Notes and Translations of Latin Terms

[1] **Narius Manlius Boethius**: Likely an OCR error or variant for *Nar. Manlius Boethius*. The father of the philosopher was *Flavius Nar. [Nartius] Manlius Boethius*, Consul in 487 A.D.

[2] ***mappa circensis***: Translated literally as "circus cloth." In Roman tradition, this was a white linen cloth dropped by the presiding magistrate (in this case, the Consul) to signal the start of the chariot races.

[3] **Peiper**: Refers to Rudolf Peiper, whose 1871 Teubner edition (*Boetii De consolatione philosophiae libri quinque*) was the standard critical text for late 19th-century scholars.

[4] ***Magister Officiorum***: Translated as "Master of Offices." This was one of the most senior administrative roles in the late Roman and Ostrogothic bureaucracy, acting as a sort of prime minister or head of the civil service and palace guard.

->Preface.<-

...like dialogue and chorus in a Greek play, is unique in literature, and has a moving <note>original: "pathetic"; here meaning evoking a sense of pity or sorrow given Boethius wrote while awaiting execution</note> interest from the time and circumstances of its composition. It ought not to be forgotten. Those who can go to the original Latin will find their reward. There may be room also for a new translation in English after an interval of close on a hundred years.

Some of the editions contain a reproduction of a bust purporting to represent Boethius. Lord Preston’s translation, for example, has such a portrait, which it refers to an original in marble at Rome. This I have been unable to trace, and suspect that it is not genuine <note>original: "apocryphal"</note>. The Hope Collection <note>A significant collection of portraits and prints housed at the University of Oxford</note> at Oxford contains a completely different portrait in a print, which gives no authority. I have ventured to use as a frontispiece a reproduction from a plaster-cast in the Ashmolean Museum <note>The University of Oxford’s museum of art and archaeology, the oldest public museum in the UK</note>, taken from an ivory diptych <note>A two-paneled folding tablet, often carved from ivory to commemorate a Roman Consul's year of office</note> preserved in the Quiriniana Library <note>original: "Bibliotheca Quiriniana"</note> at Brescia, which represents Narius Manlius Boethius, the father of the philosopher. Portraiture of this period is so rare that it seemed that, failing a likeness of the author himself, this authentic...

->PREFACE<-

[[image: Decorative woodcut initial 'A' with elaborate floral and leaf patterns.]]ANICIUS MANLIUS SEVERINUS BOETHIUS lived in the last quarter of the fifth century A.D., and the first quarter of the sixth. He was growing to manhood when Theodoric <note>Theodoric the Great, the King of the Ostrogoths who conquered Italy in 493 A.D.</note>, the famous Ostrogoth, crossed the Alps and made himself master of Italy. Boethius belonged to an ancient family <note>The Anician family, one of the most prestigious noble houses of the later Roman Empire</note>, which boasted a connection with the legendary glories of the Republic, and was still among the foremost in wealth and dignity in the days of Rome’s abasement <note>A reference to the decline and political fragmentation of the Western Roman Empire</note>. His parents dying early, he was brought up by Symmachus <note>Quintus Aurelius Memmius Symmachus, a respected Roman statesman and scholar</note>, whom the age agreed to regard as of almost saintly character, and afterwards became his son-in-law. His varied gifts, aided by an excellent education, won for him the...

reputation of the most accomplished man
of his time. He was an orator, poet, musician, and

to have handed on to the Middle Ages

Latin translations of the works of Aristotle <note>Boethius intended to translate all of Aristotle and Plato into Latin to show their fundamental agreement, though he was executed before he could finish.</note>.
Called early to a public career, the highest

and was ultimately raised by Theodoric

head of the whole civil administration.
He was no less happy in his domestic life,
in the virtues of his wife, Rusticiana, and
the fair promise of his two sons, Sym-
machus and Boethius; happy also in the
society of a refined circle of friends.
Noble, wealthy, accomplished, universally
esteemed for his virtues, high in the favour
of the Gothic King, he appeared to all
men a signal example of the union of
merit and good fortune. His felicity
seemed to culminate in the year 522 A.D.,
when, by special and extraordinary favour,
his two sons, young as they were for so
exalted an honour, were created joint
Consuls and rode to the senate-house

Philosophy, in the form of a woman of
extraordinary dignity and beauty, who through a
series of conversations convinces him of
how pointless it is to regret the lost gifts of
fortune, raises his mind once more to the
study of the true good, and explains
to him the mystery of the world’s
moral government.

### PAGE 23
**BOOK I.**  
**THE SORROWS OF BOETHIUS.**

**SUMMARY.**

Boethius’ lament (*Song I*). — Ch. I. Philosophy appears to Boethius, banishes the Muses of Poetry, and herself laments (*Song II*) the disordered state of his mind. — Ch. II. Boethius is struck dumb with astonishment. Philosophy wipes away the tears that have clouded his vision. — Ch. III. Boethius recognizes his nurse, Philosophy. In response to his wondering inquiries, she explains her presence and reminds him of the persecutions to which Philosophy has often been subjected by an ignorant world. — Ch. IV. Philosophy commands Boethius to disclose his grief. He recounts the history of his unjust accusation and downfall. He concludes with a prayer (*Song V*) that the moral order in human affairs be restored. — Ch. V. Philosophy admits the justice of his defense, but grieves for the disturbance of his mind. She intends to soothe his spirit with gentle remedies. — Ch. VI. Philosophy examines Boethius' mental state through questioning and identifies three causes of his soul's malady: (1) He has forgotten his true nature; (2) he knows not the end of the universe; (3) he knows not how the world is governed.

---

### PAGE 25
**BOOK I. SONG I.**  
**[METRUM I: *Carmina qui quondam studio florente peregi*]**

**BOETHIUS’ COMPLAINT.**

I, who once composed rhythmic measures with flourishing zeal,  
Am now compelled, alas, to begin mournful songs in tears.  
Behold, the Muses, their hair disheveled, dictate what I must write,  
And elegies of true sorrow moisten my face.  
These at least could not be overcome by fear;  
They accompany me as comrades on my journey.  
They were once the glory of my happy and green youth;  
Now they provide solace in the fates of a wretched old man.  
For old age has come unawares, accelerated by evils,  
And grief has commanded its own season to inhabit me.  
Premature white hairs are scattered upon my head,  
And my skin trembles, loose upon an exhausted frame.

---

### PAGE 26
**[SONG I CONTINUED]**

Happy is that death which does not intrude upon sweet years,  
But comes, often invoked, to those who weep.  
Alas, how death turns a deaf ear to the wretched,  
Cruelly refusing to close eyes that overflow with tears.  
While deceptive Fortune favored me with fleeting goods,  
The hour of death had nearly submerged my head.  
Now, since she has changed her deceptive countenance and clouded over,  
Impious life drags out its weary delays.  
Why, O my friends, did you so often boast that I was fortunate?  
He who has fallen was never standing on firm ground.

---

### PAGE 28
**[PROSA I: *Haec dum mecum tacitus ipse reputarem*]**

**I.**

While I was silently turning these things over within myself and recording my tearful complaint with the aid of my pen, there appeared to me, standing above my head, a woman of an exceedingly venerable countenance. Her eyes were glowing with fire and keen beyond the usual power of men; her complexion was full of life, her strength inexhaustible, though she was so full of years that she was clearly not believed to be of our age. 

Her stature was of an ambiguous measure. For at one moment she contained herself within the common human height, while at another she seemed to strike the very heavens with the crown of her head; and when she raised her head higher, she even penetrated the sky, frustrating the gaze of those looking upon her. Her garments were woven of the finest threads and indissoluble material, of sophisticated workmanship; these she had woven with her own hands, as I later learned from her own speaking. 

---

### PAGE 29
**[PROSA I CONTINUED]**

The appearance of these garments was shadowed by a certain neglect and the gloom of antiquity, much like the smoky statues of ancestors. On the lowest hem was read the Greek letter **Π** [Pi], and on the uppermost, the letter **Θ** [Theta] was perceived.* And between the two letters, steps were seen marked out like a ladder, by which there might be an ascent from the lower element to the higher. Yet the hands of certain violent men had torn this same garment and had carried away such fragments as each was able to seize.† In her right hand she carried books, and in her left she bore a scepter. 

When she saw the Muses of Poetry standing near my bed and suggesting words to my weeping, she was moved for a moment, and with eyes flashing sternly, she cried: "Who has permitted these stage-prostitutes to approach this sick man? They possess no remedies for his pains, but rather nourish them with sweet poisons. These are they who kill the fruitful harvest of reason with the sterile thorns of the passions; they accustom the minds of men to disease, they do not liberate them."

---
* *Note: Π (P) signifies Praxis (the Practical/Political life); Θ (Th) signifies Theoria (the Contemplative/Theoretical life).*
† *Note: Refers to the various philosophical sects (Stoics, Epicureans) who tore fragments of truth from the whole of Philosophy.*

---

### PAGE 30
**[PROSA I CONTINUED]**

"But if your blandishments were leading away some common man, as is your custom, I should think it less a matter for indignation; for in such a one, my labors would not be harmed. But this man has been nourished on the philosophies of the Eleatics and the Academics. Begone, you Sirens, sweet even unto destruction! Leave him to my Muses to be tended and healed." 

At these rebukes, that crowd of Muses, cast down in sadness and with faces lowered in shame, confessed their blushing and departed the threshold. But I, whose sight was dimmed by flooding tears so that I could not discern who this woman of such commanding authority might be, was struck with amazement; and with my gaze fixed upon the ground, I began to wait in silence for what she might do. Then, drawing nearer, she sat upon the edge of my bed, and looking upon my face—heavy with grief and cast down in sorrow to the earth—she...

-># BOOK I.<-

->## THE SORROWS OF BOETHIUS.<-

->SUMMARY.<-

Boethius’ complaint <note>original: "complaint." This refers to Song I, the opening poem where Boethius expresses his initial despair in exile.</note> (**Song I**).—**Ch. I.** Philosophy <note>Lady Philosophy is personified here as a woman of majestic presence who represents the culmination of human reason and wisdom.</note> appears to Boethius, drives away the **Muses of Poetry** <note>Philosophy famously dismisses the Muses as "stage-muses" or "hysterical sluts" because they merely fuel Boethius's sorrow instead of offering a cure.</note>, and herself laments (**Song II**) the disordered condition of his mind.—**Ch. II.** Boethius is speechless with amazement. Philosophy wipes away the tears that have clouded his eyesight.—**Ch. III.** Boethius recognises his mistress Philosophy. To his wondering inquiries she explains her presence, and recalls to his mind the persecutions to which **Philosophy** has oftentimes from of old been subjected by an ignorant world <note>This likely refers to historical figures like Socrates, who was executed for his teachings, or Anaxagoras, who was exiled.</note>.—**Ch. IV.** Philosophy bids Boethius declare his griefs. He relates the story of his unjust accusation <note>Boethius, a high-ranking official, was accused of treason and magic against the Ostrogothic King Theodoric.</note> and ruin. He concludes with a prayer (**Song V**) that the moral disorder in human affairs may be set right.—**Ch. V.** Phi-

->**I**<-

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the summary of Book I from the previous page, completing the sentence regarding Chapter V and transitioning into the diagnosis of Boethius's spiritual condition in Chapter VI.</meta>

->*The Consolation of Philosophy.*<-

losophy <note>original: "losophy," completing the word "Philosophy" from the end of the previous page.</note> admits the justice of Boethius’ **self-vindication** <term>self-vindication: the act of justifying one's actions or proving one's innocence against accusations.</term>, but grieves rather for the unhappy change in his mind. She will first tranquillize his spirit by soothing remedies.

**Chapter VI.** Philosophy tests Boethius’ mental state by certain questions, and discovers three chief causes of his **soul’s sickness** <term>soul’s sickness: a metaphorical "illness" where the mind is so clouded by grief and worldly loss that it can no longer perceive the truth.</term>: 

(1) He has forgotten his own true nature; 
(2) he knows not the end towards which the whole **universe** <term>universe: the entirety of the created world, which Philosophy argues is governed by a specific purpose or "end."</term> tends; 
(3) he knows not the means by which the world is governed.

->**I**<-

-># BOOK I.<-

->SONG I.<-

->BOETHIUS’ COMPLAINT.<-

**W**HO wrought my **studious numbers** <term>studious numbers: a poetic way of referring to rhythmic, metrical verses written during his life as a scholar and philosopher.</term>
Smoothly once in happier days,
Now perforce in tears and sadness
Learn a mournful strain to raise.
Lo, the **Muses** <term>Muses: the nine goddesses of inspiration in Greek and Roman mythology. Boethius portrays them here as weeping alongside him, shifting from inspiring his academic work to inspiring his funeral-like laments.</term>, grief-dishevelled,
Guide my pen and voice my woe ;
Down their cheeks unfeigned the tear
drops
To my sad complainings flow !
These alone in danger’s hour
Faithful found, have dared attend
On the footsteps of the **exile** <note>original Latin: "exulis." Boethius was charged with treason and sent into exile in Pavia, away from his home in Rome, before his eventual execution.</note>
To his lonely journey’s end.

These that were the pride and pleasure

<meta>This page transitions from the opening metrical poem (a lament) into the first prose section of the book. ->*The Consolation of Philosophy.*<-

->I.<-

While I was thus mutely pondering within myself, and recording my sorrowful complainings with my pen, it seemed to me that there appeared above my head a woman of a **countenance** <term>countenance: a person's face or facial expression.</term> exceeding **venerable** <term>venerable: worthy of great respect, particularly due to age, wisdom, or character.</term>. Her eyes were bright as fire, and of a more than human keenness; her complexion was lively, her vigour showed no trace of **enfeeblement** <term>enfeeblement: the state of being weak or lacking in physical strength.</term>; and yet her years were right full, and she plainly seemed not of our age and time.

Her stature was difficult to judge. At one moment it exceeded not the common height, at another her forehead seemed to strike the sky; and whenever she raised her head higher, she began to pierce within the very heavens <note>Lady Philosophy’s shifting height symbolizes her nature: she is grounded in human logic but also reaches into the divine mysteries of the universe.</note>, and to baffle the eyes of them that looked upon her. Her garments were of an **imperishable fabric** <term>imperishable fabric: a material that cannot decay or be destroyed, representing the eternal and unchanging nature of truth.</term>, wrought with the finest threads and of the most delicate workmanship; and these, as—

<summary>This page introduces the personification of Philosophy, who appears to Boethius as a mysterious woman of varying height and eternal vitality. Her supernatural appearance signals the beginning of his transition from worldly despair to spiritual understanding.</summary>

her own lips afterwards assured me, she had herself woven with her own hands. The beauty of this **vesture** <term>vesture: a formal or ceremonial garment.</term> had been somewhat tarnished by age and neglect, and wore that dingy look which marble contracts from exposure. On the lowermost edge was inwoven the Greek letter Π <note>The Greek letter Pi (Π) stands for "Praxis," representing the Practical or Political life—the life of action and ethics.</note>, on the topmost the letter Θ <note>The Greek letter Theta (Θ) stands for "Theoria," representing the Theoretical life—the life of contemplation and the study of divine things.</note>, and between the two were to be seen steps, like a staircase, from the lower to the upper letter. This robe, moreover, had been torn by the hands of violent persons <note>original: "violenti manibus." Boethius refers to various philosophical sects, such as the Stoics and Epicureans, who he believes have each snatched away a small fragment of the truth, mistakenly claiming they possess the whole.</note>, who had each snatched away what he could clutch. Her right hand held a note-book; in her left she bore a staff. And when she saw the **Muses of Poesie** <term>Muses of Poesie: the traditional Greek goddesses of artistic inspiration, here representing the emotional, self-pitying poetry Boethius was writing.</term> standing by my bedside, dictating the words of my lamentations, she was moved awhile to wrath, and her eyes flashed sternly. ‘Who,’ said she, ‘has allowed yon **play-acting wantons** <term>play-acting wantons: a harsh insult by Philosophy, calling the Muses "stage-actresses" or "temptresses" who provide emotional distraction rather than a cure.</term> to approach this sick man—these who, so far

<meta>This page continues the scene where Lady Philosophy has suddenly appeared in Boethius's prison cell. ->*The Consolation of Philosophy.*<-

instead of giving medicine to heal his **malady** <term>malady: a disease or ailment; here, it refers to Boethius's spiritual and mental despair.</term>, you even feed it with sweet poison? These are the ones who kill the rich crop of reason with the barren thorns of passion; they accustom men’s minds to disease, instead of setting them free. Now, if it were some common man whom your allurements were seducing, as is usually your way, I would be less indignant. On such a person I would not have spent my efforts in vain. 

But this man was nurtured in the **Eleatic** <note>The Eleatic school of philosophy, founded by Parmenides in the 5th century BCE, focused on the nature of "Being" and the use of pure logic over sensory experience.</note> and **Academic** <note>The "Academic" philosophy refers to the Academy, the school founded by Plato in Athens. It represents the highest tradition of Greek philosophical thought.</note> philosophies. No, get away from here, you **sirens** <term>sirens: in Greek mythology, creatures whose beautiful singing lured sailors to their destruction. Philosophy uses the term to describe the dangerous allure of self-pitying poetry.</term>, whose sweetness does not last; leave him for my own Muses to tend and heal!” 

At these words of **upbraiding** <term>upbraiding: a severe scolding or reproach.</term>, the whole band of poetic Muses, in deepened sadness, with downcast eyes and blushes that confessed their shame, dolefully left the room. 

But I, because my sight was dimmed with much weeping, and I could not tell who this woman of such commanding authority was—I was **dumfounded** <term>dumfounded: struck dumb with astonishment and surprise.</term>, and, with my gaze fastened on the earth, continued silently to wait for what she might do next. Then she drew near me and sat on the edge of my couch, and, looking into my face which was heavy with grief and fixed in sadness...

[[vocabulary: malady, Eleatic, Academic, sirens, upbraiding, dumfounded]]

The narrator, blinded by tears and stunned by her presence, waits in silence as she sits beside him.</summary>

Since the text provided is an existing English translation (specifically that of **H.R. James, 1897**), I have provided the original Latin text for these specific passages of Boethius’s *De Consolatione Philosophiae* (Book I, Metrum 2 through the beginning of Prosa 4) followed by a fresh, scholarly translation.

Consistent with your instructions, I have maintained the paragraph structure, preserved proper nouns, and noted textual discrepancies found in the OCR.

### PAGE 31 (Original Page 9)
**Liber I, Metrum 2**

**Latin Context:**
*Heu quam praecipiti mersa profundo / mens hebet et propria luce relicta / tendit in externas ire tenebras, / terrenis quotiens flatibus aucta / crescit in immensum noxia cura.*

*Hic quondam caelo liber aperto / suetus inethereos ire meatus / cernebat rosei lumina solis, / visebat gelidae sidera lunae / et quicumque vagos stellarum fertur in orbes / laureatus numeris victor habebat.*

**Scholarly Translation:**
**SONG II: HIS DESPONDENCY**

Alas! In what a headlong abyss is the mind drowned; its own light relinquished, it grows dull and wanders into outer darkness, as often as noxious care, swollen by earthly blasts, increases without measure.

This man once was free under the open sky; accustomed to traverse ethereal paths, he was wont to behold the light of the rosy sun and visit the constellations of the cold moon. Whatever star wanders through its circling spheres, he, as a victor, held it constrained by the calculations of number.

***

### PAGE 32 (Original Page 10)
**Liber I, Metrum 2 (cont.)**

**Latin Context:**
*Quin etiam causas unde sonora / flamina sollicitent aequora ponti, / quis regat stabilem spiritus orbem / vel cur hesperias rutilus in undas / sol cadat ex ortu roseo...*

*Quis veris placidas temperet horas, / ut terram roseis imbuit hortis, / quis dedit ut pleno turgidus anno / autumnus gravidis influat uvis...*

**Scholarly Translation:**
He sought the causes: why the sounding blasts vex the level seas; what spirit rules the stable globe; or why the ruddy sun, rising from the rosy east, sinks into the western waves.

What power tempers the placid hours of spring so that it decks the earth with rose-gardens; or who grants that autumn, turgid in the year’s fullness, should overflow with heavy grapes. All this he knew—ever accustomed to seek out the hidden laws of Nature. Now he lies, the light of his mind quenched, his neck burdened with heavy chains, his countenance cast down by the weight, forced to gaze only upon the dull earth.

***

### PAGE 33 (Original Page 11)
**Liber I, Prosa 2**

**Latin Context:**
*‘Sed medicinae,’ inquit, ‘tempus est quam querelae.’ Tum vero totis in me intenta oculis: ‘Tune ille es,’ inquit, ‘qui nostro quondam lacte nutritus, nostris educatus alimentis ad virilis animi robur evaseras? Atqui talia contuleramus arma quae nisi prius abiecisses, invicta te firmitate tuerentur. Agnoscisne me? Quid taces? Pudorne te an stupor silere fecit? Me mallem pudor sed te, ut video, stupor oppressit.’*

**Scholarly Translation:**
**II.**
“But,” she said, “it is time for medicine rather than lamentation.” Then, fixing her eyes intently upon me, she cried: “Art thou that man who, once nourished on my milk and reared on my food, had attained the vigor of a manly spirit? And yet, I had bestowed such weapons upon thee as would have guarded thee with invincible firmness, hadst thou not first cast them away. Dost thou recognize me? Why art thou silent? Is it shame or stupor that makes thee mute? I should prefer it were shame; but, as I see, a stupor has overwhelmed thee.”

***

### PAGE 34 (Original Page 12)
**Liber I, Prosa 2 (cont.) & Metrum 3**

**Latin Context:**
*...nihil pericli est; lethargum patitur communem deceptarum mentium morbum. Paulisper sui oblitus est; facile recordabitur, si quidem nos ante cognoverit. Quod ut possit, paulisper lumina eius mortalium rerum nube caligantia detergamus.’ Sic dicens contracta in lenem sinum veste fluentes oculos siccavit.*

**Scholarly Translation:**
“...there is no danger; he suffers from lethargy, the common disease of deluded minds. He has forgotten himself for a little while; he will easily remember, provided he first recognizes me. That he may do so, let me wipe his eyes, clouded as they are by a mist of mortal things.” So saying, she gathered her robe into a fold and dried my eyes, which were swimming with tears.

**SONG III: THE MISTS DISPELLED**

Then was the night dispelled and the darkness left me; my former sight returned to my eyes. Just as when the swift *Caurus* [Northwest wind] gathers the rain-clouds and the sky stands thick with watery tempests; the sun is hidden and the stars do not yet peer through the night. But if *Boreas* [North wind], sent from the Thracian cavern, strikes the storm and opens the clear day, out shines the sudden light and strikes the wondering eyes with its rays.

***

### PAGE 35 (Original Page 13)
**Liber I, Prosa 3**

**Latin Context:**
*Haud aliter tristitiae nebulis dissolutis hausi caelum et ad cognoscendam medicantis faciem mentem recepi. Itaque ubi in eam deduxi oculos intuitumque defixi, respicio nutricem meam cuius ab adulescentia sedibus fueram reversatus Philosophiam.*

**Scholarly Translation:**
**III.**
In no different wise were the clouds of my sadness dissolved; I drank in the light of heaven and recovered my senses to recognize the face of my physician. Therefore, when I turned my eyes toward her and fixed my gaze, I beheld my nurse, **Philosophy**, in whose house I had been conversant since my youth.

“Ah,” I cried, “Mistress of all virtues, why hast thou descended from the vault of heaven to the solitude of this my exile? Is it that thou mayest be subjected to false accusations along with me?”

“Should I desert thee, my child?” she replied. “Should I not share the burden thou hast taken up for the sake of my name, by dividing the labor? Truly, it was never lawful for Philosophy to leave the innocent man’s journey without a companion.”

***

### PAGE 36 (Original Page 14)
**Liber I, Prosa 3 (cont.)**

**Latin Context:**
*...itane tandem sanxti nunc primum sapientia periculis lacessita est? Nonne apud veteres quoque ante Platonis nostri aetatem magnum saepe certamen cum stultitiae temeritate certavimus? Eodemque superstite praeceptor eius Socrates iniustae victoriam mortis me adstante promeruit? Cuius hereditatem cum deinceps Epicureum vulgus ac Stoicum ceterique pro sua quisque parte raptum ire molirentur...*

**Scholarly Translation:**
“...is it, then, the first time that Wisdom has been provoked by perils in an evil age? Did I not often, among the ancients before the age of our **Plato**, wage a great contest against the rashness of folly? And while he yet lived, did not his master **Socrates** win the victory of an unjust death, with me standing by his side? When afterwards the **Epicurean** mob and the **Stoic**, and the rest, each for his own part, endeavored to seize his inheritance and dragged me away, protesting and struggling as if I were their prey, they tore the garment I had woven with my own hands. Snatching away the fragments, they departed, believing that I had passed entirely into their possession.”

**Note on OCR:** The OCR mentions *Anaxagoras*, *Socrates*, and *Zeno*. These are consistent with the Latin text (*Anaxagorae fugam, Socratis venenum, Zenonis tormenta*).

***

### PAGE 37 (Original Page 15)
**Liber I, Prosa 3 (cont.)**

**Latin Context:**
*...Atqui si Canii, si Senecae, si Sorani calamitatem nescis... Quibus nihil aliud in cladem dedit nisi meis moribus instituti vitiis improborum dissimillimi videbantur.*

**Scholarly Translation:**
“...Yet if thou art ignorant of the fate of **Canius**, of **Seneca**, and of **Soranus**—since their memories are neither ancient nor uncelebrated—know that they were brought to ruin for no other reason than that, being trained in my principles, they appeared most unlike the vices of the wicked. Therefore, there is nothing for thee to wonder at if, on the sea of this life, we are tossed by storm-blasts, for our chief aim is to displease the most wicked of men.”

**Note on OCR:** The OCR reads "**Arrius**." This is a likely transcription error for **Canius** (Julius Canius), a Stoic martyr mentioned by Boethius.

***

### PAGE 38 (Original Page 16)
**Liber I, Metrum 4**

**Latin Context:**
*Quisquis composito serenus aevo / fatum sub pedibus egit superbum / rectusque in vultum fortunae / utriusque possit inconcussus manere...*

**Scholarly Translation:**
**SONG IV: NOTHING CAN SUBDUE VIRTUE**

Whosoever, serene in a well-ordered life, has placed proud Fate beneath his feet and, looking straight upon either fortune, can remain unshaken, him neither the rage of the sea, threatening the deep from its surging waves, nor the unstable **Vesuvius**, casting forth its smoking fires from broken chimneys, nor the stroke of the brandished thunderbolt that is wont to level high towers, shall move.

Why, then, do wretched men fear fierce tyrants who are but weak and without strength? Hope for nothing, and fear nothing; thus shalt thou disarm the impotent man’s rage. But he who trembles in fear or sinks in desire—not being firm in his own right—has cast away his shield and deserted his post; he fashions the very chain by which he is bound.

***

### PAGE 39 (Original Page 17)
**Liber I, Prosa 4**

**Latin Context:**
*‘Sentisne,’ inquit, ‘haec atque animo tuo illabuntur, an "onos lyras"? Quid fles, quid lacrimis manas? "Exere, neque hoc obtexeris animo." Si operam medicantis exspectas, oportet vulnus detegas.’*

**Scholarly Translation:**
**IV.**
“Dost thou feel these things?” she asked. “Do they sink into thy mind, or art thou as the ‘ass to the lyre’? Why dost thou weep? Why do tears flow? ‘Speak out, and hide it not in thy heart.’ If thou lookest for the physician’s aid, thou must needs lay bare thy wound.”

Then I, gathering what strength of spirit I could, replied: “Is there still need for reminder? Does not the cruelty of Fortune against me appear sufficiently plain? Does not the very appearance of this place move thee? Is this the **library** which thou hadst chosen for thyself as a most sure dwelling in my house?”

***

### PAGE 40 (Original Page 18)
**Liber I, Prosa 4 (cont.)**

**Latin Context:**
*...hancine Platonis ore sententiam fixisti: "beatos fore res publicas, si aut studiosi sapientiae regerent eas aut regentes studere sapientiae contigisset"?*

**Scholarly Translation:**
“...where we sat together and discoursed on the knowledge of things human and divine? Was this my garb, was my countenance such as this, when I explored with thee the secrets of nature, and thou didst trace for me the paths of the stars with thy wand? Is this the reward for my obedience? Yet thou didst confirm this maxim by the mouth of **Plato**: ‘that commonwealths would be happy if either students of wisdom should rule them, or if it should happen that those who rule should study wisdom.’ By the same man’s mouth, thou didst teach that this was a necessary cause for wise men to undertake the republic: lest the reins of government be left to wicked or reckless citizens, bringing ruin upon the good.”

‘But the time,’ said she, ‘calls rather for healing than for lamentation.’ Then, with her eyes bent full upon me, ‘Art thou that man,’ she cries, ‘who, erstwhile fed with the milk and reared upon the nourishment which is mine to give, had grown up to the full vigour of a manly spirit? And yet I had bestowed such <term>armour</term> <note>original: "arma"; Philosophy views her teachings as protective weaponry that should have made the narrator’s mind impenetrable to the arrows of misfortune.</note> on thee as would have proved an invincible defence, hadst thou not first cast it away. 

Dost thou know me? Why art thou silent? Is it shame or amazement that hath struck thee dumb? Would it were shame; but, as I see, a stupor hath seized upon thee.’ Then, when she saw me not only answering nothing, but mute and utterly incapable of speech, she gently touched my breast with her hand, and said: ‘There is no danger; these are the symptoms of <term>lethargy</term> <note>original: "lethargum"; in the medieval medical and philosophical tradition, this was seen as a "sickness of forgetfulness" where the soul loses its connection to truth.</note>, the usual sickness of <term>deluded minds</term> <note>original: "mentium deceptarum"; those who have been led astray by the false promises of worldly success.</note>. For awhile he

She reminds him of the intellectual "armour" she provided him in his youth, which he has now abandoned.</summary>

[[original page: 13]]
-> *The Sorrows of Boethius.* 13 <-

-> III. <-

In this way, the clouds of my <term>melancholy</term> <note>In the medieval and classical sense, melancholy was seen as a physical and spiritual sickness that clouded the mind with dark "vapors."</note> were broken up. I saw the clear sky once more and regained the power to recognize the face of my physician. Accordingly, when I had lifted my eyes and fixed my gaze upon her, I beheld my nurse, Philosophy, whose <term>halls</term> <note>original: "laribus"; literally the "household gods" or home. This refers to the schools of philosophy where Boethius spent his youth studying.</note> I had frequented from my youth onward.

"Ah!" I cried, "Mistress of all excellence, why have you come down from the heavens and entered the solitude of this, my <term>exile</term> <note>Boethius was imprisoned in Pavia, far from his home and status in Rome, awaiting execution.</note>? Is it so that you, too, even as I, may be persecuted with false accusations?"

"Could I desert you, my child," she said, "and not lighten the burden which you have taken upon yourself through the hatred of my name, by sharing this trouble? Even forgetting that it was not lawful for Philosophy to leave the path of the innocent companionless, should I, do you think...

<margin>Philosophy</margin>
<margin>melancholy</margin>
<margin>exile</margin>

<summary>The narrator regains his spiritual sight and recognizes Lady Philosophy as his lifelong mentor. He questions why she has joined him in his prison, to which she responds that a teacher must never abandon their student, especially when they are persecuted for the sake of truth.</summary>

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>This page continues Philosophy’s response to the narrator. Having identified herself, she now addresses his despair by placing his suffering within a historical context. She argues that the persecution of wisdom is not a new phenomenon, citing the trials of great thinkers who came before him to show that he is part of a long tradition of those who suffered for the truth.</meta>

[[original page: 14]]
-> 14 **The Consolation of Philosophy.** <-

Do you fear being blamed, or do you shrink from it as if some strange new thing had happened? Do you think that now, for the first time in this corrupt age, Wisdom has been put in danger? 

Did I not often in the old days, long before the time of my servant <term>Plato</term> <note>A foundational Greek philosopher and student of Socrates; Philosophy calls him her "servant" because his work was dedicated to her.</note>, fight a difficult war against the recklessness of the foolish? During his lifetime, too, his teacher <term>Socrates</term> <note>Socrates was sentenced to death by the Athenian state in 399 BCE on charges of impiety and corrupting the youth, a classic example of "unjust death" for the sake of truth.</note> won a victory through an unjust death with my help. 

And when, one after the other, the <term>Epicurean herd</term> <note>Philosophy uses "herd" dismissively to describe the followers of Epicurus, who believed the world was made of atoms and that the goal of life was pleasure/absence of pain.</note>, the <term>Stoics</term> <note>A school of philosophy that taught that virtue is the only good and that one should remain indifferent to external circumstances.</note>, and the rest—each as much as they were able—tried to seize the inheritance Socrates left behind, they began dragging me off as their prize, despite my protests and resistance. They tore into pieces the garment which I had woven with my own hands. Clutching those torn scraps, they went away, believing that they possessed the whole of me. 

Because some traces of my clothing were seen on them, some of these men were even destroyed because of the mistake of the ignorant masses, who falsely judged them to be my true disciples. 

Perhaps you do not know of the exile of <term>Anaxagoras</term> <note>A pre-Socratic philosopher who was exiled from Athens for suggesting the sun was a red-hot stone rather than a god.</note>, the poison-drink given to Socrates, or the tortures suffered by <term>Zeno</term> <note>Likely Zeno of Elea, who was famously tortured for his involvement in a conspiracy against a tyrant, showing his philosophical endurance.</note>, be—

<margin>Socrates</margin>
<margin>Epicurean</margin>
<margin>Stoic</margin>
<margin>Anaxagoras</margin>
<margin>Zeno</margin>

She uses the metaphor of her torn robe to explain how different philosophical schools (like the Stoics and Epicureans) mistakenly claim to possess the "whole truth" when they only have fragments.</summary>

[[original page: 15]]
-> *The Sorrows of Boethius.* 15 <-

...because these things happened in a distant country; yet mightest thou have learnt the fate of <term>Canius</term> <note>The source text likely refers to Julius Canius, a Stoic philosopher put to death by the Emperor Caligula. The transcription "Arrius" is a common error for this name.</note>, of <term>Seneca</term> <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca, the Stoic tutor to Emperor Nero who was eventually forced to commit suicide by his former pupil.</note>, of <term>Soranus</term> <note>Barea Soranus, a Roman senator renowned for his justice, who was executed under Nero's reign.</note>, whose stories are neither old nor unknown to fame. These men were brought to destruction for no other reason than that, settled as they were in my principles, their lives were a manifest contrast to the ways of the wicked. 

So there is nothing thou shouldst wonder at, if on the seas of this life we are tossed by storm-blasts, seeing that we have made it our chiefest aim to refuse compliance with evil-doers. And though, maybe, the host of the wicked is many in number, yet is it contemptible, since it is under no leadership, but is hurried hither and thither at the blind driving of mad error. 

And if at times and seasons they set in array against us, and fall on in overwhelming strength, our leader draws off her forces into the <term>citadel</term> <note>Philosophy uses the metaphor of a fortress to represent the inner peace and reason of the mind, which remains untouched by external misfortune.</note> while they are busy plundering the useless baggage. But we from our vantage ground, safe from all this wild work, laugh to see them making prize of the most valueless of things, protected by a bulwark which aggressive folly may not aspire to reach.

[[vocabulary: <term>Canius</term>, <term>Seneca</term>, <term>Soranus</term>]]

<summary>Philosophy cites the Roman martyrs of thought—Canius, Seneca, and Soranus—to prove that the righteous are often persecuted by the wicked. She argues that while the "evil-doers" may plunder a person's material goods, the mind remains a safe citadel that folly cannot breach.</summary>
<keywords>Canius, Seneca, Soranus, citadel of the mind, persecution of philosophers, virtue vs. wickedness, inner peace</keywords>

-> # 16 *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

-> ## SONG IV. <-

-> ### NOTHING CAN SUBDUE <term>VIRTUE</term> <note>original: *virtus*. For Boethius, this means more than just being "good"; it refers to moral excellence and the inner strength of character that remains unshaken by external events.</note>. <-

Whoever is calm, serene, and steady,
Who places <term>Fate</term> <note>original: *fatum*. In this context, it refers to the external circumstances and "luck" that one cannot control.</note> beneath their feet;
Firm and steadfast, no matter what happens,
Keeping an unconquered expression;
The rage of the furious sea,
Tossing its wild threats high,
Cannot terrify them.
Neither can the flames from the smoky forges
That <term>Vesuvius</term> <note>A volcano near Naples, famous for its catastrophic eruption in 79 AD. It is used here as a symbol of uncontrollable natural violence.</note> pours out,
Nor the lightning bolt from the sky
That strikes down the tower.
Why, then, should you feel fear
At the <term>tyrant’s</term> <note>While Boethius was imprisoned by King Theodoric, Philosophy speaks generally here about any ruler who uses fear to control others.</note> powerless might?
Dread him not, and fear no harm,
And you shall disarm his rage;
But whoever gives way to hope or fear—
Having lost the rightful control of their heart—
Has thrown away their shield,
And like a coward, fled the battlefield;
They have unknowingly forged
The very <term>fetters</term> <note>Chains or shackles. Philosophy argues that our own uncontrolled emotions (like fear) are what truly imprison us, more so than physical iron bars.</note> their own neck must bear!

<summary>This poetic section (Song 4) defines the Stoic ideal: a person whose inner peace is so grounded in virtue that they are immune to the terrors of nature and the threats of political oppressors. It concludes with the warning that fearing a tyrant is what actually creates the prisoner's chains.</summary>

-> *The Sorrows of Boethius.* 17 <-

-> IV. <-

‘Do you understand?’ she asks. ‘Do my words sink into your mind? Or are you as dull as “the ass to the sound of the lyre” <note>original: *asinus ad lyram*. This was a common ancient proverb used to describe someone who is completely unable to appreciate music, art, or—in this case—philosophical wisdom.</note>? Why do you weep? Why do tears stream from your eyes?

“Speak out, hide it not in your heart.” <note>Philosophy is quoting Homer’s *Iliad* (1.363), where the goddess Thetis speaks to her son Achilles. By using this quote, Philosophy takes on a motherly, divine role, urging Boethius to share his pain.</note>

If you look for the <term>physician’s</term> <note>original: *medici*. Philosophy frequently uses the metaphor of "medicine" for the soul. Before she can cure Boethius’s despair, he must "diagnose" it by explaining his troubles.</note> help, you must disclose your wound.’

Then I, gathering together what strength I could, began: ‘Is there still need of telling? Is not the <term>cruelty of fortune</term> <note>original: *fortunae asperitas*. Boethius refers to the sudden change in his life from being a powerful official to a condemned prisoner.</note> against me plain enough? Does not the very look of this place move you? Is this the <term>library</term> <note>original: *bibliotheca*. In the Roman world, a library was a private sanctuary for study. Boethius is contrasting his former life of intellectual luxury with the bare walls of his prison cell.</note>, the room which you had chosen as your constant resort in my home, the place where we so often sat together and held discourse of all things in heaven and earth? Was my clothing and my expression like this when I explored nature’s hidden secrets with you, and you...

<summary>Philosophy urges Boethius to speak his grief aloud, comparing her role to that of a doctor who cannot treat a hidden wound. Boethius responds by lamenting the loss of his former life, specifically the library where he once studied the secrets of the universe.</summary>

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>This page continues Boethius’s response to Philosophy. In the previous page, he began to describe his fall from power, questioning why his current misery is the "reward" for a life dedicated to her teachings. He continues that thought here, invoking the political theories of Plato.</meta>

-> 18 *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

...you used your wand to map out for me the courses of the stars, shaping all the while my character and the whole conduct of my life after the pattern of the <term>celestial order</term> <note>original: *caelestis ordinis*. Boethius is referring to the ancient belief that the human soul and society should reflect the perfect, mathematical harmony found in the movement of the stars and planets.</note>? Is this the reward for my obedience? Yet you once urged, through the words of <term>Plato</term> <note>Boethius refers to a famous passage in Plato’s *Republic* (5.473d) regarding the "philosopher king."</note>, the <term>maxim</term> <note>A short statement expressing a general truth or rule of conduct.</note>: “that states would be happy, either if philosophers ruled them, or if it should so happen that their rulers would turn to philosophy.”

By his mouth likewise, you pointed out this essential reason why philosophers should enter public life: namely, that if the reins of government are left to unprincipled and <term>profligate</term> <note>original: *improborum*. Referring to citizens who are shamelessly immoral or corrupt.</note> citizens, trouble and destruction would fall upon the good. Following these precepts, I have tried to apply in the business of public administration the principles which I learned from you in peaceful study.

You are my witness—as is that <term>divinity</term> <note>likely referring to Reason or the divine mind</note> who has implanted you in the hearts of the wise—that I brought to my duties no aim but a devotion to the public good. For this cause, I have become involved in bitter and inescapable feuds; and, as happens inevitably if a man holds fast to the right...

<summary>Boethius justifies his entry into politics by citing Plato’s belief that philosophers must rule to ensure justice. He claims his current downfall is an unjust "reward" for a career spent pursuing the public good rather than personal gain.</summary>

<keywords>Plato, philosopher kings, public good, celestial order, political duty, justice, governance</keywords>

--- Page 41 ---
[From the Latin: *...ob conscientiae libertatem adversus potentes...*]

For the sake of the independence of my conscience, I have often had to disregard the offense of the powerful in the cause of justice. How many times have I confronted and thwarted Conigastus in his attacks upon the fortunes of every weak man? How many times have I checked Trigguilla, the steward of the royal household, even when his injustices were already as good as finished? How many times have I used my own authority to shield those wretched men—whom the unpunished avarice of the barbarians was forever hounding with countless false accusations—even at the risk of my own influence? 

No man has ever dragged me away from justice to oppression. When the fortunes of the provincials were being ruined by both private plunder and public taxes, I grieved no less than those who suffered. When, during a time of bitter famine, a "coemptio" [forced sale]—as burdensome as it was unjustifiable—was proclaimed, threatening to afflict the province of Campania with starvation, I engaged in a struggle with the Praetorian Prefect for the sake of the common good. I brought the case before the King himself and, with his knowledge, I fought until the enforcement of that sale was prevented.

--- Page 42 ---
[From the Latin: *Paulinum consularem...*]

I rescued the consular Paulinus from the very jaws of those courtly hounds who, in their greedy hopes, had already devoured his wealth. I shielded Albinus, a man of consular rank, from the penalties of a prejudged indictment by exposing myself to the hatred of the informer Cyprian. 

Do I seem to have stored up enough enmities for myself? But surely, among the rest of my countrymen at least, I ought to have been safer, since for the love of justice I left myself no hope of safety among the courtiers. And yet, who are the informers by whom I have been struck down? One is Basil, who, having been dismissed from the King’s service, was driven by the weight of his debts to denounce my name. There are also Opilio and Gaudentius, men whom the King’s decree had sentenced to exile for their numerous and various frauds. When they refused to comply and sought to protect themselves by fleeing to the sanctuary of the church, the King, upon learning of it, decreed that...

--- Page 43 ---
[From the Latin: *...nisi intra praescriptum diem Ravennatem civitatem...*]

...unless they departed from the city of Ravenna within a prescribed day, they should be driven out with their foreheads branded. What severity could be added to such a judgment? Yet on that very day, the information they laid against me was accepted. 

Is this the way things are? Did my way of life deserve this? Did the fact that my condemnation was already decided make them honorable accusers? Has Fortune no shame—if not for the innocence of the accused, then at least for the vileness of the accusers? 

You ask what the sum of the charges against me is? I am said to have "wished for the safety of the Senate." You ask in what manner? I am accused of having hindered an informer from producing documents to prove the Senate guilty of treason. What then is your counsel, O Teacher? Shall I deny the charge, lest I bring shame upon you? But I did wish for it, and I shall never cease to wish for it. Shall I confess? Then my effort to hinder the informer will cease. Shall I call the desire for the safety of that order a crime? Certainly, the Senate itself, by its decrees concerning me, has made it so. But Folly, though she deceives herself with false names, cannot change the merit of things; and mindful of the precept of Socrates, I do not think it right to hide the truth or to grant a lie. But this matter, however it stands, I leave to your judgment and the verdict of the wise.

--- Page 44 ---
[From the Latin: *Cuius ne ditionem rei...*]

Furthermore, lest the course of these events and the truth of the facts be hidden from posterity, I have committed an account of the transaction to writing. Why should I speak of the forged letters by which I am accused of hoping for Roman liberty? The falsity of these would have been manifest if I had been allowed to use the testimony of the informers themselves, which is the most powerful evidence in all such matters. 

For what liberty is there left to hope for? Would that there were some! I would have answered in the words of Canius, who, when accused by Gaius Caesar [Caligula] of being aware of a conspiracy against him, replied: "If I had known, you would never have known."

--- Page 45 ---
[From the Latin: *Nec propterea sic dolores hebetavit...*]

Grief has not so blunted my senses that I complain merely because wicked men plot villainy against virtue; rather, I marvel exceedingly that they have accomplished what they hoped for. For the will to do evil may perhaps be attributed to human frailty, but that the schemes of the wicked should prevail against the innocent while God looks on—this is truly monstrous. Thus, not without cause, one of your followers [Epicurus/Plato] asked: "If God exists, whence comes evil? But if He exists not, whence comes good?" 

Yet it is perhaps natural that those who seek the blood of all good men and the entire Senate should wish to destroy me also, whom they saw as a protector of both. But did I deserve such treatment from the Fathers [the Senate] as well? 

You remember, I believe—since you were always present to direct my words and deeds—you remember, I say, what happened at Verona. When the King, eager for a general ruin, attempted to transfer the charge of treason brought against Albinus to the whole senatorial order, with what disregard for my own peril...

--- Page 46 ---
[From the Latin: *...omne senatus latus...*]

...I defended the innocence of the entire Senate. You know that I speak the truth, and that I have never boasted of my own merits for the sake of praise. For whenever a man receives the reward of fame by proclaiming his good deeds, he diminishes the secret satisfaction of his conscience. 

But you see what end has overtaken my innocence. Instead of the rewards of true virtue, I suffer the punishments for a crime I did not commit. Was there ever a confession of guilt so clear that it produced such unanimous severity among judges? Even the frailty of human nature or the instability of Fortune usually moves someone to mercy. Had I been accused of wanting to burn temples, or to slaughter priests with a sacrilegious sword, or to plot the massacre of all good men, I should still have been...

--- Page 47 ---
[From the Latin: *...praesentem tamen vel confitentem...*]

...brought before the court to be punished only upon confession or conviction. But now, for my excessive devotion to the Senate, I have been condemned to death and proscription, unheard and undefended, though I am nearly five hundred miles away. O my judges, you have truly earned that no one should ever be convicted of such a "crime" again! 

Even my accusers saw the dignity of the charge they brought against me; therefore, to darken it with some shadow of a crime, they lied and said that I had stained my conscience with sacrilege for the sake of my ambitions. But your spirit, dwelling within me, had driven all desire for mortal things from the seat of my soul, and under your gaze, there was no room for sacrilege. For you daily whispered into my ears and mind that Pythagorean maxim: "Follow God" [ἕπου θεῷ]. It was not fitting that I should seek the aid of the vilest spirits when you were...

--- Page 48 ---
[From the Latin: *...molding me to such excellence...*]

...molding me into the likeness of God. Furthermore, the innocence of my private home, the company of most honorable friends, and a father-in-law [Symmachus] as revered for his sanctity as for his character, all defend me from any suspicion of such a crime. 

But—horrible as it is—they even draw support for this charge from you! They think me likely to have committed such wickedness for the very reason that I am steeped in your teachings and refined by your manners. Thus, it is not enough that my devotion to you brings me no protection; you yourself must be attacked because of the hatred I have incurred. 

Truly, this is the final weight of my misfortunes: that the judgment of the world looks not to the merit of things, but to the result; they consider only that which Fortune has favored to be the product of foresight. Thus, the first thing to desert the unfortunate is their good reputation. I remember with bitterness the various rumors and conflicting judgments of the people.

--- Page 49 ---
[From the Latin: *Hoc tantum dixerim...*]

I will say only this: the heaviest burden of adversity is that, as soon as a charge is laid against the wretched, they are believed to have deserved what they suffer. And so I, stripped of my honors, stained in my reputation, and deprived of every comfort, am punished for my very benefits. 

And now I seem to see the workshops of the wicked overflowing with joy and gladness; I see every villainous man threatening new fabricated charges. I see good men paralyzed by fear at my danger, and every criminal incited to daring by impunity and to audacity by the hope of profit. The innocent are deprived not only of their security but of their very defense. Therefore, I must cry out:

**METRUM 5**

**Boethius’ Prayer**

O Creator of the star-filled sphere,
Who, seated on Your eternal throne,
Turn the swift-moving heavens,
And compel the stars to obey Your law:

--- Page 50 ---
[From the Latin: *Ut nunc pleno lucida cornu...*]

So that the Moon, now shining with a full horn,
Reflecting all her brother’s [the Sun's] fires,
Dims the lesser stars;
But when she draws closer to that fraternal orb,
She loses her light and grows pale.

You, who at the coming of the evening,
As Hesperus, bring on the cold shadows,
And again as Lucifer, the herald of the morning,
Change the paths of the stars.
When the cold of winter binds the earth,
You shorten the span of the day;
When the heat of summer returns,
You make the swift hours of the night fly.

You govern the changing seasons:
The leaves that the harsh North Wind [Boreas] steals,
The gentle West Wind [Zephyr] restores;
The seeds that Sirius watched parched in the field,
Arcturus beholds as ripened grain.
Nothing escapes Your ancient law;
Nothing forsakes the work of its proper station.

***

**Scholarly Notes:**
*   **Abbreviations:** The Latin "ἕπου θεῷ" (Follow God) is a Greek fragment preserved in the Latin text of Boethius.
*   **Unclear Readings:** The OCR text "2—2" on page 41 and "20" etc. refer to the original printed page numbers and signature marks of the James edition. 
*   **Proper Nouns:** All names (Conigastus, Trigguilla, etc.) are kept in their Latinate forms as they appear in the historical record of the Ostrogothic court.
*   **Technical Term:** *Coemptio* refers to the state’s right to force the sale of crops to the government at a fixed (often low) price.

--- Page 51 ---
**[Source: *De Consolatione Philosophiae*, I, Metrum V]**

**THE SORROWS OF BOETHIUS**

Creator of the star-bearing wheel,
Who, resting on Your eternal throne,
Turn the heavens in their swift rotation
And compel the stars to obey Your law;
So that the Moon, now with full orb,
Reflecting all her brother’s fires,
Dims the lesser lights,
And now, growing pale with darkened horn,
Loses her light as she nears the sun.

[...] [Note: The OCR omits the central stanzas of Metrum V regarding the seasons]

Can it be that You, who bind all things
To a certain end, neglect only the deeds of men?
Why does slippery Fortune permit
Harsh changes to fall upon the innocent?
While the wicked sit on lofty thrones,
And the unjust, by a perverse reversal,
Trample upon the necks of the holy?
Virtue lies hidden in dark shadows,
And the righteous man bears the penalty of the guilty.

No falsehood harms the deceitful,
Nor does perjury stained by fraud;
But when they choose to exert their power,
They rejoice to subdue the highest kings
Whom the fears of many peoples have exalted.
Look now upon these wretched lands,
O You who bind the bonds of the world!
We men, no small part of Your great work,
Are tossed by the waves of Fortune.
O Ruler, calm these surging floods
And steady the earth with that same covenant
By which You rule the high heavens!

--- Page 52 ---
**[Source: *De Consolatione Philosophiae*, I, Prosa V]**

**THE CONSOLATION OF PHILOSOPHY**

**V.**

When I had barked out these things [Note: *delatravi*] in my continuous grief, she, with a calm countenance and entirely unmoved by my lamentations, spoke thus:
  “When I first saw you grieving and in tears, I knew immediately that you were miserable and an exile. Yet, how far off that exile was, I did not know until your speech revealed it. However, you have not been driven so far from your homeland; rather, you have strayed from it. Or, if you prefer to be thought of as exiled, it is you who have exiled yourself. For no one else could ever have lawfully done this to you.
  If you remember of what country you are a citizen, it is not governed by the rule of the multitude, as was the ancient Athenian polity, but ‘one is its Ruler, one its King,’ who rejoices in the increase of His citizens, not in

***

###

--- Page 53 ---
their expulsion; to be governed by His power and to obey His justice is the highest liberty. Are you ignorant of that most ancient law of your city, by which it is ordained that no one who has chosen to fix his dwelling there may be driven out? For there is no fear that one contained within its walls and defenses should deserve to be an exile. But whoever ceases to wish to dwell there, ceases also to deserve the protection of its law.
  Therefore, it is not so much the sight of this place that moves me as your own appearance; I do not look for library walls adorned with ivory and glass, but for the seat of your mind. It was there that I once stored not books, but that which gives books their value: the thoughts of my own former writings.
  As to what you said regarding your services to the commonweal, you have spoken truly, though perhaps too modestly considering the magnitude of your labors. The things you recounted concerning the honesty or the falsity of the charges brought against you are well known to all. Regarding the

***

###

--- Page 54 ---
crimes and deceits of the informers, you rightly thought it best to touch upon them briefly, since they are already more fully and effectively handled by the common talk of the people. You have also spoken sharply against the injustice of the Senate. You have grieved for our [Note: Philosophy refers to herself] injury and lamented the damage to my reputation. Finally, your indignation blazed out against Fortune, and you complained that rewards are not distributed equally according to merit. At the end of your frantic muse, you prayed that the same peace that rules the heavens might also rule the earth.
  But because a great tumult of passions has fallen upon you, and you are distracted by anger, pain, and grief, stronger remedies are not yet suitable for your condition. Therefore, for a time, I shall use milder means, so that the disturbances that have hardened into a tumor [Note: *tumorem*] through the influx of passions may be softened by gentle treatment, until they can endure the force of sharper medicine.”

***

###

--- Page 55 ---
**[Source: *De Consolatione Philosophiae*, I, Metrum VI]**

**SONG VI.**

**ALL THINGS HAVE THEIR NEEDFUL ORDER.**

He who sows the seed
In the unwilling furrows
When the constellation of the Crab [Note: *Cancri sidus*, i.e., Summer]
Glows hot with the rays of Phoebus,
Will be disappointed in his hope for grain
And must turn to the oak tree for acorns.

Seek not to gather violets
In the fields when the woods are white,
And the harsh North Wind
Roars with the blasts of winter.
Nor should you seek to press the vine
For grapes in the time of spring;
Bacchus brings his gifts rather
In the heat of the autumn.

God has assigned to each hour
Its own proper office;
He allows no confusion
To disturb the order He has set.
Whatever leaves its appointed path
With headlong haste,
Will find no happy ending
To its straying course.

***

###

--- Page 56 ---
**[Source: *De Consolatione Philosophiae*, I, Prosa VI]**

**VI.**

“First, then,” she said, “will you allow me to test and touch the state of your mind with a few small questions, so that I may understand the method for your cure?”
  “Ask whatever you wish,” I replied, “and I will answer.”
  Then she said: “This world—do you think it is governed by random and fortuitous chance, or do you believe there is any rational guidance in it?”
  “In no way,” I said, “could I ever imagine that such certain motions are directed by random chance. I know that God the Creator presides over His work, and no day shall ever move me from the truth of this belief.”
  “So it is,” she said, “for you sang of this very thing just now, lamenting that only men were exempt from divine care. For you were certain that all other things were moved by reason.

***

###

--- Page 57 ---
Yet I marvel greatly how, since you hold such a healthy opinion, you can be in such a state of sickness. But let us probe deeper; I feel that something is missing. Tell me, since you do not doubt that the world is ruled by God, by what means do you think He governs it?”
  “I do not quite grasp the meaning of your question,” I answered, “and so I cannot yet reply.”
  “I was not wrong,” she said, “to think that something was missing, and that a breach had been opened in your defenses through which the sickness of distraction has crept into your mind. But tell me, do you remember what is the end of all things [Note: *rerum finis*], and toward what the whole intention of nature tends?”
  “I heard it once,” I said, “but my memory has been dulled by grief.”
  “Well, do you at least know from what source all things have proceeded?”
  “I know,” I replied, “and I have said that the source is God.”
  “How then is it possible that, knowing the beginning of things, you are ignorant of their end?

***

###

--- Page 58 ---
But it is the nature of these disturbances that they may shake a man’s position, but they cannot uproot him or totally tear him away from himself. I pray you, answer me this: Do you remember that you are a man?”
  “How could I not remember?” I said.
  “Can you then define what a man is?”
  “Do you ask if I know that I am a rational and mortal animal? [Note: *animal rationale mortale*]. I know it, and I confess that this is what I am.”
  Then she said: “Do you not know yourself to be anything else?”
  “Nothing.”
  “Now,” she said, “I know the other, or rather the main, cause of your sickness: you have forgotten what you yourself are. Therefore, I have fully discovered the reason for your disease and the way to restore your health. Because you are confused by the loss of your self-identity, you lament that you are an exile and stripped of your possessions. Because you do not know the end of things, you think that wicked and abandoned men are powerful and happy. Because you have forgotten by what laws the world is governed,

***

###

--- Page 59 ---
you believe that the changes of Fortune fluctuate without a guide. These are grave causes, leading not only to sickness but to death. 
  But thanks be to the Author of Health, for nature has not yet entirely forsaken you. We have the best spark for your recovery in your true opinion concerning the governance of the world—that you believe it subject not to random chance, but to divine reason. Therefore, have no fear; from this tiny spark, the vital heat shall again blaze up. 
  But because it is not yet time for stronger remedies, and since it is the nature of the mind that when it throws away true opinions, it takes up false ones, from which a mist of distraction rises to obscure the true vision, I will try to thin this mist by gentle and moderate applications. Thus, when the darkness of deceptive passions is removed, you may be able to recognize the splendor of the true light.”

***

###

Stars shrouded
In black clouds
Can cast no light.
If the North Wind,
Stirring the sea,
Whirls the tide,
The water, which was
Once clear as glass
And bright as the fair day,
Is soon fouled
With filth and mud,
Stirred up by the storm.
The wandering stream,
Flowing down 
From the high mountains,
Is often blocked
By a stone 
Torn from the rock.

If thou also art eager

      To discern the truth,
And in the right path
   Firmly to persevere,

Joy thou must banish,

Nor let grief be present:
   Clouded is the mind,
      And bound in shackles,

grief. If thou wilt recall her nature, her character, and her merit, thou shalt realize that in her, thou didst never possess anything of value, nor hast thou lost anything of worth. I summon, therefore, the persuasiveness of Rhetoric’s sweetness—which only proceeds on the right path when it does not depart from my precepts—and I command Music, the handmaid of my house, to join in, sounding now lighter and now more solemn measures.

### PAGE 67
**[Original: Liber II, Prosa I, cont.]**

Thou hast seen, I think, something strange and unprecedented. Thou imaginest that Fortune hath changed toward thee; thou art mistaken. Such were always her ways; such is her very nature. In her very mutability, she hath maintained toward thee her only constant quality. Such was she when she seduced thee with charms, when she mocked thee with the allurements of a feigned felicity. Thou hast discovered the ambiguous face of the blind goddess. She who still conceals herself from others hath revealed herself entirely to thee. If thou approvest of her, adapt thyself to her ways and complain not. If thou dreadest her perfidy, despise her and cast her off, for her play is ever lethal. For that which is now the cause of thy grief ought to have been the source of thy peace. Thou hast been deserted by a mistress whom no man can possess with certainty. Dost thou truly prize a happiness that is destined to pass away? Is the presence of Fortune dear to thee, when she is not to be trusted to remain, and her departure brings only agony?
