### PAGE 68
**[Original: Liber II, Prosa I, cont.]**

If she cannot be detained at one’s pleasure, and her flight brings ruin, what is this fleeting presence but a herald of coming calamity? It is not enough to behold only what is before one's eyes; wisdom contemplates the end of things. The very instability of Fortune, in both aspects, makes her threats lose their terror and her flatteries lose their charm. Finally, thou must bear with equanimity whatever occurs within the court [Lat. *area*] of Fortune, once thou hast submitted thy neck to her yoke. But if thou attemptest to prescribe a law of remaining or departing to her whom thou hast freely chosen as thy sovereign, art thou not acting unjustly? Art thou not exacerbating thy lot by thy impatience, which thou canst not change? If thou shouldst commit thy sails to the winds, thou wouldst move not where thou willest, but where the blasts drive thee. If thou entrustest thy seeds to the furrows, thou must balance the lean years against the fruitful. Thou hast submitted thyself to the rule of Fortune; thou must conform to the whims of thy mistress. Dost thou truly...

**SONG I.**

**FORTUNE’S MALICE.**

When Fortune with her haughty hand turns the tide,
More shifting than Euripus’ surging swell,
She tramples mighty kings in her cruel pride,
And lifts the conquered from the depths of hell.
She hears no wail of those in misery caught,
But mocks the tears her own deceits have bred;
Such is her sport; thus is her power taught,
A monstrous marvel by her caprice led:
That in one hour a man may reach the sky,
Then, cast away, in wretched ruins lie.

**[Scholarly Note: *Euripus* refers to the Euripus Strait between Euboea and Boeotia, famous in antiquity for its violent and erratic tidal currents.]**

**II.**

“Now, I wish to dispute with thee briefly using Fortune’s own arguments. Consider whether her demands are just: ‘O Man,’ she might say, ‘why dost thou weary me with thy daily complaints? When Nature brought thee forth from the womb, I found thee naked and lacking all things. I took thee up and nourished thee with my own resources. Out of my favor, I raised thee with a certain indulgence—and it is this very thing that now makes thee impatient with me. I surrounded thee...

<meta>This page continues the summary of Book II of Boethius’s "The Consolation of Philosophy," following the breakdown of chapters and themes initiated on Page 63.</meta>

### PAGE 64
**[Original: Liber II, Prosa I, cont.]**

Fortune are extrinsic; they possess no inherent value and are incapable of conferring true happiness upon the soul. <note>In Boethian thought, "extrinsic" goods are those outside the self—wealth, jewels, and physical beauty—which can be taken away as easily as they were given.</note> — **Ch. VI.** High office and power are shown to be equally hollow; they do not possess innate goodness, as they often fall to the most wicked of men and fail to bestow virtue upon their holders. — **Ch. VII.** The pursuit of fame and glory is exposed as a triviality, for the earth is but a tiny point in the vastness of the heavens, and the memory of a name is soon extinguished by the passage of time. — **Ch. VIII.** The philosopher concludes with a paradox: "Adverse" Fortune is actually more beneficial to man than "Prosperous" Fortune. While Prosperity lies and deceives with a mask of happiness, Adversity is honest, teaching the instability of human affairs and revealing the faces of true friends. <note>This final chapter of the section introduces the famous Boethian concept that we learn more from our failures and losses than from our successes.</note>

**PROSE I.**

**THE TWO FACES OF FORTUNE**

After this, Philosophy remained silent for a short time. When she had captured my attention by her very silence, she began thus: "If I have correctly identified the causes of your sickness, you are wasting away with longing for your former state of life. The change in your <term>Fortune</term><note>Latin: "Fortuna." Often personified as a goddess who turns a wheel, representing the random and cyclical nature of luck and status.</note> has so overcome you. You are mistaken if you think that this power has changed her character toward you. This is her constant nature; this is her very essence. She has preserved her own constancy toward you in the very act of being inconstant. Such was she when she smiled upon you, when she mocked you with the allurements of a false happiness."

"You have discovered the double face of this blind goddess. To others she is still veiled; to you she is now fully revealed. If you find her ways acceptable, then use her gifts without complaint. But if you recoil from her treachery, despise her and thrust her away, for her 'games' are truly dangerous."

"For what is the cause of this great grief? Is it because she has deserted you? Yet, no man can ever be secure when she is present. Do you value a happiness that is destined to pass away? Is the presence of Fortune dear to you, when she cannot stay forever, and when her departure brings only sorrow?"

<meta>This page continues Lady Philosophy’s address to Boethius. Having established on the previous page that she would use "gentle draughts" of Rhetoric and Music to prepare his mind, she now begins to describe the true nature of Fortune. She argues that Boethius's suffering comes from a misunderstanding: he thinks Fortune has changed, but her nature is, in fact, to be eternally changing.</meta>

[[original page: 45]]

->*The Vanity of Fortune's Gifts.* 45<-

I think you have witnessed some strange and unusual sight. You believe Fortune has changed her attitude toward you, but you are mistaken. These have always been her ways; this is simply her nature. In fact, in her very **mutability**<term>changeability; the quality of being subject to change</term>, she has maintained her only true **constancy**<note>original: *constantiam*—Lady Philosophy uses a paradox here: Fortune is only "constant" in the sense that she is "constantly changing."</note> toward you. 

This is how she was when she showered you with affection and deceived you with the charms of a false happiness. You have discovered the shifting face of the **blind goddess**<note>original: *caecae numinis*—Fortune (Fortuna) was traditionally depicted with a blindfold to show that she distributes wealth and power randomly, without regard for a person's merit or character.</note>. She who remains hidden from others has now fully revealed her entire character to you. 

If you like her, accept her as she is and do not complain. If you loathe her **perfidy**<term>deceitfulness or betrayal of trust</term>, then turn away from her in disdain and renounce her, for her delusions are destructive. The very thing that is now the cause of your great grief should actually have brought you peace of mind. You have been abandoned by one whom no one can ever be certain will stay. 

Do you truly place any value on a happiness that is destined to leave? I ask you again: Is the presence of Fortune dear to you if she cannot be trusted to remain, and if her departure inevitably brings such sorrow?

[[vocabulary: **mutability**<term>the state of being liable to change</term>, **blind goddess (Fortune)**<note>the personification of luck or fate</note>, **constancy**<term>the quality of being faithful and dependable</term>, **perfidy**<term>the state of being deceitful and untrustworthy</term>]]

<summary>Lady Philosophy explains that Fortune has not "changed" her character by abandoning Boethius; rather, her nature is defined by change itself. She argues that Boethius should not grieve the loss of something so inherently unstable and untrustworthy.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Lady Philosophy's lecture. Having previously explained that Fortune is naturally inconsistent, she now argues that Boethius, by accepting Fortune's initial favors, essentially entered into a contract with her. She uses analogies of sailing and farming to show that once one submits to a process, one must accept all its possible outcomes—both good and bad.</meta>

Why, if she [Fortune] cannot be kept at your pleasure, and if her departure brings such ruin, what is this fleeting visitor but a sign of coming trouble? Truly, it is not enough to look only at what lies right before your eyes; wisdom weighs the eventual outcomes of things. This same **mutability**<term>changeability; the quality of being subject to change</term>, with its two sides, makes the threats of Fortune lose their terror, and her favors lose their appeal. 

Finally, you ought to endure whatever takes place within the boundaries of Fortune’s **demesne**<term>territory or domain; the sphere over which someone has control</term>, since you have placed your head beneath her **yoke**<term>a wooden beam used on oxen to pull a load; here, a symbol of submission or servitude</term>. But if you wish to dictate when she should stay or go—she whom you chose of your own accord to be your mistress—are you not acting wrongly? Are you not making your lot more bitter by being impatient with a destiny you cannot alter? 

If you committed your sails to the winds, you would voyage not where you intended to go, but where the winds drove you. If you entrusted your seed to the fields, you would have to balance the fruitful years against the barren ones. You have surrendered yourself to the sway of Fortune; therefore, you must submit to your mistress’s **caprices**<term>sudden, unpredictable changes of mood or behavior</term>. What! Are you truly—

->SONG I.<-

->FORTUNE’S MALICE<-

Mad Fortune sweeps along in reckless pride,
As fickle as the **Euripus**<term>a strait in Greece known for strong, fluctuating currents, often used in ancient literature as a symbol of instability</term> in its surging tide;
Now she tramples mighty kings beneath her feet;
Now she sets the conquered in the victor’s seat.
She ignores the cries of hopeless woe,
And mocks the very grief that her own mischief causes.
This is her sport; this is how she proves her power;
And it is a great wonder when, in one brief hour,

Then plunged headlong into the **abyss**<term>a deep or bottomless chasm; here, a state of total despair or ruin</term> of misery.

->II.<-

   ‘Now I would also like to reason with you a little using **Fortune’s** own words. Observe for yourself whether her arguments are fair. “Human,” she might say, “why do you pursue me with your daily complaints? What wrong have I done to you? What goods of yours have I taken away? Choose any judge you wish, and let us argue before them concerning the rightful ownership of **wealth** and **rank**<term>social standing or high office</term>. If you can succeed in showing that any of these things are the true property of mortal man, I will freely grant that those things you claim are yours. When nature brought you forth from your mother’s womb, I took you, naked and destitute as you were. I nourished you with my own resources, and in my **partiality**<note>original: "favoris mei partitione" — here meaning a biased or special favor shown to a preferred individual</note> for you, I brought you up perhaps a bit too indulgently; it is this very kindness that now makes you rebellious against me. --- Page 71 ---
**[[original page: 49]]**
**-> *The Vanity of Fortune’s Gifts.* 49 <-**

...with a royal abundance of all those things which were within my power. Now it is my pleasure to withdraw my hand. You have cause to give thanks for the use of things that were not your own; you have no right to complain as if you had lost what was entirely yours. Why then do you bemoan yourself? I have done you no violence. Wealth, honors, and all such things are under my jurisdiction. My handmaidens recognize their mistress; they come with me, and they depart when I leave. I might boldly affirm that if those things, the loss of which you lament, had been truly yours, you could never have lost them. Am I alone to be forbidden to exercise my own right? The sky is permitted to bring forth bright days, and then to bury those same days in the darkness of night. The year is permitted now to deck the face of the earth with flowers and fruits, and now to disfigure it with storms and cold. The sea is allowed to invite with a calm surface today, and tomorrow to grow rough with waves and tempests. Shall the insatiable greed of man bind me to a constancy that is foreign to my own character? This 
**-> 4 <-**

[[vocabulary: insatiate greed, constancy]]

is my art; this is the game I play without ceasing. I turn my wheel in its whirling circle; I delight to see the low brought high and the high brought low. Ascend, if you wish, but on this condition: that you do not consider it a hardship to descend when the rules of my game demand it. Were you ignorant of my habits? Did you not know how **Croesus**, King of the Lydians, once the formidable rival of **Cyrus**, was later pitiably consigned to the flames of the pyre, only to be saved by a shower sent from heaven? Has it escaped you [[abbrev: ’scaped → escaped]] how **Paullus** shed pious tears for the misfortunes of King **Perseus**, his prisoner? What else does the outcry of tragedies lament but the overthrow of kingdoms by the indiscriminate strokes of Fortune? Did you not learn as a youth that there stand at the threshold of **Zeus** “two jars,” the one full of evils, the other of blessings? What if you have drawn more liberally from the good jar? What if I have not even now entirely deserted you? What if this very mutability of mine is a just ground for hoping for better things? But listen now,

**-> SONG II. <-**
**-> MAN’S COVETOUSNESS. <-**

Though Plenty, with a lavish hand,
Should pour out wealth as grand
As the stars that count the night,
Or the sands in shifting light,
The race of man would not give o'er
Its cries and complaints for more.

Though God, all-bounteous, should grant
Gold to satisfy every want—
And heap up honors, rank, and fame—
The soul is never truly tame;
For all-devouring greed still cries,
And with each gift, its hunger sighs.

What bridle can restrain the force
Of lust upon its frantic course,
When, fed by bounty’s rising flood,
It thirsts for more than worldly good?
He is never rich who lives in fear
That grim-faced Want is drawing near.

**-> III. <-**

‘If Fortune should plead thus against you, truly you would not have a single word to offer in response. Or, if you can find any legal justification for your complaints, you must produce it. I will give you the opportunity to speak.’
Then I said: ‘Indeed, your arguments are plausible, steeped as they are in the honeyed sweetness of Rhetoric and Music. But they charm only while they are sounding in the ear; the sense of his misfortunes lies deeper in the heart of the wretched. Therefore, when the sound ceases to vibrate, the indwelling sorrow of the heart returns with renewed bitterness.’
Then she replied: ‘It is as you say. For these are not yet the remedies for your sickness, but merely lenitives to assist in the treatment of a malady that has been hitherto obstinate. The remedies that go deep I shall apply in due season. Nevertheless, to counteract

[[vocabulary: rhetoric, lenitives, malady]]

***

###

--- Page 75 ---
**[[original page: 53]]**
**-> *The Vanity of Fortune’s Gifts.* 53 <-**

your determination to be thought wretched, I ask you: Have you forgotten the extent and the limits of your former happiness? I say nothing of how, when you were orphaned and desolate, you were taken into the care of the highest men; how you were chosen for alliance with the foremost citizens of the state—and even before you were joined to their family by marriage, you were already dear to their affection, which is the most precious of ties. Who did not proclaim you most happy in the virtues of your wife, the brilliance of her father, and the blessing of your sons? I pass over—for I do not care to dwell on blessings shared by others—those distinctions often denied to the old, which you enjoyed in your youth. I choose rather to come to the unparalleled peak of your good fortune. If the enjoyment of any earthly success has weight in the balance of happiness, can the memory of that splendor be swept away by any rising tide of troubles? That day when you saw your two sons ride forth from home as **joint consuls**, escorted by a line of senators and cheered by the goodwill of the people; when they sat in

[[vocabulary: felicity, joint consuls, male issue]]

--- Page 76 ---
**[[original page: 54]]**
**-> 54 *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-**

the **curule chairs** in the Senate-house, and you, by your **panegyric** on the king, earned the fame of eloquence and talent; when in the Circus, seated between the two consuls, you satisfied the expectations of the multitude thronging around you with a triumphal **largesse**—methinks you outwitted Fortune while she caressed you and held you as her darling. You carried off a prize which she had never before granted to any private individual. Are you now minded to settle an account with Fortune? This is the first time she has looked upon you with a jealous eye. If you compare the number and the weight of your blessings and misfortunes, you cannot deny that you are still fortunate. Or if you do not consider yourself favored because your former prosperity has departed, neither should you consider yourself wretched, since what you now believe to be calamitous is also passing away. Are you a stranger to the theater of this life, having only just arrived? Do you think there is any stability in human affairs, when man himself is dissolved by the swift course of time? Even if

**-> SONG III. <-**
**-> ALL PASSES. <-**

When **Phoebus**, in his rosy car,
Brings the dawn from realms afar,
The stars that glittered in the sky
Before his flaming banners fly.
When the grove, by **Zephyrs** fed,
Blushes with the rose's red,
If the North Wind [**Auster**] breathes his cold,
The thorns are all that we behold.
Often the sea lies calm and still,
Obedient to the quiet’s will;
Often the raging tempests rise,
And toss the salt spray to the skies.
If the world’s face changes so,
In a restless, shifting flow,
Will you trust in man’s estate?
Will you trust a fleeting fate?
One law stands for things of birth:
Nothing constant stays on earth.

**-> IV. <-**

Then I said: ‘What you say is true, O nurse of all virtues; nor can I deny the rapid course of my prosperity. But this is what parches me with more cruel pain: for in every adversity of fortune, the most wretched kind of misfortune is *to have been happy*.’
‘Well,’ she replied, ‘if you are suffering the penalty for a false opinion, you cannot rightly blame the circumstances. For if it is the name of "accidental happiness" that moves you, let us calculate with me how rich you still are in the number and weight of your blessings. If, by the gift of Providence, that which you held most precious in your whole fortune is still preserved for you, safe and inviolate, can you speak of ill-fortune while still possessing

[[vocabulary: adverse fortune, felicity]]

--- Page 79 ---
**[[original page: 57]]**
**-> *The Vanity of Fortune’s Gifts.* 57 <-**

Fortune’s best gifts? **Symmachus**, your father-in-law—a man who is the very honor of the human race—is safe and sound. A man composed of wisdom and virtue, he is unhurt by your injuries and bewails your wrongs. Your wife still lives, a woman of modest character, distinguished by her chastity, and—to sum up all her gifts—the true daughter of her father. She lives, I say, and though she loathes this life, she preserves her breath for your sake alone, wasting away in tears and grief for your absence; in this alone I might grant that your happiness is diminished. What shall I say of your sons, the **consular dignitaries**, in whom already shines the character of their father and grandfather? Since, therefore, the primary concern of mortals is to preserve life, O how happy you would be—if you only recognized your blessings—you who still possess things that no one doubts are dearer than life itself! Therefore,

[[vocabulary: Symmachus, peerless modesty, consular dignity]]

dry your tears. Fortune has not yet turned her hatred against all your loved ones; the storm that has assailed you is not yet overwhelming, since there are anchors still holding fast which allow you both consolation in the present and hope for the future.’
‘I pray,’ I said, ‘that they may hold fast. For as long as they remain, I shall somehow float through, no matter how things go. But you see how much of my former splendor has been shorn away.’
‘We have moved forward a little,’ she said, ‘if there is something in your lot that does not yet cause you total weariness. But I cannot endure your luxury [daintiness] when you complain with such agonizing grief because something is lacking from your happiness. For who is of such perfectly settled happiness that he does not have some quarrel with the state of his affairs? The condition of human blessings is a troubled thing; it either never comes in full, or never remains stable. One man possesses abundant wealth, but is ashamed of his ignoble birth. Another is noble in birth,

**[English]**
...but through the embarrassments of poverty would prefer to be obscure. A third, richly endowed with both, laments the loneliness of an unwedded life. Another, though happily married, is doomed to childlessness, and nurses his wealth for a stranger to inherit. Yet another, blest with children, mournfully bewails the misdeeds of son or daughter. Wherefore, it is not easy for anyone to be at perfect peace with the circumstances of his lot. There lurks in each several portion something which they who experience it not know nothing of, but which makes the sufferer wince. Besides, the more favoured a man is by Fortune, the more fastidiously sensitive is he; and, unless all things answer to his whim, he is overwhelmed by the most trifling misfortunes, because utterly unschooled in adversity. So petty are the trifles which rob the most fortunate of perfect happiness! How many are there, dost thou imagine, who would think themselves nigh heaven, if but a small portion from the wreck of thy fortune should fall to them? This very place which thou callest exile is to them that

**[Latin: Liber II, Prosa 4 cont.]**
*...dwell therein their native land. Nihil est enim miserum nisi cum putes, contraque omnis fortuna felix est si animo aequo toleres. Quis est ille tam felix qui cum semel impatentiae frenos laxaverit, statum suum mutare non optet? Mellibus humanis quanta est aspersa amaritudo! Quae si etiam fruenti iucunda esse videatur, tamen quo minus cum velit abeat retineri non possit. Liquet igitur quam sit misera mortalium bonorum beatitudo, quae nec apud aequanimis perpetua perdurat nec anxios tota delectat. Quid igitur, o mortales, extra petitis intra vos positam felicitatem? Error vos perturbatioque confundit. Ostendam tibi breviter ipsius cardinem beatitudinis. Estne aliquid tibi te ipso pretiosius? Nihil, inquies. **[English]**
dwell therein their native land. So true is it that nothing is wretched, but thinking makes it so, and conversely every lot is happy if borne with equanimity. Who is so blest by Fortune as not to wish to change his state, if once he gives rein to a rebellious spirit? With how many bitternesses is the sweetness of human felicity blent! And even if that sweetness seem to him to bring delight in the enjoying, yet he cannot keep it from departing when it will. How manifestly wretched, then, is the bliss of earthly fortune, which lasts not for ever with those whose temper is equable, and can give no perfect satisfaction to the anxious-minded!

‘Why, then, ye children of mortality, seek ye from without that happiness whose seat is only within us? Error and ignorance bewilder you. I will show thee, in brief, the hinge on which perfect happiness turns. Is there anything more precious to thee than thyself? Nothing, thou wilt say. If, then, thou art master of thyself, thou wilt possess that which thou wilt never be willing to lose, and which Fortune cannot take from thee. And that thou

**[Latin: Liber II, Prosa 4 cont.]**
*...atque ut agnoscas in his fortuitis rebus beatitudinem constare non posse, sic collige. Si beatitudo est summum naturae bonum ratione degentis nec est summum bonum quod eripi ullo modo potest, quoniam praestantius est id quod auferri nequit, patet instabilitatem fortunae ad beatitudinem adipiscendam aspirare non posse. Ad haec, quem caduca ista felicitas vehit, aut scit eam mutabilem esse aut nescit. Si nescit, quaenam beata est ex ignorantiae caecitate felicitas? Si scit, metuat necesse est, ne amittat quod amitti posse non dubitat; quare continuus timor non sinit esse felicem. An si amiserit, neglegendum putat? Ita quoque perexile bonum est quod aequo animo feratur amissum. Et quoniam tu is es quem fides capit animos hominum nullo modo perire tecum, cumque pateat fortuitam felicitatem corporis morte terminari, dubitari nequit, si haec beatitudinem afferre potest, quin omne mortalium genus in miseriam morte decidat.*

**[English]**
mayst see that happiness cannot possibly consist in these things which are the sport of chance, reflect that, if happiness is the highest good of a creature living in accordance with reason, and if a thing which can in any wise be reft away is not the highest good, since that which cannot be taken away is better than it, it is plain that Fortune cannot aspire to bestow happiness by reason of its instability. And, besides, a man borne along by this transitory felicity must either know or not know its unstability. If he knows not, how poor is a happiness which depends on the blindness of ignorance! If he knows it, he needs must fear to lose a happiness whose loss he believes to be possible. Wherefore, a never-ceasing fear suffers him not to be happy. Or does he count the possibility of this loss a trifling matter? Insignificant, then, must be the good whose loss can be borne so equably. And, further, I know thee to be one settled in the belief that the souls of men certainly die not with them, and convinced thereof by numerous proofs; it is clear also that the felicity which Fortune bestows is brought to an

[[vocabulary: highest good (summum bonum), reason (ratio), instability (instabilitas), transitory felicity (caduca felicitas)]]

**SONG IV.**
**THE GOLDEN MEAN.**

> *Quisquis volet perennem*
> *Cautus ponere sedem,*
> *Stabilisque nec sonori*
> *Sterni flatibus Euris,*
> *Et fluctibus minantem*
> *Curat spernere pontum,*
> *Montis cacumen alti,*
> *Bibulas vitet harenas.*
> *Illud ventosus ingens*
> *Galeis verberat Eurus,*
> *Hae pendulum solutae*
> *Pondus ferre recusant.*

**[English]**
end with the death of the body: therefore, it cannot be doubted but that, if happiness is conferred in this way, the whole human race sinks into misery when death brings the close of all. But if we know that many have sought the joy of happiness not through death only, but also through pain and suffering, how can life make men happy by its presence when it makes them not wretched by its loss?’

*Sed quoniam rationum iam in te mearum remedia manswescunt, paulo validioribus utendum puto. **[English]**
‘But since my reasonings begin to work a soothing effect within thy mind, methinks I may resort to remedies somewhat stronger. Come, suppose, now, the gifts of Fortune were not fleeting and transitory, what is there in them capable of ever becoming truly thine, or which does not lose value when looked at steadily and fairly weighed in the balance? Are riches, I pray thee, precious either through thy nature or in their own? What are they but mere gold and heaps of money? Yet these fine things show their quality better in the spending than in the hoarding; for I suppose ’tis plain that greed always makes men hateful, while liberality brings fame. But that which is transferred to another cannot remain in one’s own possession; and, if that be so, then money is only precious when it is given away, and, by

**[English]**
being transferred to others, ceases to be one’s own. Again, if all the money in the world were heaped up in one man’s possession, all others would be made poor. Sound fills the ears of many at the same time without being broken into parts, but your riches cannot pass to many without being lessened in the process. And when this happens, they must needs impoverish those whom they leave. How poor and cramped a thing, then, is riches, which more than one cannot possess as an unbroken whole, which falls not to any one man’s lot without the impoverishment of everyone else! Or is it the glitter of gems that allures the eye? Yet, how rarely excellent soever may be their splendour, remember the flashing light is in the jewels, not in the man. Indeed, I greatly marvel at men’s admiration of them; for what can rightly seem beautiful to a being endowed with life and reason, if it lack the movement and structure of life? And although such things do in the end take on them more beauty from their Maker’s care and their own brilliancy, still they in no wise merit your admiration

**[English]**
since their excellence is set at a lower grade than your own.
‘Does the beauty of the fields delight you? Surely, yes; it is a beautiful part of a right beautiful whole. Fitly indeed do we at times enjoy the serene calm of the sea, admire the sky, the stars, the moon, the sun. Yet is any of these thy concern? Dost thou venture to boast thyself of the beauty of any one of them? *Art thou* decked with spring’s flowers? is it *thy* fertility that swelleth in the fruits of autumn? Why art thou moved with empty transports? why embracest thou an alien excellence as thine own? Never will fortune make thine that which the nature of things has excluded from thy ownership. Doubtless the fruits of the earth are given for the sustenance of living creatures. But if thou art content to supply thy wants so far as suffices nature, there is no need to resort to fortune’s bounty. Nature is content with few things, and with a very little of these. If thou art minded to force superfluities upon her when she is satisfied, that which thou addest will prove either unpleasant

‘Or perhaps it is a long train of servants that makes thee happy? Why, if they behave viciously, they are a ruinous burden to thy house, and exceeding dangerous to their own master; while if they are honest, how canst thou count other men’s virtue in the sum of thy possessions? From all which ’tis plainly proved that not one of these things which thou reckonest in the number of thy possessions is really thine. And if there is in them no beauty to be desired, why shouldst thou either grieve for their loss or find joy in their continued possession? While if they are beautiful in their own nature, what is that to thee? They would have been not less pleasing in themselves, though never included among thy possessions. For they derive not their preciousness from being counted in thy riches, but rather thou hast chosen to count them

**[Latin: Liber II, Prosa 5 cont.]**
*Quid autem tanto fortunae strepitu vobis expetitis? Inopiam, credo, reicere copiis nitimini. Atqui hoc vobis in contrarium cedit. Multis enim adminiculis opus est ad tuendam varietatem pretiosae supellectilis, verumque illud est multo indigere eos qui multa possideant, contraque minimum, qui abundantiam suam naturae mensura non superfluitatis ambitu metiantur. Itane autem vobis nullo proprio et insito bonum est, ut in externis sepositisque rebus bona vestra quaeratis? Rerumne condicio vertitur, ut animal divina rationis possessione magnificum non aliter sibi splendere videatur nisi vitreae supellectilis possessione? Et cetera quidem suis contenta sunt; vos autem deo mente similes ab rebus infimis petitis ornamenta naturae praestantissimae nec intellegitis quantam Conditori vestro faciatis iniuriam. Ille genus humanum terris omnibus praestare voluit; vos dignitatem vestram infra infima quaeque detruditis.*

**[English]**
in thy riches because they seemed to thee precious.
‘Then, what seek ye by all this noisy outcry about fortune? To chase away poverty, I ween, by means of abundance. And yet ye find the result just contrary. Why, this varied array of precious furniture needs more accessories for its protection; it is a true saying that they want most who possess most, and, conversely, they want very little who measure their abundance by nature’s requirements, not by the superfluity of vain display. Have ye no good of your own implanted within you, that ye seek your good in things external and separate? Is the nature of things so reversed that a creature divine by right of reason can in no other way be splendid in his own eyes save by the possession of lifeless chattels? Yet, while other things are content with their own, ye who in your intellect are God-like seek from the lowest of things adornment for a nature of supreme excellence, and perceive not how great a wrong ye do your Maker. His will was that mankind should excel all things on earth. Ye thrust down your

[[vocabulary: abundance (copiis), accessories (adminiculis), nature's requirements (naturae mensura), chattels (supellectilis), intellect (mente)]]

As a scholarly translator, I have identified the source text as Boethius’s *De Consolatione Philosophiae*, Book II, Prose 5 through the beginning of Prose 7. The OCR text provided is an existing English translation (specifically that of H.R. James, 1897).

### PAGE 91
[[original page: 69]]
**[Book II, Prose 5, cont.]**
**The Vanity of Fortune's Gifts.**

...worth beneath the lowest of things. For if that in which each thing finds its good is clearly more precious than the thing whose good it is, then by your own estimation you place yourselves below the vilest of objects when you judge these cheap things to be your "good." Nor does this occur without merit. Indeed, the condition of human nature is such that man only excels other things when he knows himself; but he is relegated to a status lower than the beasts if he ceases to know himself. For in other living creatures, the ignorance of self is natural; in man, it is a moral defect. 

How far-reaching, then, is this error of yours, to believe that anything can be made beautiful by ornaments that are not its own! It cannot be. For if a thing gains luster from what is added to it, it is the additions that are praised; however, that which is covered and veiled remains in its own inherent foulness. Furthermore, I deny that anything is a *good* which harms its possessor. Is this false? "No," you say. And yet, riches have frequently harmed those who possessed them, since every desperate man—who is all the more covetous because of his wickedness—believes himself alone...

[[vocabulary: self-knowledge (*se cognoscit*), virtue, adornments (*ornamentis*)]]

***

### PAGE 92
[[original page: 70]]
**70 The Consolation of Philosophy.**

...worthy to possess all the gold and gems the world holds. You, therefore, who now tremble at the spear and the sword, if you had entered the path of this life as a penniless traveler, you would sing in the face of the thief [1]. O, the distinguished excellence of mortal wealth! Once you have acquired it, you cease to be secure.

**72 The Consolation of Philosophy.**

**VI.**

What shall I say of dignities and power, which you—ignorant of true power and true dignity—extol to the heavens? When these fall to the most wicked of men, what Etnas belching with shifting flames, what deluges have ever caused such slaughter? Surely, as I think you remember, your ancestors desired to abolish the Consular power (which had been the beginning of liberty) because of the pride of the Consuls, just as earlier they had removed the Kingly name from the city for that same pride. 

But if—as happens rarely—honors are conferred upon virtuous men, what else is pleasing in them but the integrity of those who use them? Thus, honor does not come to virtue from the dignity, but rather dignity comes to the honor from the virtue. Consider, moreover, the nature of that power you find so magnificent and enviable. Do you not consider, O...

[[vocabulary: consular power (*consulare imperium*), rank, power, virtue]]

***

### PAGE 95
[[original page: 73]]
**The Vanity of Fortune's Gifts. 73**

...creatures of earth, over whom you think you rule? If you saw a mouse claiming for himself jurisdiction and power over other mice, to what laughter would you be moved! Yet, if you consider the body, what could you find more fragile than man, whom the bite of a tiny fly or even a worm creeping into his vitals may often kill? 

Indeed, how can anyone exercise right over another, except over the body, or that which is lower than the body—I mean Fortune? Can you ever command a free mind? Can you move a soul established in its own peace and joined to reason? When a certain tyrant [2] thought he could drive a free man through torture to betray his fellow conspirators, the man bit off his own tongue and spat it into the face of the raging tyrant. Thus, the tortures which the tyrant thought were an instrument of cruelty, the sage turned into an opportunity for virtue. Furthermore, what is there that one man can do to another, which he himself might not have to endure...

[[vocabulary: mouse tribe (*murem*), free spirit, reason, tyrant]]

***

### PAGE 96
[[original page: 74]]
**74 The Consolation of Philosophy.**

...in his turn? We are told that Busiris, who used to kill his guests, was himself slain by his guest, Hercules. Regulus had put in chains many of the Carthaginians captured in war; but not long after, he himself yielded his own hands to the chains of the vanquished. Do you then consider him to have power, who cannot prevent another from doing to him exactly what he does to others?

Furthermore, if there were any natural or inherent good in these dignities and powers, they would never fall to the wicked. For contraries are not accustomed to be joined; nature refuses the union of opposites. Therefore, since there is no doubt that the most wicked men often hold these offices, it is also clear that such things are not good by their own nature, as they allow themselves to be joined with the wicked. This same judgment may rightfully be applied to all the gifts of Fortune, which are distributed more abundantly to the most criminal. 

This must also be considered: no one doubts that a man is brave in whom they have...

[[vocabulary: Busiris, Hercules, Regulus, contraries, Fortune]]

### PAGE 97
[[original page: 75]]
**The Vanity of Fortune's Gifts. 75**

...perceived the quality of bravery. And it is clear that he who has speed is swift. So too, music makes men musical, medicine makes them physicians, and rhetoric makes them orators. For the nature of each thing performs its own proper function; it is not confused by the effects of contrary things, and it naturally repels what is opposed to it. 

And yet, wealth cannot extinguish insatiable avarice; nor can power make a man master of himself if he is bound by the unbreakable chains of vicious lusts. When dignity is conferred upon the wicked, it does not make them worthy, but rather exposes and reveals their unworthiness. Why does this happen? Because you delight in giving false names to things that are quite different in nature—names which are easily refuted by the reality of the things themselves. Therefore, these riches, that power, this dignity, are not what they are called. Finally, the same may be concluded regarding the whole of Fortune: there is nothing in her to be desired, nothing of intrinsic goodness, for she...

[[vocabulary: music, rhetoric, dignity (*dignitas*), Fortune]]

**SONG VI.**
**NERO'S INFAMY.**

> We know how many ruins he caused—
> He who burned the city, who slew the Senators,
> Who, in his cruelty, once killed his brother
> And grew wet with the blood of his mother.

> Without a single tear falling on his face;
> He stood as a judge over the beauty
> Of his own dead mother.
>

> From where the sun rises to where it sets,
> From where the frozen Northern Wain [3] hangs
> To where the parching South-wind burns the sands.
> 
> Could such high power ever curb
> The madness of the wicked Nero?
> Alas! What a heavy fate it is, whenever
> The cruel sword is joined to deadly poison!

**The Vanity of Fortune's Gifts. 77**

**VII.**

Then I said: "You yourself know that the ambition for mortal things has never much ruled me. However, I have desired the means for managing affairs, lest virtue should grow silent and age away in lack of use."

Then she replied: "This is the one thing that can entice minds which are naturally excellent, yet not yet led to the final finishing of their virtues: namely, the desire for glory and the reputation for having deserved well of the commonwealth. But consider how small and empty such glory is. 

As you have learned from the demonstrations of the astronomers, the whole circumference of the earth is like a mere point when compared to the vastness of the heavens. That is, if it is compared to the magnitude of the celestial sphere, it is judged to have no space at all. Of this tiny...

[[vocabulary: ambition, glory (*gloria*), commonweal (*re publica*), astronomy]]

***

### PAGE 100
[[original page: 78]]
**78 The Consolation of Philosophy.**

...portion of the world, it is only a fourth part—as you have learned from the proofs of Ptolemy—which is inhabited by living things known to us. If you subtract from this fourth part the space occupied by seas and marshes, and the regions parched by waterless deserts, only the narrowest area is left for human habitation. 

Do you then, who are hemmed in and shut within this smallest point of a point, consider how to broadcast your fame or promote your name? What can glory have that is grand or magnificent, when it is constricted within such small and narrow limits? 

Furthermore, this narrow enclosure of your habitation is populated by many nations, differing in language, customs, and their whole way of life. Because of the difficulty of travel, the diversity of speech, and the rarity of commerce, the fame not only of individual men, but even of great cities, cannot reach them. In the time of Marcus Tullius [Cicero], as he himself mentions somewhere, the fame of the Roman Republic had not yet crossed the Caucasus mountains, though...

[[vocabulary: Ptolemy, Cicero, Caucasus, renown]]

***

**Scholarly Notes on the Translation:**

1.  **"In the robber's face"**: A reference to Juvenal, *Satires* X.22: *"Cantabit vacuus coram latrone viator"* (The empty-handed traveler will sing in the face of the robber).
2.  **"A certain tyrant"**: Traditionally identified as the philosopher Zeno of Elea (not the Stoic), who was tortured by the tyrant Nearchus (or Diomedon). 
3.  **"Northern Wain"**: The Latin *Septem... triones* refers to the seven stars of the Great Bear (Ursa Major). 
4.  **Abbreviations/Readings**: The Latin text of Boethius is remarkably stable in the manuscript tradition (e.g., *Codex Bambergensis*). In Prose 6, the mention of "Etna" functions as a recurring trope for uncontrollable destructive power. The phrase "last infirmity" in Prose 7 (*solum hoc... quod possit*) is the source of Milton's famous line in *Lycidas*.

-> 72 *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

-> VI. <-

'What now shall I say of rank and power, whereby, because ye know not true power and dignity, ye hope to reach the sky? Yet, when rank and power have fallen to the worst of men, did ever an Etna <note>original: "Aetna." A reference to the active volcano in Sicily, used here as a metaphor for the destructive, uncontainable nature of a wicked ruler.</note>, belching forth flame and fiery deluge, work such mischief? Verily, as I think, thou dost remember how thine ancestors sought to abolish the <term>consular power</term> <note>The office of "Consul" was the highest civil and military rank in the Roman Republic. Two were elected each year to serve as a check on one another’s power.</note>, which had been the foundation of their liberties, on account of the overweening pride of the consuls, and how for that self-same pride they had already abolished the kingly title! <note>This refers to the Roman Revolution in 509 BCE, when the Roman people overthrew their last king, Tarquin the Proud, and established a Republic to avoid the tyranny of a single ruler.</note> And if, as happens but rarely, these prerogatives are conferred on virtuous men, it is only the virtue of those who exercise them that pleases. So it appears that honour cometh not to virtue from rank, but to rank from virtue. Look, too, at the nature of that power which ye find so attractive and glorious! Do ye never consider, ye mortals...

[[vocabulary: consular power, rank, power, virtue]]

<summary>Lady Philosophy argues that political power and social rank have no inherent value and can even be destructive if held by wicked men. Using Roman history as an example, she suggests that a position is only as honorable as the person who holds it.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument from Prose 6 of Book II. Lady Philosophy is dismantling the idea that political power or social status has any inherent value or authority over the true self. She uses a famous satirical analogy of a "mouse king" and a historical anecdote about a philosopher's defiance of a tyrant to prove that power is an illusion that only affects the physical body, not the mind.</meta>

[[original page: 73]]
-> *The Vanity of Fortune's Gifts.* 73 <-

...creatures of earth, what ye are, and over whom ye exercise your fancied lordship? Suppose, now, that in the mouse tribe <note>original: "murem." This satirical comparison is meant to show that human politics, when viewed from the perspective of the vast universe, are as insignificant as the power struggles of small rodents.</note> there should rise up one claiming rights and powers for himself above the rest, would ye not laugh consumedly? Yet if thou lookest to his body alone, what creature canst thou find more feeble than man, who oftentimes is killed by the bite of a fly, or by some insect creeping into the inner passage of his system!

Yet what rights can one exercise over another, save only as regards the body, and that which is lower than the body—I mean fortune? What! wilt thou bind with thy mandates the free spirit? Canst thou force from its due tranquillity the mind that is firmly composed by reason? A tyrant thought to drive a man of free birth <note>This likely refers to the philosopher Zeno of Elea or Anaxarchus, who both reportedly bit off their own tongues while being tortured by a tyrant (such as Nearchus or Nicocreon) to avoid betraying their comrades in a conspiracy.</note> to reveal his accomplices in a conspiracy, but the prisoner bit off his tongue and threw it into the furious tyrant's face; thus, the tortures which the tyrant thought the instrument of his cruelty the sage made an opportunity for heroism. Moreover, what is there that one man can do to another which he himself may not have to undergo...

| :--- | :--- |

| **tyrant** | A ruler who exercises power without moral restraint, ultimately shown to be powerless over the mind of the wise. |

<meta>This page continues Lady Philosophy’s argument from Prose 6 of Book II. Having just illustrated that physical power cannot touch the free mind, she now provides historical examples to show that those who wield power are often subjected to the very same cruelties they once inflicted on others, proving that worldly power is fleeting and lacks inherent moral value.</meta>

[[original page: 74]]

-> 74 *The Consolation of Philosophy* <-

...in his turn? We are told that Busiris <note>A mythical King of Egypt who sacrificed all visiting foreigners to appease the gods; he was famously killed by Hercules, who had been brought to the altar as a victim.</note>, who used to kill his guests, was himself slain by his guest, Hercules. Regulus <note>Marcus Atilius Regulus (d. 250 BCE), a Roman general during the First Punic War. He was celebrated as a hero of Roman honor because, after being captured by the Carthaginians and sent to Rome to negotiate peace, he advised the Senate against it and voluntarily returned to Carthage to face execution.</note> had thrown into bonds many of the Carthaginians whom he had taken in war; soon after, he himself submitted his hands to the chains of the vanquished. Then, do you think that a man has any power if he cannot prevent another from being able to do to him exactly what he himself can do to others?

Besides, if there were any element of natural and proper good in rank and power, they would never be granted to the utterly wicked, since opposites <term>contraries: a philosophical concept referring to qualities that are mutually exclusive and cannot coexist in the same thing at the same time (e.g., heat and cold)</term> are not accustomed to being associated with one another. Nature does not allow the union of contraries. So, seeing as there is no doubt that wicked wretches are oftentimes set in high places, it is also clear that things which allow themselves to be associated with the worst of men cannot be good in their own nature. 

Indeed, this judgment may with some reason be passed concerning all the gifts of Fortune <note>original: "fortunae." This refers to the external goods like wealth, fame, and power that are subject to chance.</note> which fall so plentifully to the most wicked. This ought also to be considered here, I think: no one doubts a man is brave if they have ob— <note>The text breaks off mid-sentence; Boethius is moving toward the argument that virtues like bravery or wisdom are inherent to a person, unlike the external "gifts" of power or rank.</note>

<summary>Lady Philosophy argues that power is not a true "good" because it is often held by the wicked and provides no protection from misfortune, citing the historical reversals of Busiris and Regulus. She concludes that because "contraries" cannot mix, power cannot be inherently good if it can coexist with evil character.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Lady Philosophy and Boethius. Having established on the previous page that worldly power is fleeting and cannot prevent one from suffering the same fate they inflict on others, Philosophy now argues that worldly "goods" lack the power to transform the character of the person holding them.</meta>

[[original page: 75]]

-> *The Vanity of Fortune's Gifts.* 75 <-

...was observed a brave spirit residing. It is plain that one who is endowed with speed is swift-footed. So also music makes men musical, the healing art makes physicians, and rhetoric <term>rhetoric: the ancient art of persuasive public speaking and logical argument</term> makes public speakers. For each of these has naturally its own proper working; there is no confusion with the effects of contrary <term>contraries: a philosophical concept where two opposite qualities—like heat and cold—cannot exist in the same thing at the same time</term> things—nay, even of itself, a skill rejects what is incompatible with it. 

And yet wealth cannot extinguish insatiable greed, nor has power ever made a man master of himself if vicious lusts <gloss>harmful passions</gloss> kept him bound in indissoluble <gloss>unbreakable</gloss> fetters; dignity <note>In this context, "dignity" (Latin: *dignitas*) refers to high-ranking public office or social status rather than personal pride.</note> conferred on the wicked not only fails to make them worthy, but contrarily reveals and displays their unworthiness. Why does it so happen? 

Because you take pleasure in calling things by false names—names whose nature is quite incongruous <note>original: *incongrua*; meaning things that are inconsistent or do not fit the reality of the situation</note> to the things themselves. These names are easily proved false by the very effects of the things; and so it is: these riches, that power, and this dignity are none of them rightly so called. Finally, we may draw the same conclusion concerning the whole sphere of Fortune, within which there is plainly nothing to be truly desired and nothing of intrinsic excellence; for she...

<meta>This page transitions from the specific example of the tyrant Nero to a broader philosophical discussion on the nature of ambition. 'Then I said: "You yourself know that ambition for worldly success has not had much power over me. However, I did desire the opportunity for action, so that my virtue might not grow weak <gloss>languish away</gloss> from a lack of use."

Then she replied: "This is that 'last weakness' <note>Philosophy is referring to the 'last infirmity of noble mind'—the idea that the desire for fame is the hardest worldly passion for a truly great person to give up.</note> which is able to tempt minds that are noble by nature, but have not yet been shaped to a state of perfect refinement by the completion of the virtues—I mean, the love of glory and the desire for a reputation for high services rendered to the public good <term>commonweal: the state or the general welfare of the community</term>. And yet, consider with me how poor and empty a thing this glory is! The entire globe of the earth, as you have learned from the proofs of astronomy, is found to be no larger than a tiny point when compared to the vastness of the heavens. That is to say, if measured against the scale of the celestial sphere, the earth is considered to occupy absolutely no space at all. Now, of this so insignificant..."

<meta>This page continues Philosophy’s geographical argument. Having established that the Earth is a mere point compared to the heavens, she now demonstrates that only a tiny fraction of that point is even habitable, further diminishing the value of human "fame."</meta>

[[original page: 78]]
-> 78 *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

portion of the universe, only about a fourth part, as the proofs of Ptolemy <term>Claudius Ptolemy: a 2nd-century astronomer and geographer whose work was the standard for over a thousand years</term> have taught us, is inhabited by living creatures known to us. If from this fourth part you mentally subtract all that is seized by the seas and marshes, or lies as a vast waste of waterless desert, barely a narrow and cramped area is left for human habitation. You, then, who are shut in and imprisoned in this tiniest fraction of a point’s space, do you really concern yourselves with the broadcasting of your fame or the spreading of your renown? Indeed, what kind of greatness or magnificence can glory have when it is confined to such narrow and petty limits?

Besides, the cramped boundaries of this meager dwelling-place are inhabited by many nations differing widely in speech, in customs, and in their mode of life. To many of these, because of the difficulty of travel, the diversity of languages, and the lack of trade, the fame not only of individual men, but even of entire cities, is unable to reach. Why, in the days of Cicero <term>Marcus Tullius Cicero: the famous Roman statesman and orator</term>, as he himself points out in his writings, the fame of the Roman Republic had not yet even crossed the Caucasus <note>The Caucasus Mountains, then considered a remote and formidable boundary of the known world</note> mountains—and yet by that time her

<summary>Philosophy argues that human fame is physically limited; because the Earth is mostly uninhabitable and divided by geography and language, true "global" renown is an impossibility.</summary>

### Scholarly Note on the Text
The provided OCR text is an English translation of **Boethius’ *De Consolatione Philosophiae*** (specifically Book II, Prose 7 through the Summary of Book III). The style and content correspond to the 1897 translation by H.R. James. Per your instructions, I have refined the text into a scholarly English edition, cleaning OCR artifacts while preserving the structure, proper nouns, and formatting of the source.

***

### PAGE 101
**[Original Page 79]**
**### The Vanity of Fortune's Gifts ###**

[The Roman] name had grown formidable to the Parthians and other nations of those parts. Seest thou, then, how narrow, how confined, is the glory ye take pains to spread abroad and extend! Can the fame of a single Roman penetrate where the glory of the Roman name fails to pass? Moreover, the customs and institutions of different races agree not together, so that what is deemed praiseworthy in one country is thought punishable in another. Wherefore, if any love the applause of fame, it shall not profit him to publish his name among many peoples. Then, each must be content to have the range of his glory limited to his own people; the splendid immortality of fame must be confined within the bounds of a single race.

Once more, how many of high renown in their own times have been lost in oblivion for want of a record! Indeed, of what avail are written records even, which, with their authors, are overtaken by the dimness of age after a somewhat longer time? But ye, when ye think on future fame, fancy it an immortality that ye are begetting for yourselves. Why, if...

***

### PAGE 102
**[Original Page 80]**
**### 80 The Consolation of Philosophy ###**

...thou scannest the infinite spaces of eternity, what room hast thou left for rejoicing in the durability of thy name? Verily, if a single moment's space be compared with ten thousand years, it has a certain relative duration, however little, since each period is definite. But this same number of years—ay, and a number many times as great—cannot even be compared with endless duration; for, indeed, finite periods may in a sort be compared one with another, but a finite and an infinite never. 

So it comes to pass that fame, though it extend to ever so wide a space of years, if it be compared to never-lessening eternity, seems not short-lived merely, but altogether nothing. But as for you, ye know not how to act aright, unless it be to court the popular breeze, and win the empty applause of the multitude—nay, ye abandon the superlative worth of conscience and virtue, and ask a recompense from the poor words of others. Let me tell thee how wittily one did mock the shallowness of this sort of arrogance. A certain man assailed one who had put on the name of philosopher as a cloak to pride and vain-glory, not for...

***

### PAGE 103
**[Original Page 81]**
**### The Vanity of Fortune's Gifts. 81 ###**

...the practice of real virtue, and added: "Now shall I know if thou art a philosopher if thou bearest reproaches calmly and patiently." The other for a while affected to be patient, and, having endured to be abused, cried out derisively: "Now, do you see that I am a philosopher?" The other, with biting sarcasm, retorted: "I should have [hadst thou] [^1] held thy peace." 

Moreover, what concern have choice spirits—for it is of such men we speak, men who seek glory by virtue—what concern, I say, have these with fame after the dissolution of the body in death's last hour? For if men die wholly—which our reasonings forbid us to believe—there is no such thing as glory at all, since he to whom the glory is said to belong is altogether non-existent. But if the mind, conscious of its own rectitude, is released from its earthly prison, and seeks heaven in free flight, doth it not despise all earthly things when it rejoices in its deliverance from earthly bonds, and enters upon the joys of heaven?

**### 82 The Consolation of Philosophy ###**

**SONG VII**
**GLORY MAY NOT LAST**

Oh, let him, who pants for glory's guerdon,
Deeming glory all in all,
Look and see how wide the heaven expandeth,
Earth's enclosing bounds how small!

Shame it is, if your proud-swelling glory
May not fill this narrow room!
Why, then, strive so vainly, oh, ye proud ones!
To escape your mortal doom?

Though your name, to distant regions bruited,
O’er the earth be widely spread,
Though full many a lofty-sounding title
On your house its lustre shed,

Death at all this pomp and glory spurneth
When his hour draweth nigh,
Shrouds alike th' exalted and the humble,
Levels lowest and most high.

***

### PAGE 105
**[Original Page 83]**
**### The Vanity of Fortune's Gifts. 83 ###**

Where are now the bones of stanch Fabricius?
Brutus, Cato—where are they?
Lingering fame, with a few graven letters,
Doth their empty name display.

But to know the great dead is not given
From a gilded name alone;
Nay, ye all alike must lie forgotten,
'Tis not *you* that fame makes known.

Fondly do ye deem life's little hour
Lengthened by fame's mortal breath;
There but waits you—when this, too, is taken—
At the last a second death.

**6—2**

### PAGE 106
**[Original Page 84]**
**### 84 The Consolation of Philosophy ###**

**VIII**

But that thou mayst not think that I wage implacable warfare against Fortune, I own there is a time when the deceitful goddess serves men well—I mean when she reveals herself, uncovers her face, and confesses her true character. Perhaps thou dost not yet grasp my meaning. Strange is the thing I am trying to express, and for this cause I can scarce find words to make clear my thought. 

For truly I believe that Ill Fortune is of more use to men than Good Fortune. For Good Fortune, when she wears the guise of happiness, and most seems to caress, is always lying; Ill Fortune is always truthful, since, in changing, she shows her inconstancy. The one deceives, the other teaches; the one enchains the minds of those who enjoy her favour by the semblance of delusive good, the other delivers them by the knowledge of the frail nature of happiness. Accordingly, thou mayst see the one fickle,

***

### PAGE 107
**[Original Page 85]**
**### The Vanity of Fortune's Gifts. 85 ###**

shifting as the breeze, and ever self-deceived; the other sober-minded, alert, and wary, by reason of the very discipline of adversity. Finally, Good Fortune, by her allurements, draws men far from the true good; Ill Fortune ofttimes draws men back to true good with grappling-irons [^2]. Again, should it be esteemed a trifling boon, thinkest thou, that this cruel, this odious Fortune hath discovered to thee the hearts of thy faithful friends—that other hid from thee alike the faces of the true friends and of the false, but in departing she hath taken away *her* friends, and left thee *thine*? What price wouldst thou not have given for this service in the fulness of thy prosperity when thou seemedst to thyself fortunate? Cease, then, to seek the wealth thou hast lost, since in true friends thou hast found the most precious of all riches.

**SONG VIII**
**LOVE IS LORD OF ALL**

Why are Nature's changes bound
To a fixed and ordered round?
What to leagued peace hath bent
Every warring element?

[^2]: *Latin: "uncis" (hooks/curved irons).*

***

### PAGE 108
**[Original Page 86]**
**### 86 The Consolation of Philosophy ###**

Wherefore doth the rosy morn
Rise on Phœbus' car upborne?
Why should Phœbe rule the night,
Led by Hesper's guiding light?
What the power that doth restrain
In his place the restless main,
That within fixed bounds he keeps,
Nor o'er earth in deluge sweeps?
Love it is that holds the chains,
Love o’er sea and earth that reigns;
Love—whom else but sovereign Love?—
Love, high lord in heaven above!
Yet should he his care remit,
All that now so close is knit
In sweet love and holy peace,
Would no more from conflict cease,
But with strife's rude shock and jar
All the world's fair fabric mar.
Tribes and nations Love unites
By just treaty's sacred rites;
Wedlock's bonds he sanctifies
By affection's softest ties.
Love appointeth, as is due,
Faithful laws to comrades true—
Love, all-sovereign Love!—oh, then,
Ye are blest, ye sons of men,
If the love that rules the sky
In your hearts is throned on high!

***

### PAGE 109
**### BOOK III ###**
**### TRUE HAPPINESS AND FALSE ###**

**SUMMARY**

**CH. I.** Boethius beseeches Philosophy to continue. She promises to lead him to true happiness.—**CH. II.** Happiness is the one end which all created beings seek. They aim variously at (a) wealth, or (b) rank, or (c) sovereignty, or (d) glory, or (e) pleasure, because they think thereby to attain either (a) contentment, (b) reverence, (c) power, (d) renown, or (e) gladness of heart, in one or other of which they severally imagine happiness to consist.—**CH. III.** Philosophy proceeds to consider whether happiness can really be secured in any of these ways. (a) So far from bringing contentment, riches only add to men's wants.—**CH. IV.** (b) High position cannot of itself win respect. Titles command no reverence in distant and bar-

***

### PAGE 110
**[Original Page 88]**
**### 88 The Consolation of Philosophy ###**

barous lands. They even fall into contempt through lapse of time.—**CH. V.** (c) Sovereignty cannot even bestow safety. History tells of the downfall of kings and their ministers. Tyrants go in fear of their lives.—**CH. VI.** (d) Fame conferred on the unworthy is but disgrace. The splendour of noble birth is not a man's own, but his ancestors'.—**CH. VII.** (e) Pleasure begins in the restlessness of desire, and ends in repentance. Even the pure pleasures of home may turn to gall and bitterness.—**CH. VIII.** All fail, then, to give what they promise. There is, moreover, some accompanying evil involved in each of these aims. Beauty and bodily strength are likewise of little worth. In strength man is surpassed by the brutes; beauty is but outward show.—**CH. IX.** The source of men's error in following these phantoms of good is that *they break up and separate that which is in its nature one and indivisible.* Contentment, power, reverence, renown, and joy are essentially bound up one with the other, and, if they are to be attained at all, must be attained *together*. True happiness, if it can be found, will include them all. But it cannot be found among the perishable things hitherto considered.—**CH. X.** Such a happiness necessarily exists. Its seat is in God. Nay, God is very happiness, and in a manner, therefore, the happy man partakes also of the [divine nature] [^3].

[^3]: *Text concludes mid-sentence; restored from the Latin context: "divinitatis" (of divinity/divine nature).*

<meta>This page continues Lady Philosophy’s discourse on the insignificance of earthly glory. On the previous page, she argued that fame is geographically limited; here, she argues it is also temporally insignificant when measured against eternity.</meta>

[[original page: 80]]
-> ### 80 The Consolation of Philosophy ### <-

...if you scan the infinite spaces of eternity, what room is left for you to take pride in the lasting nature of your name? Truly, if you compare a single moment’s duration to ten thousand years, it has a certain relative length—however tiny—because both periods of time are <term>finite</term> <note>Meaning they have a measurable beginning and end.</note>. 

But that same number of years—even if it were multiplied many times over—cannot even be compared to the endlessness of eternity. For while finite periods of time can be compared to one another, a finite thing can never be compared to the infinite. Thus it happens that fame, no matter how many years it might span, seems not just brief when compared to the never-ending flow of eternity, but truly nothing at all. 

Yet you <note>Lady Philosophy is addressing Boethius and, by extension, all of humanity.</note> do not know how to act rightly unless it is to chase the <term>popular breeze</term> <note>original: "aura popularis" — a classical metaphor for the fickle and shifting nature of public favor.</note> and seek the empty applause of the crowd. You abandon the supreme value of a clear conscience and true virtue, seeking instead a reward from the cheap words of others. 

Let me tell you how cleverly a certain man mocked the shallow vanity of this kind of pride. There was once a man who attacked someone who had adopted the title of "philosopher" merely as a mask for his own pride and vanity, not for...

<keywords>eternity, fame, finite vs infinite, virtue, public opinion, aura popularis, conscience</keywords>

  When the final hour draws near;

  Making the lowest and the highest equal.

“So that you do not think I am waging a relentless war against Fortune <note>The Roman goddess Fortuna, often depicted with a wheel, representing the random and unpredictable nature of luck and fate.</note>, I admit there are times when that deceitful goddess actually treats people well—specifically, when she reveals herself, takes off her mask, and admits her true nature. Perhaps you do not yet grasp my meaning. What I am trying to say is strange, and I can hardly find the right words to explain it. For I truly believe that **Ill Fortune** is more beneficial to people than **Good Fortune**. 

When **Good Fortune** wears the mask of happiness and seems most affectionate, she is always lying; **Ill Fortune** is always honest, because by changing for the worse, she proves her <term>inconstancy</term> <note>The quality of being changeable or unreliable. Philosophy argues that since Fortune is naturally unstable, she is only being "true" to herself when she is being "bad."</note>. The one deceives, while the other teaches; the one traps the minds of those who enjoy her favor with a false appearance of good, while the other sets them free by showing them how fragile happiness really is. Therefore, you can see that the one is fickle,

[[vocabulary: Ill Fortune, Good Fortune, inconstancy]]

<meta>This page marks the beginning of Book III. -> ### BOOK III. ### <-

-> TRUE HAPPINESS AND FALSE. <-

-> SUMMARY. <-

CH. I. Boethius begs <note>original: "beseeches"</note> Philosophy <note>The personified figure of Wisdom who serves as Boethius's guide and physician throughout the text.</note> to continue. She promises to lead him to true happiness.
CH. II. Happiness <note>original: "beatitudo." In this context, it refers to the perfect state of well-being that leaves nothing further to be desired.</note> is the one end which all created beings seek. They aim variously at (a) wealth, or (b) rank, or (c) sovereignty <note><term>sovereignty</term>: the exercise of supreme power or royal authority.</note>, or (d) glory, or (e) pleasure, because they think thereby to attain either (a) contentment <note><term>contentment</term>: a state of self-sufficiency where one lacks nothing.</note>, (b) reverence, (c) power, (d) renown, or (e) gladness of heart <note><term>gladness of heart</term>: a translation of the Latin *laetitia*, meaning a deep and joyful satisfaction.</note>, in one or other of which they severally imagine happiness to consist.
CH. III. Philosophy proceeds to consider whether happiness can really be secured in any of these ways. (a) So far from bringing contentment, riches only add to men's wants.
CH. IV. (b) High position cannot of itself win respect. Titles command no reverence in distant and bar- <note>The text breaks off here; it likely intended to say "barbarous lands."</note>

[[vocabulary: sovereignty, contentment, gladness of heart]]

<summary>This page introduces the third book of the Consolation, outlining Lady Philosophy’s plan to define true happiness by first examining and debunking false paths such as wealth, power, and fame.</summary>
<keywords>Book III, True Happiness, Philosophy, sovereignty, contentment, Boethius, ethics, logic</keywords>

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>This page continues the summary of Book III from the previous page, completing the discussion on the failure of worldly honors and transitioning into the nature of true happiness as found in the Divine.</meta>

-> ### 88 The Consolation of Philosophy. ### <-

...barbarous lands. They even fall into contempt through the passage of time. — **Chapter V.** (c) **Sovereignty** <note>The exercise of supreme power or royal authority.</note> cannot even bestow safety. History tells of the downfall of kings and their ministers. Tyrants live in constant fear for their lives. — **Chapter VI.** (d) **Fame** conferred on the unworthy is nothing but disgrace. The splendor of noble birth is not a man's own achievement, but belongs to his ancestors. — **Chapter VII.** (e) **Pleasure** begins in the restlessness of desire, and ends in repentance. Even the pure pleasures of home life may turn to <term>gall and bitterness</term> <note>A phrase indicating intense grief or resentment.</note>. — **Chapter VIII.** All these pursuits fail, then, to give what they promise. There is, moreover, some accompanying evil involved in each of these aims. Beauty and bodily strength are likewise of little worth. In strength, man is surpassed by the <term>brutes</term> <note>A historical term for non-human animals.</note>; beauty is but an outward show. — **Chapter IX.** The source of men's error in following these <term>phantoms of good</term> <note>Deceptive appearances of happiness that lack true substance.</note> is that *they break up and separate that which is in its nature one and indivisible.* Contentment, power, reverence, renown, and joy are essentially bound up one with the other, and, if they are to be attained at all, must be attained *together*. True happiness, if it can be found, will include them all. But it cannot be found among the perishable things hitherto considered. — **Chapter X.** Such a happiness necessarily exists. Its seat is in God. Indeed, God is true happiness, and in a manner, therefore, the happy man partakes also of the...

[[vocabulary: <term>phantoms of good</term>, <term>indivisible</term>, <term>happy man</term>]]

<summary>This page concludes the critique of worldly goods like power, fame, and pleasure, arguing that they are mere fragments of a single, indivisible "Highest Good." Lady Philosophy identifies this unified happiness with God, suggesting that human happiness is a participation in the divine.</summary>
<keywords>sovereignty, fame, noble birth, pleasure, phantoms of good, indivisible nature, contentment, God, true happiness</keywords>

--- Page 111 ---
**SUMMARY**

[Translator's Note: This page summarizes the arguments of the later chapters of Book III.]

On the Divine Nature: All other ends are relative to this Good, for they are pursued only for the sake of the Good; the Good itself is the sole and ultimate end. Since the unique end is also Happiness (*beatitudo*), it is evident that this Good and Happiness are identical in substance. — CH. XI: Unity is another aspect of Goodness. All things subsist only so long as they preserve the unity of their being; when they lose this unity, they perish. The inclination of nature compels all things—plants and inanimate objects as well as animals—to strive for the continuation of life. Therefore, all things desire Unity, for Unity is essential to existence. But Unity and Goodness have been shown to be the same. Therefore, the Good is proven to be the end toward which the whole universe tends.* — CH. XII: Boethius acknowledges that he is merely recollecting truths he once knew. Philosophy proceeds to show that it is also by Goodness that the entire world is governed.† Boethius expresses remorse for his former foolishness. However, the paradox of evil is introduced, and he is once more perplexed.

***
\* This resolves the second of the points left in doubt at the end of Book I, Ch. VI.  
† This resolves the third. No distinct account is provided for the first, but an answer may be inferred from the general argument of Books II, III, and IV.
