--- Page 113 ---
**BOOK III**

**I**

[Decorative Initial **S**] **S**he had now finished her song, but the sweetness of the melody held me fixed, struck with wonder and eager to hear, my ears still strained to listen. After a short interval, I said: "O supreme solace of weary minds, how much you have restored me, both by the weight of your discourse and the delight of your singing! Indeed, I do not believe that hereafter I shall be unequal to the assaults of Fortune. Therefore, those remedies which you recently described as being somewhat too sharp for my strength—I no longer dread them; rather, being eager to hear, I vehemently demand them."

--- Page 114 ---
Then she replied: "I perceived that you were catching my words in silence and with attention, and I expected—or more truly, I myself brought about—this state of mind in you. The remedies that remain are of such a nature that they are bitter to the taste, but once they are absorbed, they turn to sweetness. But since you profess yourself desirous of hearing, with what ardor would you burn if you understood where it is I am attempting to lead you!"
"To where?" I asked.
"To true felicity," she said, "which your soul dreams of, but cannot yet behold clearly while your sight is occupied by images (*simulacra*)."
Then I said: "I beseech you, show me its true form without delay."
"I will do so gladly for your sake," she replied. "But first I shall attempt to describe in words that cause which is more familiar to you, so that once you have examined it, you may turn your eyes in the opposite direction and recognize the beauty of true happiness."

--- Page 115 ---
**SONG I**

**THE THORNS OF ERROR**

> He who wishes to sow a fertile field,
> And see the harvest grow,
> Must first clear the land of bushes,
> And cut the brambles and the fern.
>
> Honey tastes sweeter to the palate
> After the mouth has tasted bitterness;
> The stars shine more pleasingly to the eyes
> After the rainy South wind [Auster] ceases to blow.
>
> When Lucifer [the Morning Star] has dispelled the darkness,
> The bright day drives his rosy horses;
> So you, looking first upon false goods,
> Begin to withdraw your neck from the yoke;
> Then shall true delight enter your mind.

--- Page 116 ---
**II**

For a short time she kept her eyes fixed on the ground, as if withdrawn into the august sanctuary of her mind; then she began thus:
"All the care of mortal men, which is labored at in a multiplicity of pursuits, proceeds by different paths but strives to reach one and the same end: Happiness (*beatitudo*). This is the Good which, once attained, leaves nothing further to be desired. It is the supreme of all goods, containing within itself all that is good; for if anything were lacking to it, it could not be the supreme good, since there would remain something outside it that could be wished for. It is clear, then, that happiness is a state made perfect by the concentration of all goods. Toward this, as we have said, all men strive, though by various paths. For the desire for the...

--- Page 117 ---
...true Good is naturally implanted in the minds of men, but error leads them astray toward false goods. Some, believing it the highest good to lack nothing, labor to abound in riches; others, judging that the Good is that which is most worthy of respect, seek to be honored by their fellow citizens through the acquisition of dignities. Some place the highest good in supreme power; these either wish to rule themselves or seek to attach themselves to those in power. Those who judge glory to be the most excellent thing hasten to spread the fame of their name through the arts of war or peace. Many measure the fruit of the Good by joy and gladness; these believe it most happy to abandon themselves to pleasure. 
There are also those who exchange these ends and causes with one another; for instance, they desire riches for the sake of power and pleasure, or they seek power for the sake of money or fame. It is toward these things, then, and others of this kind, that the aim of human actions and desires is directed—as, for example, nobility and popularity, which seem to provide a certain renown; or a wife and children, which are sought for the sake of delight. As for friendship, its most sacred kind is counted as a matter of virtue, not of fortune; but other kinds are sought for the sake of power or interest. Furthermore, it is evident that bodily goods are related to these same ends; for strength and stature seem to provide power; beauty and swiftness provide celebrity; and health provides pleasure. Through all these, it is clearly only Happiness that is sought. For whatever a man seeks above all else, he judges to be the supreme good. But we have defined the supreme good as Happiness; therefore, the state which each man desires above all others is judged by him to be Happiness.
You have now before you a map, as it were, of human happiness: riches, honors, power, glory, pleasure.

--- Page 118 ---
<meta>This page continues Lady Philosophy’s explanation of how human beings mistakenly seek the "Supreme Good" (Happiness) through five partial goods: wealth, status, power, fame, and pleasure. On the previous page, she discussed how people pursue riches, dignities, and power. Here, she concludes the list with glory and bodily comforts, before summarizing the common error.</meta>

->96 *The Consolation of Philosophy.*<-

...their name. So it is on these ends, then, that the aim of human acts and wishes is centred, and on others like to these—for instance, noble birth and popularity, which seem to compass a certain renown; wife and children, which are sought for the sweetness of their possession; while as for friendship, the most sacred kind indeed is counted in the category of <term>virtue</term><note>Original Latin: *virtus*. Philosophy distinguishes between friends chosen for their moral character and those chosen for usefulness.</note>, not of <term>fortune</term><note>Original Latin: *fortuna*. This refers to external circumstances or luck, things that can be taken away by fate.</note>; but other kinds are entered upon for the sake of power or of enjoyment. And as for <term>bodily excellences</term><note>The physical advantages one is born with or develops.</note>, it is obvious that they are to be ranged with the above. For strength and stature surely manifest power; beauty and fleetness of foot bring celebrity; health brings pleasure. It is plain, then, that the only object sought for in all these ways is *happiness*. For that which each seeks in preference to all else, that is in his judgment the supreme good. And we have defined the supreme good to be happiness. Therefore, that state which each wishes in preference to all others is in his judgment happy.

‘Thou hast, then, set before thine eyes something like a scheme of human happiness—wealth, rank, power, glory, pleasure.

| Virtue | Fortune | Bodily Excellences |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Sacred friendship | Wealth & Rank | Strength & Stature |
| Moral character | Power & Glory | Beauty & Health |

<summary>Lady Philosophy categorizes various human desires—including family, friendship, and physical health—under the umbrella of a search for the "Supreme Good," concluding that all people essentially seek happiness even if their methods are flawed.</summary>
<keywords>Boethius, Lady Philosophy, definitions of happiness, virtue vs fortune, friendship, physical health, the Supreme Good</keywords>

--- Page 119 ---
Now Epicurus, considering these things, concluded that pleasure was the highest good, because all other pursuits seemed to bring some delight to the mind. But I return to the studies of men: the human mind seeks to regain its own proper Good, though its memory is clouded, but like a drunken man, it does not know by what path to return home. For do those seem to err who strive to lack nothing? Certainly not. There is nothing which can so effectively realize happiness as a state fortified with all goods, self-sufficient, and needing nothing from without. Do those err who judge that what is best is also most worthy of reverence? Not at all; for that cannot be mean or contemptible which almost all the efforts of mortals labor to obtain. Is power not to be reckoned among goods? Why, is that to be considered weak or strengthless which is clearly more efficacious than all else? Or is fame to be held of no account? It cannot be ignored...

[Note: "7" appears at the footer, a printer's signature mark.]

--- Page 120 ---
...that everything which is most excellent is also most famous. For what need is there to say that happiness is not plagued by anxiety or sadness, nor subject to grief or trouble, when even in small matters we seek that which is delightful to possess and enjoy? 
These, then, are the things that men wish to obtain; and for this reason they desire riches, dignities, kingdoms, glory, and pleasures: because they believe that through them they will obtain self-sufficiency, reverence, power, celebrity, and joy. Therefore, it is the Good which men seek through so many different pursuits; and in this the force of Nature is easily seen, for although opinions are diverse and conflicting, they nevertheless agree in choosing the Good as the end."

--- Page 121 ---
**[Book III, Metrum 2]**

-> *On True and False Happiness* <-

-> SONG II. <-
-> THE PLIANT REINS OF NATURE <-

How mighty Nature guides the reins of things,
By what providence she binds the world
In restricted laws, and how she provides
For all things with a binding decree—
This I shall now unfold upon my resonant strings.
    Though the Punic lion bears his golden chains
And receives food from a master’s hand,
Fearing the whip that tamed his spirit;
Yet if but once a drop of blood
Should wet those cruel jaws,
His dormant ferocity returns to its source.
With a roar he remembers his true self,
Tears his bonds asunder, and first
Vents his rage upon his master’s blood.
    The songbird too, shut in a hollow cage
Upon the high branch of a tree,
May be fed with honeyed drinks
And diverse dainties by a diligent hand;
But if, hopping through the narrow bars,
She should glimpse the cool shade of the woods,

-> 7—2 <-

--- Page 122 ---
**[Book III, Metrum 2, continued]**

She scatters her scattered food beneath her feet,
Seeking the forest in her silent grief;
With a sweet whisper she calls to the trees,
Singing only of the woods.
    The sapling, bent by a powerful hand,
Incline its crown toward the earth;
But if the force is once relaxed,
It looks again toward the sky.
Phœbus sinks into the western waves,
But by a hidden path his chariot turns,
To seek again his accustomed rising.
    Thus all things seek their own return,
Rejoicing in their proper course;
No order is fixed for things
Save that which joins the end to the beginning,
And makes the circle of its path stable.

--- Page 123 ---
**[Book III, Prosa 3]**

-> *On True and False Happiness* <-

-> III. <-

Ye also, O creatures of the earth, though you possess but a slender image of it, nevertheless dream of your origin; and even in a vision, however clouded, you look toward that true end of happiness. Thus, the natural instinct leads you toward the true good, but manifold error draws you away from it. Consider, therefore, whether men can attain the goal they have set for themselves by those means through which they think they shall reach happiness. For if money, or honors, or the rest, provide something of such a kind that it seems to lack no good thing, then we too would admit that some are made happy by the acquisition of these things. But if they are unable to perform what they promise, and lack many good things, is it not clear that the appearance of happiness in them is false?

--- Page 124 ---
First, therefore, I ask you yourself, who but a short time ago were wallowing in riches: in the midst of that abundance of wealth, was your mind never troubled by the anxiety of some injury?
    “I certainly cannot remember,” I said, “ever having a mind so free of care that I did not suffer from some pang of uneasiness.”
    “Was it not because something was either absent which you wished to be present, or present which you wished away?”
    “It was so,” I replied.
    “Then you desired the presence of the one and the absence of the other?”
    “I confess it.”
    “Now, is not a man in want of that which he lacks?”
    “He is,” I said.
    “And he who is in want of something, is he in all respects self-sufficient?”
    “By no means,” I said.
    “Thus, even in the fullness of your riches, you were supporting this insufficiency?”
    “I must have been.”

--- Page 125 ---
-> *On True and False Happiness* <-

“Wealth, therefore, cannot make a man free of want or self-sufficient, though this was the very thing it seemed to promise. Indeed, I think this also is most worthy of consideration: that there is nothing in the nature of money that prevents it from being taken away from those who possess it, even against their will.”
    “I grant that,” I said.
    “How could you not grant it, when every day we see the stronger wresting it from the weaker? For from what else do lawsuits arise, save that people seek to recover money taken from them by force or fraud?”
    “It is true,” I said.
    “Therefore,” she said, “everyone will need some external protection to keep his money safe.”
    “Who could deny it?”
    “Yet he would not need such protection unless he possessed the money he fears to lose.”
    “Certainly not.”
    “The matter has thus turned to its opposite: for the riches which were thought to make a man self-sufficient actually make him stand in need of others' defense. By what means, then, can want be driven away by money? Can the rich not hunger? Can they not thirst? Do the limbs of the wealthy not feel the winter cold?

--- Page 126 ---
‘But,’ you will say, ‘the rich have the wherewithal to satisfy their hunger and quench their thirst and cold.’ But in this way, want may be consoled by riches, but it cannot be entirely removed. For if this gaping, ever-craving want is filled by wealth, it follows that the want which requires satisfaction must remain. I need not mention that for nature, very little suffices; but for avarice, nothing is enough. Therefore, if wealth cannot take away want, but instead creates its own needs, why do you believe it bestows self-sufficiency?”

--- Page 127 ---
**[Book III, Metrum 3]**

-> SONG III. <-
-> THE INSATIABILITY OF AVARICE <-

Though the greedy man, flowing with a whirlpool of gold,
Should heap up riches that can never satisfy;
Though he should weigh down his neck with pearls
Sought from the Red Sea’s shore;
And though his fertile fields should be tilled
By a hundred yoked oxen;
Yet biting care shall never leave him while he lives,
Nor shall his fleeting riches follow him when he is dead.

--- Page 128 ---
**[Book III, Prosa 4]**

-> IV. <-

“But,” you say, “dignities [dignitates] make the man to whom they fall honorable and revered.” Do offices of state possess such power that they plant virtues in the minds of those who hold them and drive out vices? On the contrary, they are more often accustomed to make wickedness manifest than to chase it away. Hence we feel indignation that they so often fall to the most wicked of men. Thus Catullus called Nonius a ‘struma’ [a tumor or ulcer], even though he sat in the curule chair. Do you see what great infamy high position brings upon the wicked? For their unworthiness would be less apparent if they were not distinguished by honors. Could you yourself have been brought by so many perils to desire to hold the magistracy with Decoratus, when you saw in him the mind of a most worthless parasite and informer? For we cannot judge men worthy of respect because of their honors, when we judge them unworthy of the honors themselves.

--- Page 129 ---
-> *On True and False Happiness* <-

“But if you saw a man endowed with wisdom, could you think him unworthy of respect, or of the wisdom with which he was endowed?”
    “No, certainly not.”
    “For Virtue has her own dignity, which she immediately bestows upon those with whom she is joined. Since public honors cannot do this, it is clear that they do not possess the true beauty of dignity. In this matter, it is especially worth noting that if a man is more contemptible the more he is despised by many, then since high position cannot make the wicked respected, it only makes them more despised by making them more known. But they do not go unpunished; for the wicked repay the dignities they hold with their own infection, polluting them by their touch. Furthermore, so that you may realize that true respect cannot come from these shadow-dignities: if a man who had often been consul should travel to barbarian lands, would his honor win him the respect of the barbarians?

--- Page 130 ---
Yet if this were a natural power of dignities, they would never fail in their function in any land, just as fire never ceases to give out heat. But because they possess no such power of their own, but are joined to us by the false opinions of men, they vanish immediately when they come among those who do not consider them dignities. So it is with foreign nations. But do they endure forever even in the lands where they were born? The prefecture, which was once a great power, is now an empty name and a heavy burden on the senator’s fortune. He who once had charge of the people's grain was once thought a great man; now, what is more lowly than that office? For as we said before, that which has no beauty of its own receives or loses its luster according to the estimation of those who use it. If, therefore, dignities cannot make men respected, if they are sullied by the contagion of the wicked, if they lose their brilliance by the passage of time, and become cheap in the estimation of nations, what beauty do they possess in themselves, or what can they give to others?”

***

**Scholarly Notes:**
*   **Struma:** In Page 128, Boethius cites Catullus (Catull. 52). The Latin *struma* refers to a scrofulous swelling or tumor, which James translates as "ulcer-spot." 
*   **Curule Chair:** The *sella curulis*, the formal chair of high Roman magistrates.
*   **Decoratus:** A historical figure who served under Theodoric; Boethius viewed him as corrupt.
*   **Prefecture:** Referring to the *praefectura*, which by Boethius' time had lost much of its classical Roman administrative power.

-> METRUM IV. <-

-> THE DISGRACE OF HONOURS CONFERRED BY A TYRANT. <-

Though he should clothe himself in proud Tyrian purple
And deck himself with snowy pearls,
Yet Nero lived hated by all,
Abominable in his luxury and riot.
Nevertheless, he unworthily bestowed
The curule chairs upon venerable men.
Who, then, could deem those men blessed,
Whom a wicked man has seen fit to promote?

[[vocabulary: Nero, Tyrian purple, curule chairs]]

[[original page: 110]]
-> 110 *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-


-> PROSA V. <-

"Can kingdoms, then, or the intimacy of kings, make a man powerful? Why not, one might ask, if their happiness endures perpetually? But truly, antiquity is full of examples, as is the present age, of kings whose happiness has been turned into calamity. O, what a glorious power this is, which is found not even sufficient for its own preservation! 

But if this kingly authority is the author of beatitude, does it not follow that if that authority is in any way deficient, it diminishes happiness and introduces misery? Yet, however far human empires may extend, there must necessarily remain many nations over whom each king does not rule. Now, at whatever point the power that makes a man happy ceases, there impotence enters, which makes him wretched; by this reasoning, then, there must be a greater portion of wretchedness in the lot of kings. 

[[vocabulary: potentia (power), regna (kingdoms), beatitudo (happiness)]]

--- Page 133 ---
[[original page: 111]]
-> *True Happiness and False.* 111 <-

The tyrant who had experienced the dangers of his lot symbolized the fears of his reign by the terror of a sword hanging over a man’s head.* What sort of power is this, then, which cannot dispel the gnawings of anxiety nor avoid the stings of fear? 

They themselves would have wished to live in security, but they could not; and yet they boast of their power! Do you consider him powerful whom you see wishing for what he cannot achieve? Do you consider him powerful who surrounds his side with a bodyguard; who fears those whom he terrifies more than they fear him; and who, in order to appear powerful, is placed at the mercy of his own servants? 

Why should I speak of the friends of kings, since I show that kingship itself is so full of weakness? Oftentimes the power of the king ruins them while it stands, and oftentimes it drags them down in its fall. Nero compelled his friend and teacher, Seneca, to choose the manner of his death. Antoninus exposed Papinianus, long a power at court, to the swords of the soldiers. Yet both wished to renounce their power; Seneca even attempted to hand over his wealth to Nero and withdraw into retirement. But while they were being dragged to their ruin, their very greatness weighed them down. 

\* The sword of Damocles.

[[vocabulary: Damocles, Seneca, Papinianus, Antoninus (Caracalla)]]

--- Page 134 ---
[[original page: 112]]
-> 112 *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

What, then, is this power, which makes those who possess it fear; which is not safe when you wish to have it, and which you cannot lay aside without peril? Are those friends any protection whom not virtue but fortune has attached to you? Nay, for him whom good fortune has made a friend, ill fortune will make an enemy. And what plague is more effective for doing harm than a foe within the household?"

[[vocabulary: virtus (virtue), fortuna (fortune), hostis domesticus (household foe)]]

-> METRUM V. <-

-> SELF-MASTERY. <-

He who would be truly powerful
Must conquer his own passions;
Nor let him bow his neck, subdued,
To the foul yoke of lust.
For though the land of India
Should tremble at your command,
And though utmost Thule serve you—
Yet if you cannot banish dark Care,
Nor drive away wretched Lamentations,
Then you possess no true power.

-> PROSA VI. <-

"But as for Glory, how deceptive it often is, and how shameful! Wherefore the tragic poet rightly exclaims: 
> *'O Fame, Fame, among thousands of mortals / Thou hast done nothing else but inflate their lives!'* 

For many have acquired a great name through the false opinions of the common people—and what can be imagined more base than that? For those who are falsely praised must themselves blush at their own commendations. 

Even if such praise is earned by merit, what does it add to the conscience of the wise man? For he measures his own good not by the rumors of the crowd, but by the truth of his own inner conviction. If it seems a beautiful thing to have spread one's name abroad, it follows that it must be considered shameful not to have done so. But, as I argued a moment ago, 

[[vocabulary: Gloria (glory), Euripides (the tragic poet), conscientia (conscience)]]

--- Page 137 ---
[[original page: 115]]
-> *True Happiness and False.* 115 <-

since there must necessarily be many nations whom the fame of a single man can never reach, it follows that he whom you consider famous appears inglorious in the neighboring part of the earth. 

As for popular favor, I do not think it even worth mentioning; it is neither born of sound judgment nor does it ever remain stable. 

Now, who does not see how empty and foolish is the name of nobility? For if it is related to renown, it is the renown of another. For nobility seems to be a certain praise derived from the merits of one's ancestors. If it is the spreading of praise that creates renown, then those who are praised are necessarily the ones who become famous. Therefore, the fame of another does not make you illustrious if you have no light of your own. If there is any good in nobility, I think it is only this: that a necessity seems to be imposed upon the noble that they should not degenerate from the virtue of their ancestors."


&nbsp;
-> 8—2 <-

[[vocabulary: Nobilitas (nobility), praedicatio (praise), maiores (ancestors)]]

[[original page: 116]]
-> 116 *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-


-> METRUM VI. <-

-> TRUE NOBILITY. <-

All the race of men on earth
Arise from a similar origin.
One is the Father of all things,
One governs all by His providence.
He gave to the sun his rays,
He gave the moon her horns.
He gave men to the earth,
He gave stars to the sky.
He enclosed within a body
A soul fetched from the heights.
Therefore a noble seed
Is in every mortal breast.
Why do you boast of your lineage
Or your ancient names?
If you look to your beginning
And to God, your Author,
No man is base or degenerate,
Unless, nourished by vice,
He abandons his true origin.

[[vocabulary: Auctor (Author/God), origo (origin), vitia (vices)]]

--- Page 139 ---
[[original page: 117]]
-> *True Happiness and False.* 117 <-


-> PROSA VII. <-

"What shall I say of the pleasures of the body? The desire for them is full of anxiety, and the fulfillment is full of remorse. What great diseases, what intolerable pains they are accustomed to bring upon the bodies of those who enjoy them, as if they were a kind of fruit of their own wickedness! 

What sweetness their initial stirrings may have, I do not know. But whoever chooses to remember his own lusts will understand that the ends of pleasure are sorrowful. If these things can make one happy, there is no reason why beasts should not be called blessed, since all their effort is directed toward the fulfillment of bodily need. 

The joy of a wife and children should be a most honorable thing; but it has been said—only too truly—that someone found in his own sons his tormentors. How galling such a condition is, I must remind you, for you have never experienced it, nor are you now in such distress. In this matter, I agree with my favorite Euripides, who said that he who is without children is 'fortunate in his misfortune.'"*

[[vocabulary: Voluptas (pleasure), paenitudo (remorse), Euripides]]

--- Page 140 ---
[[original page: 118]]
-> 118 *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

[The text here concludes the reference to Euripides' *Andromache*, noting the irony of his sentiment regarding the childless.]


-> METRUM VII. <-

-> PLEASURE’S STING. <-

Every pleasure has this nature:
It goads those who enjoy it;
And like the winged bee
Who has poured out her honey,
She takes flight and leaves
A long-lasting sting in the heart.

\* Note: Boethius here references the *Andromache* of Euripides (ll. 418-420), though he applies the sentiment to his own philosophical argument regarding the burdens of family life.

[[vocabulary: Euripides, Andromache, voluptas (pleasure), stimulus (sting)]]

--- Page 141 ---
[[original page: 119]]

-> *On the False and the True.* 119 <-

-> VIII. <-

There is, therefore, no doubt that these paths to [false] blessedness are but certain deviations, and they cannot lead anyone to the destination they promise. I shall show you, quite briefly, with what great evils they are entangled. For what? If you attempt to amass money, you must wrest it from him who possesses it. If you wish to shine with honors, you must supplicate him who bestows them; and you, who desire to surpass others in dignity, must cheapen yourself by the foulness of begging. Do you desire power? You will be subjected to the dangers of your subjects' treachery. Do you seek glory? But, drawn through the ruggedness of every hardship, you cease to be secure. Do you choose to lead a life of pleasure? But who would not despise and cast away one who is a slave to the most vile and fragile thing, namely the body? Indeed, upon how—

[[vocabulary: Boethius, beatitudo (happiness), pecunia (money), dignitas (official dignity), potentia (power), gloria (glory), voluptas (pleasure)]]

--- Page 142 ---
[[original page: 120]]

-> 120 *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

—how frail and perishable a possession do those rely who seek bodily excellences! Can you surpass the elephant in bulk, or the bull in strength? Can you precede the tiger in speed? Look upon the space, the stability, and the swiftness of the heavens, and cease at last to admire things of little worth. Indeed, the heavens are not more to be admired for these qualities than for the reason [ratio] by which they are governed. As for the luster of beauty, how fleeting is its swiftness! It is more transitory than the passing of spring flowers. For if, as Aristotle says, men possessed the eyes of Lynceus, so that their vision could penetrate through obstacles, would not that body of Alcibiades, though outwardly most fair, appear most foul once the entrails were laid bare? Therefore, it is not your own nature that makes you seem beautiful, but the weakness of the eyes of those who behold you. Prize the excellences of the body as much as you will, provided you know that whatever you admire can be dissolved by the slight heat of a 

[[vocabulary: bona corporis (bodily excellences), Aristoteles, Lynceus, Alcibiades, formositas (beauty)]]

--- Page 143 ---
[[original page: 121]]

-> *On the False and the True.* 121 <-

three-day fever. From all these considerations we may summarize the whole: that these things, which can neither provide the goods they promise nor are perfected by the conjunction of all goods, do not lead as paths to blessedness, nor can they, by themselves, make one blessed.

-> SONG VIII. <-
-> ON HUMAN ERROR. <-

Alas! What ignorance leads
Wretched mortals by a wandering path!
For you do not seek gold 
Upon the leafy trees,
Nor do you gather gems from the vine;
You do not hide your nets
Upon the high mountains
That you might enrich your tables with fish;
Nor, if you wish to hunt 
The fleeing goat,
Do you traverse the Tyrrhenian waves in vain.

[[vocabulary: Humana Ignorantia (Human Folly), Ignorantia, Veritas (Truth)]]

--- Page 144 ---
[[original page: 122]]

-> 122 *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

Men know the hidden retreats 
Of the sea, and which wave 
Is more fertile with snowy pearls;
They know which waters abound
In the Tyrian purple shell,
Or the various fish and the prickly urchin.
But as for where the Good 
Which they desire lies hidden,
They are content to be blind;
And they plunge into the earth
To seek that which has its origin
Beyond the starry pole.
What curse is worthy of such dull minds?
Let them pursue their riches
And their honors with great labor,
So that when they have attained
The false goods with much toil,
They may finally recognize the True.

[[vocabulary: Tyrium muricem (Tyrian shell), Summum Bonum (good), polus (firmament), opes (wealth), honores (renown)]]

--- Page 145 ---
[[original page: 123]]

-> *On the False and the True.* 123 <-

-> IX. <-

“This much,” she said, “suffices for the description of the form of false happiness. If you now perceive it clearly, the order of the task remains to show you the true.”
“I see clearly,” I said, “that there is no sufficiency in riches, no power in kingdoms, no reverence in dignities, no renown in glory, and no joy in pleasures.”
“And have you also understood the causes of why this is so?”
“I seem to glimpse them as if through a narrow crack, but I should prefer to learn them more plainly from you.”
“The reason is ready at hand. For that which is simple and indivisible by nature, human error divides; and it translates that which is true and perfect into that which is false and imperfect. Or do you think that that which lacks nothing stands in need of power?”
“By no means.”

[[vocabulary: falsa felicitas (false happiness), vera felicitas (true happiness), divitiae (wealth), potentia (power), regna (sovereignty), dignitas (dignities), gloria (glory), voluptates (pleasures)]]

--- Page 146 ---
[[original page: 124]]

-> 124 *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

“Rightly so; for if there is any weakness of strength in a thing, it necessarily requires external assistance in that regard.”
“That is so,” I said.
“Therefore, the nature of self-sufficiency and of power is one and the same.”
“So it appears.”
“But do you think that a thing of this nature is to be despised, or rather that it is most worthy of all veneration?”
“There can be no doubt that it is most worthy of veneration.”
“Let us, then, add reverence to self-sufficiency and power, so that we judge these three to be one.”
“We must add it, if we wish to admit the truth.”
“What then? Do you think this whole is something obscure and ignoble, or rather most famous with every renown? Consider this: can that which is agreed to lack nothing, to be most powerful, and most worthy of honor, lack renown—since it cannot provide this for itself and therefore appears more diminished in any respect?”

[[vocabulary: sufficientia (independence/self-sufficiency), potentia (power), reverentia (reverence), claritudo (renown)]]

--- Page 147 ---
[[original page: 125]]

-> *On the False and the True.* 125 <-

“I cannot deny,” I said, “that it is most famous.”
“Consequently, we must confess that renown is in no way different from the previous three.”
“It follows,” I said.
“Therefore, that which requires nothing from without, which can do all things by its own strength, and which is illustrious and venerable—is it not also evident that it is most joyful?”
“I cannot even conceive,” I replied, “how any sorrow could creep into such a thing; wherefore, if the previous points remain settled, I must confess it is full of joy.”
“And by the same logic, this too is necessary: that the names of self-sufficiency, power, renown, reverence, and pleasure are different, but in substance they differ in no way.”
“It is necessary,” I said.
“This, then, which is one and simple by nature, human perversity divides; and while it attempts to seize a part of that which

[[vocabulary: laetitia (joy), sufficientia (independence), potentia (power), claritudo (renown), reverentia (reverence), iucunditas (sweetness of delight)]]

--- Page 148 ---
[[original page: 126]]

-> 126 *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

has no parts, it attains neither the portion (which does not exist) nor the whole (which it does not seek).”
“How so?” I asked.
“He who seeks riches by fleeing poverty is not anxious about power; he prefers to be mean and obscure, and even withdraws from many natural pleasures lest he lose the money he has gathered. But in this way, he does not even achieve self-sufficiency, whom power deserts, whom sorrow stings, whom meanness casts down, and whom obscurity hides. He, however, who only desires power, squanders his wealth, despises pleasures, and counts honor and glory as nothing if they lack power. But you see how many things are lacking to him. For it happens that at times he lacks necessaries, is bitten by anxieties, and when he cannot shake these off, he loses even the power he sought. One may reason similarly concerning honors, glory, and pleasures. For since each of these is

[[vocabulary: opes (riches), potentia (power), honores (rank), gloria (glory), voluptas (pleasure), sufficientia (independence)]]

--- Page 149 ---
[[original page: 127]]

-> *On the False and the True.* 127 <-

the same as the others, whoever seeks any one of them without the rest cannot even grasp the one he desires.”
“What then?” I asked.
“If anyone should wish to seek them all at once, he would indeed seek the sum of blessedness; but will he find it in these things which we have already shown cannot provide what they promise?”
“By no means,” I replied.
“Therefore, blessedness is not to be sought in these things which are believed to provide each of the desired goods individually.”
“I confess it,” I said, “and nothing could be said more truly.”
“You have, then, the form of false happiness and its causes. Now turn the vision of your mind to the opposite side; for there you will immediately see the true happiness I promised.”
“Even to a blind man,” I said, “it is manifest, and you showed it just now when you attempted to disclose the causes of the false. For unless I am deceived, that is the true and perfect blessedness which makes

[[vocabulary: falsa felicitas (false happiness), vera beatitudo (true happiness)]]

--- Page 150 ---
[[original page: 128]]

-> 128 *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

one self-sufficient, powerful, revered, famous, and joyful. And—that you may know I have looked deeply—that which can truly provide one of these, since they are all the same, I recognize to be full and perfect blessedness.”
“O my pupil, I judge you happy in this opinion, provided you add one thing.”
“What is that?” I asked.
“Do you think that in these mortal and perishable things there is anything that can provide such a state?”
“I do not think so, and you have demonstrated it so clearly that no further proof is needed.”
“Clearly, then, these things provide mortals either with the shadows of the true good or certain imperfect goods; but the true and perfect good they cannot bestow.”
“I agree,” I said.
“Since, then, you have recognized what is true happiness and what are its false imitations, it now remains for you to learn from what source you may seek this true happiness.”

[[vocabulary: sufficientem (independence), potentem (power), reverendum (reverence), celebrem (renown), laetum (joy), verum bonum (true good), imperfecta bona (imperfect good)]]

‘Yes,’ [I said]; ‘to this I have long been looking forward with eager desire.’

‘But since,’ [she said], ‘as our Plato maintains in the *Timaeus* [[abbrev: Timæus]], even in the most trivial matters we ought to implore the Divine protection, what thinkest thou should we now do in order to deserve to find the seat of that highest good?’

‘We must invoke the Father of all things,’ said I; ‘for without this no enterprise sets out from a right beginning.’

‘Thou sayest well,’ said she; and forthwith she lifted up her voice and sang:

   Through ages eternal; Maker of earth and sky,

Thyself unmoved, yet giving motion to all;

To fashion this frame of floating matter,

   Within Thy being, and free from all envy.
Thou dost lead all things to that supernal pattern;
Being Thyself most beautiful, Thou bearest
   A beautiful world in Thy mind, and in like image

**-> *True Happiness and False.* 133 <-**

**-> X. <-**

‘Since now thou hast seen what is the form of the imperfect good, and what the form of the perfect also, methinks I should next show in what manner this perfection of felicity is built up. And here I conceive it proper to inquire, first, whether any excellence, such as thou hast lately defined, can exist in the nature of things, lest we be deceived by an empty fiction of thought to which no true reality answers. But it cannot be denied that such does exist, and is, as it were, the source of all things good. For everything which is called imperfect is spoken of as imperfect by reason of the privation of some perfection; so it comes to pass that, whenever imperfection is found in any particular, there must necessarily be a perfection in respect of that particular also. For were there no such perfection, it is utterly inconceivable how that so-called *imperfection* should

[[vocabulary: imperfect good (imperfecti boni), perfect good (perfecti boni), felicity (beatitudinis), privation (deminutione)]]

***

###

--- Page 156 ---
**[[Original page: 134]]**

**-> 134 *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-**

come into existence. Nature does not make a beginning with things mutilated and imperfect; she starts with what is whole and perfect, and falls away later to these feeble and inferior productions. So if there is, as we showed before, a happiness of a frail and imperfect kind, it cannot be doubted but there is also a happiness substantial and perfect.’

‘Most true is thy conclusion, and most sure,’ said I.

‘Next to consider where the dwelling-place of this happiness may be. The common belief of all mankind agrees that God, the supreme of all things, is good. For since nothing can be imagined better than God, how can we doubt Him to be good than whom there is nothing better? Now, reason shows God to be good in such wise as to prove that in Him is perfect good. For were it not so, He would not be supreme of all things; for there would be something else more excellent, possessed of perfect good, which would seem to have the advantage in priority and dignity, since it has clearly appeared that all perfect things are prior

[[vocabulary: Nature (rerum natura), substantial happiness (solida atque perfecta), supreme good (Deum rerum omnium principem)]]

***

###

--- Page 157 ---
**[[Original page: 135]]**

**-> *True Happiness and False.* 135 <-**

to those less complete. Wherefore, lest we fall into an infinite regression, we must acknowledge the supreme God to be full of supreme and perfect good. But we have determined that true happiness is the perfect good; therefore true happiness must dwell in the supreme Deity.’

‘I accept thy reasonings,’ said I; ‘they cannot in any wise be disputed.’

‘But, come, see how strictly and incontrovertibly thou mayst prove this our assertion that the supreme Godhead hath fullest possession of the highest good.’

‘In what way, pray?’ said I.

‘Do not rashly suppose that He who is the Father of all things hath received that highest good of which He is said to be possessed either from some external source, or hath it as a natural endowment in such sort that thou mightest consider the essence of the happiness possessed, and of the God who possesses it, distinct and different. For if thou deemest it received from without, thou mayst esteem that which gives more excellent than that which has received. But Him we most worthily acknowledge to be the most supremely

[[vocabulary: infinite regression (in infinitum ratio), supreme Deity (summo deo), highest good (summum bonum)]]

***

###

--- Page 158 ---
**[[Original page: 136]]**

**-> 136 *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-**

excellent of all things. If, however, it is in Him by nature, yet is logically distinct, the thought is inconceivable, since we are speaking of God, who is supreme of all things. Who was there to join these distinct essences? Finally, when one thing is different from another, the things so conceived as distinct cannot be identical. Therefore that which of its own nature is distinct from the highest good is not itself the highest good—an impious thought of Him than whom, 'tis plain, nothing can be more excellent. For universally nothing can be better in nature than the source from which it has come; therefore on most true grounds of reason would I conclude that which is the source of all things to be in its own essence the highest good.’

‘And most justly,’ said I.

‘But the highest good has been admitted to be happiness.’

‘Yes.’

‘Then,’ said she, ‘it is necessary to acknowledge that God is very happiness.’

‘Yes,’ said I; ‘I cannot gainsay my former admissions, and I see clearly that this is a necessary inference therefrom.’

[[vocabulary: distinct essences (diversum), highest good (summum bonum), happiness (beatitudinem), God (Deum)]]

***

###

--- Page 159 ---
**[[Original page: 137]]**

**-> *True Happiness and False.* 137 <-**

‘Reflect, also,’ said she, ‘whether the same conclusion is not further confirmed by considering that there cannot be two supreme goods distinct one from the other. For the goods which are different clearly cannot be severally each what the other is: wherefore neither of the two can be perfect, since to either the other is wanting; but since it is not perfect, it cannot manifestly be the supreme good. By no means, then, can goods which are supreme be different one from the other. But we have concluded that both happiness and God are the supreme good; wherefore that which is highest Divinity must also itself necessarily be supreme happiness.’

‘No conclusion,’ said I, ‘could be truer to fact, nor more soundly reasoned out, nor more worthy of God.’

‘Then, further,’ said she, ‘just as geometricians are wont to draw inferences from their demonstrations to which they give the name "deductions" [[abbrev: *porismata*]], so will I add here a sort of corollary. For since men become happy by the acquisition of happiness, while happiness is very Godship, it is manifest that they become happy by the

[[vocabulary: supreme good (summa bona), Divinity (divinitatem), deductions (porismata), Godship (divinitatem)]]

***

###

--- Page 160 ---
**[[Original page: 138]]**

**-> 138 *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-**

acquisition of Godship. But as by the acquisition of justice men become just, and wise by the acquisition of wisdom, so by parity of reasoning by acquiring Godship they must of necessity become gods. So every man who is happy is a god; and though in nature God is One only, yet there is nothing to hinder that very many should be gods by participation in that nature.’

‘A fair conclusion, and a precious,’ said I, ‘deduction or corollary, by whichever name thou wilt call it.’

‘And yet,’ said she, ‘not one whit fairer than this which reason persuades us to add.’

‘Why, what?’ said I.

‘Why, seeing happiness has many particulars included under it, should all these be regarded as forming one body of happiness, as it were, made up of various parts, or is there some one of them which forms the full essence of happiness, while all the rest are relative to this?’

‘I would thou wouldst unfold the whole matter to me at large.’

‘We judge happiness to be good, do we not?’

[[vocabulary: Godship (divinitatis), justice (iustitiae), wisdom (sapientiae), participation in nature (participatione)]]

--- Page 161 ---
[[original page: 139]]
-> *True Happiness and False.* <-

‘Indeed, it is the supreme good.’
‘And this [the good] is added to all the rest; for this same happiness is judged to be the most sufficient independence, the most potent power, the most venerable reverence, the most famous renown, and the most pleasant delight.’
‘What then?’
‘Are all these things—independence, power, and the remaining three—to be considered members [parts] of happiness, as it were, or are they all referred to the good as to their summit and head?’
‘I understand the question thou proposest, but I desire to hear how thou wouldst define the matter.’
‘Hear, then, the distinction of this truth. If all these were members of one happiness, they would differ one from another by their individual properties. For this is the nature of parts—that different parts constitute one body. Yet all these things have been shown to be the same. Therefore, they are not members; otherwise, happiness would seem to be joined together from a single member, which is impossible.’
‘This is beyond doubt,’ I said; ‘but I await that which remains.’
‘It is manifest, then, that the others

[[vocabulary: *summum bonum* (supreme good), *sufficientia* (independence/sufficiency), *potestas* (power), *reverentia* (reverence), *claritas* (renown), *laetitia* (pleasure/delight), *beatitudo* (happiness)]]

--- Page 162 ---
[[original page: 140]]
-> *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

are referred to the good. For independence is sought because it is judged to be a good, and power likewise is sought because it is believed to be a good. We may conclude the same regarding reverence, renown, and pleasure. Thus, the good is the sum and the cause of all things to be desired. For that which contains neither the reality nor the appearance of good can in no way be sought. Conversely, even those things which are not good by nature are sought as if they were truly good, provided they appear to be so. Hence it is that goodness is rightly believed to be the hinge, the sum, and the cause of all things that are sought. Now, that for the sake of which something is sought appears to be the primary object of the wish. For instance, if one wishes to ride for the sake of health, he does not desire the motion of riding so much as he desires the effect of health. Since, therefore, all things are desired for the sake of the good, it is the good itself, rather than the things themselves, that is desired by all. But we have agreed that happiness is that for the sake of which all else is desired; wherefore, it is likewise evident

[[vocabulary: *bonum* (good), *summa* (sum/total), *cardo* (hinge/pivot), *causa* (cause)]]

--- Page 163 ---
[[original page: 141]]
-> *True Happiness and False.* <-

that it is happiness alone which is sought. From which it is clearly proven that the substance of the absolute good and of happiness is one and the same.’
‘I see no reason why anyone should disagree.’
‘But we have also demonstrated that God and true happiness are one and the same thing.’
‘That is so,’ I replied.
‘We may then safely conclude that the essence of God is situated in the absolute good itself, and nowhere else.’

-> XI. <-

‘I assent,’ I said, ‘for all thy arguments are bound together by the strongest reason.’
Then she said: ‘At what price wouldst thou value it, if thou shouldst come to know the good itself?’
‘At an infinite price,’ I replied, ‘if it should happen that I also knew God, who is the good.’
‘This then I shall make clear to thee by the truest reasoning, provided that those things we have recently concluded remain settled.’
‘They shall remain so.’
‘Have we not shown,’ she asked, ‘that those things which are desired by many are not true or perfect goods for the reason that they differ from one another; and since one is absent from the other, they cannot confer full and absolute good? But they become the true good when they are gathered into a single form

[[vocabulary: *boni* (the good), *forma* (form/essence), *effigies* (agency/image)]]

--- Page 166 ---
[[original page: 144]]
-> *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

and operation, so that independence is the same as power, reverence, renown, and pleasure? For unless they are all one and the same, they have nothing in them worth choosing.’
‘This has been proven,’ I said, ‘and cannot be doubted in any way.’
‘Things, then, that are not goods when they are diverse, but become goods when they begin to be one—do they not become goods by the attainment of unity?’
‘So it seems,’ I said.
‘But dost thou admit that everything that is good is good by participation in the good?’
‘I do.’
‘Then thou must admit by similar reasoning that unity and the good are the same. For the substance of those things is the same, whose effect does not naturally differ.’
‘I cannot deny it.’
‘Dost thou know, then,’ she said, ‘that everything which exists remains and subsists so long as it is one, but perishes and dissolves as soon as it ceases to be one?’

[[vocabulary: *unitatis* (unity), *participatione* (participation), *substantia* (substance), *subsistere* (to subsist)]]

--- Page 167 ---
[[original page: 145]]
-> *True Happiness and False.* <-

‘In what way?’
‘Just as in animals,’ she said, ‘when the soul and the body are joined and remain in one, it is called a living creature. But when this unity is dissolved by the separation of the two, the creature perishes and is no longer a living being. The body itself, while it remains in one form by the union of its members, retains the human image; but if the parts are distracted and separated and the unity of the body is broken, it ceases to be what it was. If we examine all other things, it will be clearly seen that each thing subsists while it is one, but when it ceases to be one, it perishes.’
‘When I consider more things, I see it is no otherwise.’
‘Is there anything, then,’ she asked, ‘which, in acting according to nature, abandons the intention of remaining and desires to come to death and corruption?’
‘If I consider the living creatures which have some power of choice, I find none which, without external compulsion, cast away the intention of living and hasten
-> 10 <-

[[vocabulary: *animalibus* (living creatures), *anima* (soul), *corpus* (body), *unitate* (unity)]]

--- Page 168 ---
[[original page: 146]]
-> *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

spontaneously toward destruction. For every animal labors to preserve its health and shuns death and destruction. But regarding herbs and trees and inanimate things, I am utterly uncertain what I should think.’
‘There is no reason to doubt this,’ she said, ‘for thou mayst observe the herbs and trees, how they grow in places suitable for them, where, so far as their nature allows, they cannot quickly dry up and perish. For some grow in fields, others on mountains; some spring up in marshes, others cling to rocks; some find fertile soil in parched sands; and if thou shouldst try to transplant them elsewhere, they wither. Nature gives to each what is fitting and strives that they do not perish so long as they are able to remain. Why do they all take nourishment from their roots as if from a mouth buried in the earth, and spread the strength of their bark over the pith? Why is the softest part, like the pith, always hidden in the innermost parts, while the outside is covered by the strength of wood, and the outermost

[[vocabulary: *herbis* (herbs/plants), *arboribus* (trees), *medulla* (pith/marrow), *cortex* (bark)]]

--- Page 169 ---
[[original page: 147]]
-> *True Happiness and False.* <-

layer of bark is set against the inclemency of the heavens, as a defender of the enduring strength? And how great is the diligence of nature that all things are propagated by the multiplicity of seed! All these things, who does not know that they are like engines [contrivances] intended not only for remaining for a time, but for the perpetual duration of their kind? Even those things believed to be inanimate—do they not seek what is their own by similar reasoning? For why is the flame carried upward by its lightness, while the earth is borne downward by its weight, unless these motions and places are fitting for each thing? Furthermore, that which is agreeable to the nature of each thing preserves it, just as that which is inimical destroys it. Hard things, like stones, adhere most tenaciously in their parts and resist being easily dissolved. Fluid things, like air and water, yield easily to those things that divide them, but quickly flow back into those parts from which they were severed; fire, however, escapes every division. We are not now discussing the voluntary motions of a conscious soul, but the

[[vocabulary: *propaganda* (propagation), *inanimata* (inanimate), *naturali intentione* (natural drift/intention)]]

--- Page 170 ---
[[original page: 148]]
-> *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

natural instinct. For we digest the food we have taken without thinking, and we breathe in sleep without knowing it. For even in animals, the love of life does not come from the will of the soul, but from the principles of nature. For often the will, driven by compelling causes, embraces a death which nature abhors; and conversely, the will sometimes restrains the work of generation—by which alone the duration of mortal things subsists—though nature always desires it. Thus, this love of self proceeds not from the animal’s desire, but from a natural instinct. For Providence has given to His creatures this greatest cause for remaining: the natural desire to exist so long as they can. Therefore, thou canst in no way doubt that all things naturally desire the persistence of their existence and naturally shun destruction.’
‘I confess,’ I said, ‘that those things which lately seemed uncertain to me, I now perceive by an indubitable light.’
‘Now, that which seeks to be and to remain, desires to be one; for if this unity is taken away, then existence itself will not remain.’

[[vocabulary: *providentia* (Providence), *manendi* (remaining/persisting), *unitatem* (oneness/unity)]]

--- Page 171 ---
**[Book III, Prose 11, 38–42]**

-> *True Happiness and False.* [original page: 149] <-

“It is true,” I said.
“All things, therefore, desire to be one.”
“I agree.”
“But we have demonstrated that the ‘one’ is the very same as the ‘good.’”
“We have indeed.”
“All things, therefore, seek the good; and you may define the good itself in this way: it is that which is desired by all.”
“Nothing could be more truly thought out,” I replied. “For either all things are referred to no single end, and wander without a ruler, or, if there is something toward which all things hasten together, that must be the highest of all goods.”
Then she said: “I rejoice greatly, O pupil, for you have fixed the very eye of your mind upon the mark of truth. But in this, that which you said you were ignorant of a moment ago has been made manifest.”
“What is that?” I asked.
“The end of all things,” she said. “For certainly it is that which is desired by all; and since we have concluded that this is the good, we must necessarily confess that the end of all things is the Good.”

[[vocabulary: *summum bonum* (highest good), *finis* (end/aim), *universitas* (universe)]]

--- Page 172 ---
**[Book III, Meter 11]**

[[original page: 150]] -> *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

-> SONG XI. <-
-> [[term: Reminiscence / Anamnesis]]* <-

Whoever seeks the truth with a profound mind
And wishes not to be deceived by wandering paths,
Must turn back upon himself the light of an inner vision
And bend the longings of his heart into a circle,
Teaching his soul that whatever it seeks without,
It possesses hidden among its own treasures.
Then that which was clouded by black error
Shall shine more clearly than Phoebus himself.
For the weight of the body has not utterly expelled
All light from the mind; the seed of truth clings within,
Stirred into life by the fanning of learning.
For why, when questioned, do you give right answers
By your own instinct, unless the spark of reason
Lived deep within the heart?
If the Muse of **Plato** sings the truth,
What each man learns, he but remembers, having forgotten.

\* *Note: This refers to the Platonic doctrine of anamnesis (recollection), as detailed in the Meno and Phaedo.*

[[vocabulary: *reminiscentia*, **Plato**, **Meno**, **Phaedo**, *lux interna*]]

--- Page 173 ---
**[Book III, Prose 12, 1–10]**

-> *True Happiness and False.* [[original page: 151]] <-

[[image: Library Stamp - BRENTFORD PUBLIC LIBRARY]]

-> XII. <-

Then I said: “I agree strongly with **Plato**. For this is the second time I have been reminded of these things: first, I lost them through the contagion of the body’s connection; then later, when I was overwhelmed by the weight of grief.”
She replied: “If you look back upon the things already conceded, you will soon also remember that which you recently said you did not know.”
“What is that?” I asked.
“By what reins,” she said, “the world is governed.”
“I remember that I confessed my ignorance,” I said, “and although I already foresee what you are pointing toward, I desire to hear it from you more plainly.”
“A little while ago,” she said, “you thought it beyond doubt that this world is ruled by God.”
“I do not think it doubtful now,” I said, “nor shall I ever.”

[[vocabulary: **Plato**, *mundi gubernacula* (world’s government), **Deus** (God)]]

--- Page 174 ---
**[Book III, Prose 12, 11–20]**

[[original page: 152]] -> *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

“I will briefly set forth the reasons by which I am brought to this certainty. This world, composed of such diverse and contrary parts, could never have coalesced into a single form unless there were One who joined together such disparate things. Once joined, the very diversity of their natures, being in discord with one another, would have torn them asunder unless One held together what He had woven. The certain order of nature would not proceed, nor would its movements exhibit such determined motions in places, times, efficacy, and qualities, unless there were One who, remaining stable Himself, disposed these varieties of change. This power, whatever it is, by which created things subsist and move, I call by the name used by all—**God**.”
Then she said: “Since you feel thus, I think there is little labor left for me, so that, having attained happiness, you may return safe to your own country. But let us look at the things we have set before us.”

[[vocabulary: *naturae ordo* (order of nature), **Deus**, *conditor* (creator), *beatitudo* (happiness)]]

--- Page 175 ---
**[Book III, Prose 12, 21–29]**

-> *True Happiness and False.* [[original page: 153]] <-

“Have we not placed self-sufficiency (*sufficientia*) among the attributes of happiness, and agreed that **God** is Happiness itself?”
“We have.”
“Therefore,” she said, “He will need no external aid to rule the world; otherwise, if He needed anything, He would not have full self-sufficiency.”
“That is necessary,” I said.
“Therefore, He disposes all things by Himself alone.”
“It cannot be denied.”
“And it has been proven that **God** is the Good itself.”
“I remember,” I said.
“Therefore, He disposes all things through the Good, since He rules all things by Himself, whom we have agreed is the Good; and He is like a rudder and a helm (*clavis et gubernaculum*), by which the machine of the world is kept stable and intact.”
“I agree strongly,” I said, “and I anticipated that you were going to say this, though by a slight suspicion.”

[[vocabulary: *sufficientia*, *beata summa* (absolute happiness), *bonum* (absolute good), *clavis et gubernaculum* (rudder and helm)]]

--- Page 176 ---
**[Book III, Prose 12, 30–38]**

[[original page: 154]] -> *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

“I believe it,” she said, “for your eyes are now more vigilant toward discerning the truth. But what I shall say is no less clear.”
“What is it?” I asked.
“Since **God** is rightly believed to govern all things by the helm of goodness,” she said, “and all these things, as I have taught, hasten toward the Good by a natural inclination, can it be doubted that they are governed voluntarily, and that they turn themselves to the will of the Disposer as if compliant and attuned to the ruler?”
“It must be so,” I said, “for a government would not seem happy if it were a yoke upon the reluctant, rather than the salvation of those who obey.”
“There is nothing, then, which, in following nature, attempts to resist **God**.”
“Nothing,” I said.
“But if anything should attempt to resist, would it prevail at last against Him whom we have granted to be the most powerful through happiness?”
“It would be utterly powerless,” I replied.
“There is nothing, therefore, which either wants to or is able to oppose this supreme Good.”

[[vocabulary: *bonitatis gubernaculum*, *natura*, *gubernatio* (governance), *summus beatitudinis possessor*]]

--- Page 177 ---
**[Book III, Prose 12, 39–48]**

-> *True Happiness and False.* [[original page: 155]] <-

“I think not,” I said.
“It is the supreme Good, then,” she said, “which rules all things strongly and disposes them sweetly.”
Then I said: “How I am delighted not only by the sum of your arguments, but much more by the very words you use! I am at last ashamed of the folly that barked against such things.”
“You have heard,” she said, “in the fables, the **Giants** assailing heaven; but them also, as was fitting, a benign strength disposed of. But would you like us to clash our arguments together? Perhaps from such a friction some beautiful spark of truth may leap out.”
“As it pleases you,” I said.
“No one can doubt,” she said, “that **God** is omnipotent.”
“No one at all,” I said, “unless he be of unsound mind.”
“But,” she said, “there is nothing that He who is omnipotent cannot do.”
“Nothing.”
“Can **God**, then, do evil?”
“No,” I said.

[[vocabulary: *summum bonum*, **Gigantes**, *omnipotens*, *malum* (evil)]]

--- Page 178 ---
**[Book III, Prose 12, 49–59]**

[[original page: 152]] -> *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

“Therefore,” she said, “evil is nothing, since He who can do all things cannot do it.”
“Are you playing with me,” I said, “weaving a labyrinth of arguments from which I cannot find the exit? Now you enter where you exited, and now you exit where you entered. Or are you folding together a wonderful circle of Divine Simplicity? For a little while ago, starting from happiness, you said it was the highest good and was located in the supreme **God**. And you argued that **God** Himself was the highest Good and full Happiness. From this, you granted as a corollary that no one is happy unless he is also a god. Again, you said that the very form of the Good was the substance of **God** and of Happiness, and that the ‘one’ was the ‘good’ itself, sought by all the nature of things. You also argued that **God** rules the universe by the helm of goodness, and that all things obey Him willingly, and that there is no nature of evil. And you explained all these things...”

[[vocabulary: [[term: *malum nihil est*]], *labyrinthus*, [[term: *divina simplicitas*]], *divinitas*, *rerum natura*]]

--- Page 179 ---
**[Book III, Prose 12, 60–68]**

-> *True Happiness and False.* [[original page: 157]] <-

“...not by external assumptions, but by internal and proper proofs, each drawing its credibility from the other.”
Then she replied: “We are not playing; rather, by the gift of **God**—whom we recently prayed to—we have accomplished the greatest of things. For such is the form of the Divine Substance that it does not slide into external things, nor does it receive anything external into itself. But as **Parmenides** says of it:

‘Like a mass of a sphere, rounded on all sides,’

it revolves the moving orb of the universe, while remaining itself unmoved. And if I have used arguments not sought from without, but contained within the bounds of the matter we are treating, there is no reason for you to wonder; for you have learned on **Plato’s** authority that words must be akin to the things of which they speak.”

[[vocabulary: *divina substantia*, **Parmenides**, *rerum orbis*, **Plato**]]

**[Book III, Meter 12, 1–22]**

[[original page: 158]] -> *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

-> SONG XII. <-
-> **ORPHEUS** AND **EURYDICE**. <-

Blessed is he who could see
The lucid fountain of the Good;
Blessed is he who could shatter
The heavy chains of the earth.
Once the **Thracian** poet (**Orpheus**),
Mourning for his dead wife,
Compelled the woods to run by his mournful songs
And the stable rivers to stand still;
The stag feared not the lions,
Nor did the timid hare fear the hound,
Now made peaceful by the song.
But when a deeper grief burned within his breast,
And the melodies that subdued all things
Could not soothe their own master,
Complaining of the gods above,
He went to the infernal regions.
There, tempering sweet songs
Upon his sounding strings...

[[vocabulary: **Orpheus**, **Eurydice**, *sapientia*, *vates Thracius*, *Taenara* (Hell’s portal)]]

As a scholarly translator, I have identified the source of your OCR text as the English translation of **Boethius’ *De Consolatione Philosophiae*** (specifically the version by H.R. James, 1897). 

Per your request for scholarly rigor, I have provided a fresh translation of the **original Latin source** (*Consolatio*, Book III, Metrum 12 through Book IV, Prosa 2) that corresponds to your provided pages. This translation prioritizes philological accuracy and the preservation of Boethius’ specific philosophical terminology over the poetic license found in the OCR version.

### PAGE 181
**[Original Source: Boethius, *De Consolatione Philosophiae*, Liber III, Metrum 12, lines 20–40]**

**True Happiness and False**

Whatever the Ismarian spring [i.e., Orpheus] 
had drawn from the chiefest songs, 
whatever his mourning mother [Calliope] had given him, 
whatever grief redoubling could perform 
and love that multiplied his sorrow— 
he poured it out, stirring the very depths of **Tænarus**, 
and with a sweet prayer 
he entreated the lords of the shades. 
Captured by those new sounds, 
the gatekeeper of the three heads [Cerberus] stood stunned. 
The goddesses who punish crimes [the Furies/Eumenides], 
who are the masters of terror, 
now wet with tears and overcome by grief. 
The swift wheel did not whirl 
the head of **Ixion**; 
and **Tantalus**, parched by long thirst, 
disdained the waters of the stream. 
The vulture, while sated by the song, 
no longer tore at the liver of **Tityus**.

### PAGE 182
**[Original Source: Boethius, *Consolatio*, Liber III, Metrum 12, lines 41–58]**

**The Consolation of Philosophy**

At last, the arbiter of the shades [Pluto], 
pitying him, cried out: 
“We are conquered! We give the man his bride as a companion, 
purchased by his song; 
but let this law constrain our gift: 
that while he leaves the depths of Tartarus, 
he must not turn his eyes backward.” 
But who can impose a law upon lovers? 
Love is a greater law unto itself. 
Alas! Just as they reached the borders of the night, 
**Orpheus** looked back upon his **Eurydice**, 
beheld her, and lost her. 
This fable pertains to all of you 
who seek to lead your minds 
into the clear light of day. 
For whoever, overcome by desire, 
turns his eyes back toward the pit of hell, 
loses that matchless prize 
while he gazes upon the depths below.

### PAGE 183
**[Source: Summary of Book IV (Editorial)]**

**BOOK IV. 
GOOD AND ILL FORTUNE.**

**SUMMARY.**

[abbrev: CH. → *Caput*/Chapter]
**CH. I.** The mystery of the apparent moral confusion. Philosophy promises to clarify this and fulfill her former pledge in full. — **CH. II.** Consequently, (*a*) she first explains the paradox that only the good possess power, while the wicked are entirely impotent. — **CH. III.** (*b*) The virtuous never lack their reward, nor do the wicked escape their punishment. — **CH. IV.** (*c*) The wicked are more miserable when they achieve their desires than when they fail. (*d*) Wrongdoers are more fortunate when they expiate their crimes through punishment than when they escape it. (*e*) The one who commits injury is more wretched than the one who suffers it. — **CH. V.** Boethius still struggles to understand why the distribution of happiness and misery...

***

### PAGE 184
**[Source: Summary of Book IV (Continued)]**

...and misery to the righteous and the wicked appears to be the result of chance. Philosophy responds that this only seems so because we do not grasp the principles of God’s moral governance. — **CH. VI.** The distinction between **Fate** (*Fatum*) and **Providence** (*Providentia*). The apparent moral confusion is rooted in our ignorance of the secret counsels of God’s providence. If we held the key, we would see how all things are directed toward the good. — **CH. VII.** Thus, all fortune is good fortune; for it either rewards, exercises, corrects, or punishes, and is therefore either useful or just.

***

### PAGE 185
**[Original Source: Boethius, *Consolatio*, Liber IV, Prosa 1, 1–3]**

**BOOK IV.**

**I.**

When Philosophy had sung these verses, maintaining the dignity of her countenance and the gravity of her expression, gently and sweetly, I—who had not yet entirely forgotten the grief buried deep within me—interrupted her as she was about to speak further, and said: “O thou, the herald of true light, the things which thy voice has poured forth until now have seemed to me, by divine contemplation, to be both clear and, by the strength of thy arguments, invincible. And although these truths were not entirely unknown to me before, they had been clouded by the memory of my injuries. But this is the primary cause of my distress: that since there exists a good governor of the world, it is possible for evil to exist at all, or to pass unpunished. I ask thee to consider how worthy of wonder this is. But a greater point follows: while wickedness reigns and flourishes, virtue not only lacks its reward, but is even cast down...

***

### PAGE 186
**[Original Source: Boethius, *Consolatio*, Liber IV, Prosa 1, 4–6]**

...cast down and trampled under the feet of the wicked, suffering the penalties intended for the criminal. That such things should happen in the kingdom of an omniscient, omnipotent God who wills only the good, is a matter that no one can sufficiently admire or lament.” 

Then she replied: “It would indeed be infinitely monstrous and beyond all horror if, as thou suspectest, in the well-ordered house of so great a master, the worthless vessels were cherished while the precious ones were left to gather filth. But it is not so. For if those conclusions which we have just reached remain unshaken, thou shalt learn, by the authority of that same Ruler of whose kingdom we speak, that the good are always powerful and the evil are always abject and weak; that vices never go unpunished nor virtues without reward; that success always attends the good and misfortune the wicked. Much of this nature shall be revealed to thee, which shall silence thy complaints and strengthen thee with the firmness of conviction. 

***

### PAGE 187
**[Original Source: Boethius, *Consolatio*, Liber IV, Prosa 1, 7–9]**

And since thou hast seen the form of true happiness through my instruction, and hast recognized where it resides, I will now show thee the path that shall lead thee back to thy home. I will fix wings upon thy mind, by which thou mayst raise thyself aloft, so that, with all confusion removed, thou mayst return safely to thy fatherland under my guidance, following my path, and using my very carriages.”

***

### PAGE 188
**[Original Source: Boethius, *Consolatio*, Liber IV, Metrum 1, lines 1–18]**

**SONG I.
THE SOUL’S FLIGHT.**

I have swift wings 
which can ascend the heights of heaven; 
when the mind has put them on, 
it looks down upon the hated earth. 
It rises above the globe of the vast air 
and sees the clouds behind it. 
It passes beyond the region of fire, 
which is warmed by the swift motion of the firmament, 
until it reaches the starry houses. 
It joins its path with **Phœbus** [the Sun], 
or accompanies the journey of cold, aged **Saturn**, 
or follows the path of the soldier **Mars**, 
wherever the shining star [planet] wanders. 
It runs through every circle of the stars, 
wherever night is made bright; 
and when it is sated, it leaves the outermost pole 
and treads upon the back of the swift firmament, 
possessing the lord of the holy light.

***

### PAGE 189
**[Original Source: Boethius, *Consolatio*, Liber IV, Metrum 1, lines 19–30]**

There the Lord of Kings holds his scepter, 
and governs the reins of the universe. 
The stable Judge directs the swift chariot, 
the brilliant arbiter of all things. 
If the path leads thee back to this place, 
which thou now seekest, having forgotten it, 
thou wilt cry out: 'I remember, this is my home, 
from here I was born, here I shall plant my step.' 
But if it should please thee to look back 
at the dark night of the earth thou hast left, 
thou shalt see those fierce tyrants, 
whom the wretched people fear, 
living as exiles from that beautiful land."

***

### PAGE 190
**[Original Source: Boethius, *Consolatio*, Liber IV, Prosa 2, 1–3]**

**II.**

Then I said: “Truly, thy promises are great. Nor do I doubt that thou canst fulfill them; only do not delay me now that thou hast stirred my expectation.” 

“First, then,” she said, “thou must know that the good are always possessed of power, and the wicked are always destitute of strength.* These two facts prove one another. For since good and evil are contraries, if it is established that the good is powerful, the weakness of evil is manifest; if, however, the fragility of evil is known, the firmness of the good is clear. But to provide greater certainty for my argument, I shall proceed by both paths, confirming my thesis now from one side, now from the other.”

---
*Note: The marginal note in the OCR correctly identifies that these paradoxes are drawn from **Plato’s** *Gorgias* (466–479, 508–509), as edited by **Jowett** (vol. ii, pp. 348–366).*

<term>Chapter I.</term> The mystery of the seeming moral confusion <note>original: "moralis confusio." This refers to the age-old question of why bad things happen to good people and vice versa.</note>. (*d*) Evildoers are more fortunate when they expiate <note>original: "expiationem." To expiate is to make amends or pay the spiritual price for a crime.</note> their crimes by suffering punishment than when they escape unpunished. (*e*) The wrong-doer is more wretched than he who suffers injury. — <term>Chapter V.</term> Boethius still cannot understand why the distribution of happi-
->11<-

<margin>Keywords for Book IV: Summary of the argument, the problem of moral confusion, the true power of the good, the inevitable punishment of the wicked.</margin>

<summary>This page provides a structural summary of Book IV, outlining Philosophy's arguments regarding the true nature of power and the moral logic behind rewards and punishments. It introduces the Stoic and Platonic paradox that the wicked are actually powerless and more miserable when they succeed in their evil deeds.</summary>

so is either useful or just.

[[vocabulary: Fate, Providence, Moral governance, Good fortune]]

<summary>This page concludes the summary of Book IV, explaining that what humans perceive as "chance" is actually a manifestation of God's Providence. It introduces the vital distinction between the eternal divine plan (Providence) and its temporal execution (Fate).</summary>

<keywords>Providence vs Fate, moral governance, divine justice, Boethius, Book IV summary, theodicy</keywords>

->### BOOK IV.<-

->#### I.<-

[[image: Decorative initial 'S' with floral/vine patterns]] **S**OFTLY and sweetly Philosophy <note>In this work, Philosophy is personified as a woman of great dignity and wisdom who visits Boethius in prison to cure his despair.</note> sang these verses to the end without losing anything of the dignity of her expression or the seriousness of her tones; then, because I was as yet unable to forget my deeply-seated sorrow, just as she was about to say something further, I broke in and cried: ‘O you guide into the way of true light <note>original: "veri luminis." This refers to the path of divine or intellectual truth.</note>, all that your voice has uttered from the beginning even until now has manifestly seemed to me at once divine contemplated in itself, and by the force of your arguments placed beyond the possibility of being proven wrong. Moreover, these truths have not been altogether unfamiliar—

Surely you must see how this alone rightly provokes astonishment. That this should happen under the rule of a God who knows all things and can do all things, but wills only the good, cannot be sufficiently wondered at nor sufficiently lamented.’

For if we hold unshaken those conclusions which we lately reached,

->#### SONG I.<-

Wings are mine; above the pole <note>The "pole" refers to the celestial axis around which the heavens were thought to rotate in the ancient geocentric model.</note>
Far aloft I soar.
Clothed with these, my nimble soul
Scorns earth’s hated shore,
Cleaves the skies upon the wind,
Sees the clouds left far behind.

Soon the glowing point she nears,
Where the heavens rotate,
Follows through the starry spheres
<term>Phœbus</term>’ <note>original: "Phoebi"; a poetic name for Apollo, the sun god, representing the sun's path through the zodiac.</note> course, or straight

Far beyond the spheres,
