**Key Vocabulary:**
**Soul's flight:** The Neoplatonic concept of the mind returning to its divine origin.
**Celestial spheres:** The concentric layers of the universe (Moon, Sun, Planets, Stars).
**Phœbus:** The Sun.
**Saturn & Mars:** Planets representing the further reaches of the physical heavens.
**Fount of light:** The spiritual center of the universe; God.

<summary>In this poem, Lady Philosophy describes the journey of the soul as it leaves the physical world, passing the sun and planets to reach the ultimate divine source. It serves as a metaphor for the philosophical mind rising above earthly suffering to find peace in universal truth.</summary>

--- Page 190 ---
<meta>This page marks the transition from the poetic description of the soul’s ascent to a logical prose argument. Following the previous page’s conclusion that tyrants are truly in "exile" from their spiritual home, Philosophy now begins to prove that wickedness is a form of weakness rather than power.</meta>

[[original page: 168]]

->*The Consolation of Philosophy.*<-

->#### II.<-

Then I said: ‘Truly, your promises are wonderfully great; yet I do not doubt that you can fulfill them. Only, do not keep me in suspense now that you have raised such hopes.’

‘First, then, learn,’ she said, ‘how power always accompanies the good, while the wicked are left completely without strength. <note>Philosophy introduces a core paradox of ancient thought: that those who seem powerful (like tyrants) are actually weak because they cannot achieve the ultimate goal of human existence—true happiness.</note> These two truths prove each other; for since good and evil are opposites, if it is shown that goodness is power, the weakness of evil is clearly seen. Conversely, if the fragile nature of evil is revealed, the strength of goodness is made known. However, to make my conclusion even more believable, I will examine both paths, confirming my points first from one side and then from the other.’

<note>The logical paradoxes in this chapter and Chapter IV are drawn from [[term: Plato]]’s dialogue, the *[[term: Gorgias]]*. This work explores the idea that it is better to suffer injustice than to commit it, and that the "powerful" but unjust man is actually the most miserable of all. See [[term: Benjamin Jowett]]’s translation (a famous 19th-century scholar), volume 2, pages 348-366 and 400-401 (corresponding to the standard *Gorgias* citations 466-479 and 508-509).</note>

<summary>Philosophy begins a logical proof to demonstrate that goodness is synonymous with power and evil with weakness. She bases her argument on the principle of contraries, preparing to use Platonic logic to console Boethius.</summary>

<keywords>Boethius, Philosophy, goodness as power, weakness of evil, Plato, Gorgias, paradox, Benjamin Jowett, moral strength</keywords>

--- Page 191 ---
[[original page: 169]]

-> *Good and Ill Fortune.* <-

[IV, Pr. 2] ...and I shall draw confirmation for my statements from both sides. For the effectuation of any human action depends upon two things—namely, will (*voluntas*) and power (*potestas*); if either be lacking, nothing can be brought to fruition. For if the will be absent, no one attempts to do what they do not desire; whereas if power fails, the will is frustrated. Thus, if you see someone wishing to obtain what they do not obtain, you cannot doubt that they lacked the power to acquire what they willed.’

‘That is clear,’ I said, ‘nor can it be denied.’

‘And if you see someone accomplish what they willed, can you doubt they had the power?’

‘By no means.’

‘Therefore, in that which a man is able to do, he is to be judged strong; but in that which he cannot do, he must be deemed weak?’

‘I admit it,’ I replied.

‘Do you remember, then, that it was concluded from our earlier reasonings...’

[[vocabulary: Boethius, Philosophia, Fortuna, Voluntas, Potestas]]

--- Page 192 ---
[[original page: 170]]

-> *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

‘...that the entire intent of the human will, though pursued by diverse means, hastens toward happiness (*beatitudo*)?’

‘I remember,’ I said, ‘that this also was demonstrated.’

‘And do you recall that happiness is the Good itself, and thus, when happiness is sought, it is the Good that is desired by all?’

‘I do not merely recall it,’ I said, ‘I hold it fixed in my memory.’

‘Therefore, all men, both good and evil, strive to reach the Good by a common intent?’

‘That follows.’

‘But it is certain that men become good by the attainment of good?’

‘It is.’

‘Do the good, then, obtain what they desire?’

‘So it seems.’

‘But if the evil were to attain the good they seek, they could no longer be evil?’

‘No.’

‘Therefore, since both seek the good, but the former attain it while the latter do not, can there be any doubt that the good are powerful, while those who are evil are weak?’

[[vocabulary: Beatitudo, Summum Bonum, Virtus, Consolatio Philosophiae]]

--- Page 193 ---
[[original page: 171]]

-> *Good and Ill Fortune.* <-

‘If anyone doubts this, he is unable to consider the nature of things or the logical consequences of the arguments.’

‘Again,’ she said, ‘if there are two things to which the same function is prescribed by nature, and one of them performs that function by its natural action, but the other cannot exercise that natural power, but instead—in a manner not suited to its nature—imitates the function it cannot fulfill: which of these two would you judge more powerful?’

‘Though I suspect your meaning,’ I said, ‘I desire you to explain it more fully.’

‘The act of walking is a natural motion for men, is it not?’

‘Certainly.’

‘And you do not doubt that the duty of this motion belongs naturally to the feet?’

‘I do not doubt it.’

‘If, then, a man who is able to walk on his feet progresses, and another, whose natural function of the feet is missing, attempts to walk upon his hands...’

[[vocabulary: Officium, Natura, Ratio]]

--- Page 194 ---
[[original page: 172]]

-> *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

‘...which of these two would you rightly consider the stronger?

‘Proceed,’ I said, ‘for no one doubts that he who can perform a natural action is stronger than he who cannot.’

‘Now, the Supreme Good is proposed as the end for the good and the evil alike; but the good seek it through the natural action of the virtues, while the evil strive to attain it through various desires (*cupiditates*), which is not the natural way of attaining the Good. Or do you think otherwise?’

‘Not at all; for the conclusion is manifest. From what I have granted, it must follow that the good are powerful and the wicked are powerless.’

‘You run ahead correctly,’ she said, ‘and as physicians hope, it is a sign of a nature that is resisting and repelling disease. But because I see you are so quick to perceive, I shall multiply the arguments. Consider the great weakness of vicious men, who cannot even reach that goal to which the very aim of nature leads and almost compels them.’

[[vocabulary: Cupiditas, Virtus, Natura, Impotentia]]

--- Page 195 ---
[[original page: 173]]

-> *Good and Ill Fortune.* <-

‘What if they were deprived of this powerful and nearly invincible aid of nature’s guidance? Consider how great is the impotence of the wicked. For the prizes they seek are not light or trivial [1]; they fail to achieve the very sum and crown of things. The wretched fail to obtain that for which they labor day and night; and in this very failure, the strength of the good is made manifest. For as you would judge him a most potent walker who could reach on foot a place beyond which there was no further path, so must you judge him most powerful who attains the end of all things to be desired, beyond which there is nothing. From which it follows that the wicked are seen to be destitute of all strength.’

[1] *Translator's Note: The Latin text here alludes to Virgil:* “neque enim levia aut ludicra petuntur / praemia” (*Aeneid XII, 764-5*), *which in turn references the Iliad (XXII, 159-162) regarding the race for the life of Hector/Turnus.*

[[vocabulary: Virgilius, Aeneis, Homerus, Ilias, Turnus, Conington]]

--- Page 196 ---
[[original page: 174]]

-> *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

‘For why do they leave virtue and follow vice? Is it from ignorance of the Good? But what is more feeble than the blindness of ignorance? Do they know what they should follow, but are cast down by lust? If so, they are weak through incontinence, being unable to struggle against vice. Do they knowingly and willfully desert the Good and turn to vice? In this way, they not only cease to be powerful, but they cease to be at all. For those who abandon the common end of all things that are, likewise cease to exist. This may seem strange to some—that we say the evil, who constitute the majority of men, do not exist—but such is the fact. For I do not deny that those who are evil are evil; but I deny that they *exist* [Lat. *esse*] purely and simply. For as you might call a corpse a "dead man," but cannot call it simply a "man," so I grant that the vicious are evil, but that they *are* [exist] absolutely, I cannot concede.’

[[vocabulary: Vitium, Libido, Incontinentia, Esse, Essentia]]

--- Page 197 ---
[[original page: 175]]

-> *Good and Ill Fortune.* <-

‘For a thing exists which maintains its order and preserves its nature; whatever falls away from this, deserts the existence which is seated in its nature. But, you will say, the wicked have "power." I do not deny it, but this power of theirs is derived not from strength but from weakness. They have the power to do evil, which they could not do if they retained the power to do good. This "possibility" clearly demonstrates that they can do nothing; for if evil is nothing, as we have established, then since they can only do evil, the wicked can do nothing.’

‘This is evident.’

‘And so that you may understand the nature of this power: we defined earlier that nothing is more powerful than the Supreme Good?’

‘True,’ I said.

‘But the Supreme Good cannot do evil?’

‘By no means.’

‘Is there anyone, then, who thinks men can do all things?’

‘No one but a madman.’

[[vocabulary: Efficacia, Summum Bonum, Malum, Possibilitas]]

--- Page 198 ---
[[original page: 176]]

-> *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

‘Yet men can do evil?’

‘Would that they could not!’ I exclaimed.

‘Since, then, he who can do only good is omnipotent, while those who can do evil are not omnipotent, it is manifest that they who can do evil have less power. Furthermore, all power is to be counted among desirable things, and all desirable things are referred to the Good as the peak of their nature. But the possibility of committing crime cannot be referred to the Good; therefore it is not desirable. Since all power is desirable, it follows that the ability to do evil is not a power. From all this, the power of the good and the undeniable weakness of the evil appear; and it is clear that Plato’s opinion was true: that only the wise are able to do what they desire, while the wicked may follow their lusts, but what they truly desire, they cannot achieve. For they do what they please, thinking to reach the Good through the pleasures they follow; but they do not reach it, for shameful things do not lead to happiness.’

[[vocabulary: Omnipotens, Facinus, Plato, Beatitudo]]

--- Page 199 ---
[[original page: 177]]

-> *Good and Ill Fortune.* <-

SONG II.
-> THE BONDAGE OF PASSION. <-

[IV, Met. 2]
The kings you see sitting on high,
In the pride of their purple robes,
Hedged in by grim weapons,
Breathing out cruel threats from a fierce heart;
If one should strip from these proud ones
The coverings of their empty honors,
You would see that the lords within
Are weighed down by heavy chains.
For here, Lust poisons the heart;
Here, turbid Wrath raises its waves and lashes the mind;
Here, Sorrow wearys the captive,
Or slippery Hope torments them.
When one head is driven by so many tyrants,
He cannot do what he wills,
This wretch who is pressed down by such cruel masters.

[[vocabulary: Passio, Libido, Maeror, Servitium, Vincit]]

--- Page 200 ---
[[original page: 178]]

-> *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

III.

[IV, Pr. 3] ‘Do you see, then, in what a slough of wickedness the shameful are rolled, and with what splendor the righteous shine? From this it is manifest that the good are never without their rewards, nor the wicked without their punishments. For in every action, that for the sake of which the action is performed is rightly termed the reward—as when one runs a race, the crown for which he runs is the reward. But we have shown that happiness is the Good for which all things are done. Therefore, the Good itself is set as the common reward for all human actions. And this reward can never be separated from the good man. For he who lacks the Good can no longer be called good; wherefore, honest conduct never lacks its own prize. No matter how much the wicked may rage, the crown shall not fall from the head of the wise...’

[[vocabulary: Probrum, Praemium, Poena, Facinus, Beatitudo]]

--- Page 201 ---
### Translator’s Note
The text provided for translation is an early 20th-century English translation of Boethius’ *De consolatione philosophiae* (specifically, the translation by H.R. James, first published in 1897). As the source text is already in English, I have provided a scholarly transcription and editorial presentation that preserves the formatting, headers, and linguistic nuances of the period edition while noting abbreviations and technical markers found in the OCR.

***

### [PAGE 201]
**Original Page: 179**
**Header: Good and Ill Fortune.**

wise, nor wither. Verily, other men's unrighteousness cannot pluck from righteous souls their proper glory. Were the reward in which the soul of the righteous delighteth received from without, then might it be taken away by him who gave it, or some other; but since it is conferred by his own righteousness, then only will he lose his prize when he has ceased to be righteous. Lastly, since every prize is desired because it is believed to be good, who can account him who possesses good to be without reward? And what a prize, the fairest and grandest of all! For remember the corollary which I chiefly insisted on a little while back, and reason thus: Since absolute good is happiness, it is [Abbrev: *'tis*] clear that all the good must be happy for the very reason that they are good. But it was agreed that those who are happy are gods. So, then, the prize of the good is one which no time may impair, no man's power lessen, no man's unrighteousness tarnish; it is [Abbrev: *'tis*] very Godship. And this being so, the wise man cannot doubt that punishment is inseparable from the bad. For since good and bad, and

[Printer’s Mark: 12—2]

***

### [PAGE 202]
**Original Page: 180**
**Header: The Consolation of Philosophy.**

likewise reward and punishment, are contraries, it necessarily follows that, corresponding to all that we see accrue as reward of the good, there is some penalty attached as punishment of evil. As, then, righteousness itself is the reward of the righteous, so wickedness itself is the punishment of the unrighteous. Now, no one who is visited with punishment doubts that he is visited with evil. Accordingly, if they were but willing to weigh their own case, could *they* think themselves free from punishment whom wickedness, worst of all evils, has not only touched, but deeply tainted?

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;'See, also, from the opposite standpoint—the standpoint of the good—what a penalty attends upon the wicked. Thou didst learn a little since that whatever is is one, and that unity itself is good. Accordingly, by this way of reckoning, whatever falls away from goodness ceases to be; whence it comes to pass that the bad cease to be what they were, while only the outward aspect is still left to show they have been men. Wherefore [Note: *OCR reads "Wherewhere"*], by their perversion to badness, they have lost their true human nature. Further, since

***

### [PAGE 203]
**Original Page: 181**
**Header: Good and Ill Fortune.**

righteousness alone can raise men above the level of humanity, it must needs be that unrighteousness degrades below man's level those whom it has cast out of man's estate. It results, then, that thou canst not consider him human whom thou seest transformed by vice. The violent despoiler of other men's goods, enflamed with covetousness, surely resembles a wolf. A bold and restless spirit, ever wrangling in law-courts, is like some yelping cur. The secret schemer, taking pleasure in fraud and stealth, is own brother to the fox. The passionate man, phrenzied with rage, we might believe to be animated with the soul of a lion. The coward and runaway, afraid where no fear is, may be likened to the timid deer. He who is sunk in ignorance and stupidity lives like a dull ass. He who is light and inconstant, never holding long to one thing, is for all the world like a bird. He who wallows in foul and unclean lusts is sunk in the pleasures of a filthy hog. So it comes to pass that he who by forsaking righteousness ceases to be a man cannot pass into a Godlike condition, but actually turns into a brute beast.'

**IV.**

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Then said I : ‘This is very true. I see that the vicious, though they keep the outward form of man, are rightly said to be changed into beasts in respect of their spiritual nature ; but, inasmuch as their cruel and polluted minds vent their rage in the destruction of the good, I would this license were not permitted to them.’
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;‘Nor is it,’ said she, ‘as shall be shown in the fitting place. Yet if that license which thou believest to be permitted to them were taken away, the punishment of the wicked would be in great part remitted. For verily, incredible as it may seem to some, it needs must be that the bad are more unfortunate when they have accomplished their desires than if they are unable to get them fulfilled. If it is wretched to **will** evil, to have been able to accomplish evil is more wretched ; for without the power the wretched will would fail of

***

### [PAGE 207]
**Original Page: 185**
**Header: Good and Ill Fortune.**

effect. Accordingly, those whom thou seest to will, to be able to accomplish, and to accomplish crime, must needs be the victims of a threefold wretchedness, since each one of these states has its own measure of wretchedness.’
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;‘Yes,’ said I ; ‘yet I earnestly wish they might speedily be quit of this misfortune by losing the ability to accomplish crime.’
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;‘They will lose it,’ said she, ‘sooner than perchance thou wishest, or they themselves think likely ; since, verily, within the narrow bounds of our brief life there is nothing so late in coming that anyone, least of all an immortal spirit, should deem it long to wait for. Their great expectations, the lofty fabric of their crimes, is oft overthrown by a sudden and unlooked-for ending, and this but sets a limit to their misery. For if wickedness makes men wretched, he is necessarily more wretched who is wicked for a longer time ; and were it not that death, at all events, puts an end to the evil doings of the wicked, I should account them wretched to the last degree. Indeed, if we have formed true conclusions about

***

### [PAGE 208]
**Original Page: 186**
**Header: The Consolation of Philosophy.**

the ill fortune of wickedness, that wretchedness is plainly infinite which is doomed to be eternal.’
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Then said I : ‘A wonderful inference, and difficult to grant ; but I see that it agrees entirely with our previous conclusions.’
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;‘Thou art right,’ said she ; ‘but if anyone finds it hard to admit the conclusion, he ought in fairness either to prove some falsity in the premises, or to show that the combination of propositions does not adequately enforce the necessity of the conclusion ; otherwise, if the premises be granted, nothing whatever can be said against the inference of the conclusion. And here is another statement which seems not less wonderful, but on the premises assumed is equally necessary.’
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;‘What is that ?’
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;‘The wicked are happier in undergoing punishment than if no penalty of justice chasten them. And I am not now meaning what might occur to anyone—that bad character is amended by retribution, and is brought into the right path by the terror of punishment, or that it serves as an

***

### [PAGE 209]
**Original Page: 187**
**Header: Good and Ill Fortune.**

example to warn others to avoid transgression ; but I believe that in another way the wicked are more unfortunate when they go unpunished, even though no account be taken of amendment, and no regard be paid to example.’
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;‘Why, what other way is there beside these ?’ said I.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Then said she : ‘Have we not agreed that the good are happy, and the evil wretched ?’
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;‘Yes,’ said I.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;‘Now, if,’ said she, ‘to one in affliction there be given along with his misery some good thing, is he not happier than one whose misery is misery pure and simple without admixture of any good ?’
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;‘It would seem so.’
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;‘But if to one thus wretched, one destitute of all good, some further evil be added besides those which make him wretched, is he not to be judged far more unhappy than he whose ill fortune is alleviated by some share of good ?’
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;‘It could scarcely be otherwise.’
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;‘Surely, then, the wicked, when they are punished, have a good thing added to

***

### [PAGE 210]
**Original Page: 188**
**Header: The Consolation of Philosophy.**

them—to wit, the punishment which by the law of justice is good ; and likewise, when they escape punishment, a new evil attaches to them in that very freedom from punishment which thou hast rightly acknowledged to be an evil in the case of the unrighteous.’
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;‘I cannot deny it.’
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;‘Then, the wicked are far more unhappy when indulged with an unjust freedom from punishment than when punished by a just retribution. Now, it is manifest that it is just for the wicked to be punished, and for them to escape unpunished is unjust.’
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;‘Why, who would venture to deny it ?’
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;‘This, too, no one can possibly deny—that all which is just is good, and, conversely, all which is unjust is bad.’
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Then I answered : ‘These inferences do indeed follow from what we lately concluded ; but tell me,’ said I, ‘dost thou take no account of the punishment of the soul after the death of the body ?’

<meta>This page completes the sentence started on page 179: "For since good and bad, and likewise reward and punishment, are contraries..." It continues the dialogue where Lady Philosophy explains the metaphysical consequences of wickedness to the prisoner, Boethius.</meta>

->180 *The Consolation of Philosophy.*<-

likewise reward and punishment, are contraries <note>Opposites that cannot exist in the same place at the same time.</note>, it necessarily follows that, corresponding to all that we see accrue as reward of the good, there is some penalty attached as punishment of evil. As, then, righteousness itself is the reward of the righteous, so wickedness itself is the punishment of the unrighteous. Now, no one who is visited with punishment doubts that he is visited with evil. Accordingly, if they were but willing to weigh their own case, could *they* think themselves free from punishment whom wickedness, worst of all evils, has not only touched, but deeply tainted?

     'See, also, from the opposite standpoint—the standpoint of the good—what a penalty attends upon the wicked. Thou didst learn a little since that whatever is is one, and that <term>unity <note>In this philosophical system, to exist is to be a "unity" or a whole. Since God is the ultimate Unity and the ultimate Good, anything that moves away from goodness becomes "fragmented" and loses its claim to true existence.</note></term> itself is good. Accordingly, by this way of reckoning, whatever falls away from goodness ceases to be; whence it comes to pass that the bad cease to be what they were, while only the outward aspect is still left to show they have been men. Wherefore, by their perversion to badness, they have lost their true human nature. <note>original: "humanam amisere naturam." -> Good and Ill Fortune. 189 <-

...others by a cleansing mercy. But it is not my purpose to speak of these things at present. For we have labored thus far that you might recognize that the power of the wicked, which so greatly disturbed you, is in fact no power at all; and that you might see that those very men whom you complained were left unpunished are never actually free from the penalties of their own wickedness; and that you might learn that the license you prayed would be short-lived is not, in fact, long-enduring—and that it would be more miserable if it lasted longer, and most unhappy of all if it were eternal. Furthermore, I have sought to show that the unrighteous are more wretched when they are unjustly spared punishment than when they are restrained by a just retribution. From this principle, it follows that the wicked are burdened by more severe penalties precisely at the moment they are believed to have escaped them.”
   Then I said: “As I follow your arguments, I am struck by how they agree with the dictates of reason. Yet if I turn to the common judgments of men, I find no one who would consider them even worth listening to, let alone worthy of belief.”

[[vocabulary: purification (purgatoria), unrighteousness (improbitas), retribution (iusta retributione)]]

--- Page 212 ---
[[original page: 190]]
-> 190 The Consolation of Philosophy. <-

“It is even so,” she replied. “For their eyes, accustomed to the darkness, cannot lift themselves to the light of manifest truth. They are like those birds whose sight the night illumines and the day blinds. For as long as they look not upon the order of things, but only upon their own internal impulses, they imagine that the freedom to commit crimes and the lack of punishment are things to be desired. But consider the decree of the eternal law. If you have fashioned your mind toward better things, you have no need of a judge to award a prize; you have, by your own act, joined yourself to the more excellent order. If you have turned your desires toward baser things, seek no punisher from without; you have cast yourself down into a lower state. It is as if you were to look alternately at the squalid earth and the heavens: though all external things remain still, you would seem by the law of sight to be now in the mire and now among the stars. But the common crowd does not perceive these things. What then? Shall we join ourselves to those whom we have already proven to be like brute beasts? Suppose a man…

[[vocabulary: ordinance of eternal law (aeternae legis), common herd (vulgus), brute beasts (beluis)]]

--- Page 213 ---
[[original page: 191]]
-> Good and Ill Fortune. 191 <-

…who had utterly lost his sight were even to forget that he had ever possessed the power of vision, and should think that he lacked nothing pertaining to human perfection; should we therefore believe that those who see are themselves blind? For the common people will not even grant this: that those who commit an injury are more wretched than those who suffer one, even though this is supported by no less weight of reasoning.”
   “I would hear those very reasons,” I said.
   “Do you deny,” she asked, “that every wicked man deserves punishment?”
   “Certainly not.”
   “And that those who are wicked are unhappy is evident in many ways?”
   “It is clear,” I replied.
   “Then you do not doubt that those who are worthy of punishment are indeed wretched?”
   “That is consistent,” I said.
   “If, then, you were sitting as a judge, upon whom would you think punishment should be inflicted: upon him who did the wrong, or him who suffered it?”

[[vocabulary: faculty of vision (visendi lumine), wretched (miser)]]

--- Page 214 ---
[[original page: 192]]
-> 192 The Consolation of Philosophy. <-

“I have no doubt that I would satisfy the sufferer by the punishment of the wrongdoer.”
   “Therefore, the inflictor of the injury would seem to you more miserable than the victim?”
   “It follows logically.”
   “For this and other reasons based on the same principle—namely, that wickedness by its very nature makes men wretched—it is manifest that the misery lies with the one who commits the injustice, not the one who endures it.”
   “And yet,” she continued, “the practice of modern advocates is the reverse. They strive to move the judges to pity for those who have suffered some harsh or grievous injury; whereas pity is more rightly due to the perpetrators. These ought to be led to judgment not by angry accusers, but by those moved by compassion and kindness—as if they were sick men being led to a physician—so that the disease of their guilt might be lanced by punishment. Under such a system, the work of the advocate would either cease entirely or, if men wished to be of service to humanity, it would be turned toward the office of accusation. The wicked themselves, if…

[[vocabulary: commiseration (misericordia), advocate (orator), ulcer of fault (culpae morbus)]]

--- Page 215 ---
[[original page: 193]]
-> Good and Ill Fortune. 193 <-

…through some small chink they were permitted to look upon the virtue they have abandoned, and could see that they might cast off the filth of their vices through the pains of punishment in order to attain the reward of righteousness, they would no longer consider these sufferings to be painful. They would reject the assistance of advocates and entrust themselves entirely to their accusers and their judges. From this it follows that among the wise, there is no place left for hatred. For only the most foolish would hate the good; and to hate the evil is irrational. For if a vice is a disease of the soul, much like a sickness of the body, then just as we do not think those who are physically ill deserve our hatred but rather our pity, so much more should we pity those whose minds are afflicted by a wickedness more cruel than any bodily ailment.”

-> 13 <-

[[vocabulary: vicious propensity (vitiositas), sickness of the soul (animae morbus)]]

--- Page 216 ---
[[original page: 194]]
-> 194 The Consolation of Philosophy. <-

-> SONG IV. <-

-> THE UNREASONABLENESS OF HATRED. <-

Why does it please you to stir such great strife,
And to summon your fate with your own hand?
If it is death you seek, it approaches of its own accord;
It does not delay its swift-winged steeds.

Those whom the wild beasts and the serpent attack,
Men yet seek to strike with the sword.
Is it because their customs and lands differ
That they join in unjust and bloody battle?

Such a reason for slaughter is not just.
Would you render to each his rightful merit?
Then love the good with a righteous love,
And let your heart feel pity for the bad.

[[vocabulary: coursers (equos), murderous steel (ferro)]]

--- Page 217 ---
[[original page: 195]]
-> Good and Ill Fortune. 195 <-

-> V. <-

   To this I said: “I see now what happiness or what misery is found in the deserts of both the righteous and the wicked. Yet I perceive that there is also something of good or evil in the fortune that befalls the common people. For surely no wise man would prefer to be an exile, impoverished and disgraced, rather than to remain in his own city, flourishing in wealth, powerful in office, and strong in influence. For the office of wisdom is fulfilled in a more manifest and clear way when the happiness of those who rule is somehow communicated to the people they govern; and this is especially true since the prison, the law, and the other penalties of legal torture are more rightly intended for those harmful citizens for whom they were devised. Therefore, I marvel greatly why the order of things is so completely inverted: why the punishments for crimes weigh upon the good…

-> 13—2 <-

[[vocabulary: deserts (meritis), mischievous citizens (perniciosis civibus)]]

--- Page 218 ---
[[original page: 196]]
-> 196 The Consolation of Philosophy. <-

…while the wicked seize the rewards of virtue. I desire to know from you what the reason for such an unjust confusion could be. I would wonder less if I believed that all things were mixed by the randomness of chance. But now, my amazement is increased by the knowledge that God is the governor. Since He often distributes pleasant things to the good and bitter things to the bad, but conversely grants harshness to the good and fulfills the desires of the wicked, what distinguishes this from the accidents of chance, unless the underlying cause be understood?”
   “It is no wonder,” she replied, “if something is thought to be random and confused when the principle of its order is unknown. But although you may be ignorant of the causes that direct so great a system, nevertheless, because a good Governor rules the world, do not doubt that all things are done rightly.”

[[vocabulary: governance (regimine), principle of order (ratio ordinis)]]

--- Page 219 ---
[[original page: 197]]
-> Good and Ill Fortune. 197 <-

-> SONG V. <-

-> WONDER AND IGNORANCE. <-

Whoever knows not how the stars of Arcturus
Glide in their course near the highest pole,
Will marvel why Bootes drives his wain
So slowly and plunges his flames so late into the sea,
Though he rises again so quickly to the dawn;
The laws of the high heaven will strike him with awe.

And when the moon, with its full horn,
Grows pale, infected by the shadows of the night,
And the stars, which she had hidden with her face,
Now reappear, the confused nations
Clamor and beat the air with frequent brass.*

No one marvels when the blasts of the North Wind
Beat upon the shore with the surging wave,
Nor when the hardened mass of snow
Is melted by the fervor of the sun;
For the causes here are open and clear.
But things hidden trouble the mind with wonder.

* Refers to the ancient custom of making noise during an eclipse to drive away the shadow.

[[vocabulary: Bootes, Wain (plaustra), full-orbèd moon (plena cornua), astonied (stupet)]]

--- Page 220 ---
[[original page: 198]]
-> 198 The Consolation of Philosophy. <-

The fickle crowd is struck with astonishment
By everything that is rare or occurs late.
But if the cloud of ignorance should vanish
And the errors of the mind be cleared away,
Then that which appears wonderful would cease
To be a matter of amazement.

[[vocabulary: dull herd (vulgus), enlightened minds (sapientia)]]

--- Page 221 ---
-> *Good and Ill Fortune.*<- ->199<-

->VI.<-

"It is so," I said. "But since it is your office to unfold the hidden causes of things and to explain principles veiled in darkness, I beg you to determine your conclusions in this matter, for this marvel especially disturbs my mind."

Then she, smiling slightly for a moment, said: "You call me to the greatest of all inquiries, for which a most exhaustive discourse is scarcely sufficient. For such is its nature that, when one doubt is cut away, innumerable others grow back, like the heads of the Hydra; nor would there be any limit, unless one should restrain them with the most vibrant fire of the mind. For in this matter, the questions of the simplicity of Providence, the sequence of Fate, sudden chance, Divine knowledge and predestination, and the freedom of the will are typically raised.

[[vocabulary: Hydra, providentia (Providence), fatum (Fate), praedestinatio (Predestination)]]

--- Page 222 ---
->200<- ->*The Consolation of Philosophy.*<-

How great the weight of these things is, you yourself can perceive. But because it is part of the medicine for your malady to know these things, although we are confined by narrow limits of time, we shall attempt to touch upon them to some extent. Furthermore, even if you find delight in the pleasures of song, you must postpone that pleasure for a short time, while I weave together the connected train of reasons in their proper order."

"As you please," I said.

Then, as if taking a new beginning, she spoke thus: "The generation of all things, and the whole progression of changing natures, and whatever is moved in any way, receives its causes, its order, and its form from the stability of the Divine Mind. This Mind, established in the citadel of its own simplicity, has decreed a manifold mode for governing things. This mode, when it is perceived in the purity of the Divine Intelligence itself, is called *Providence*; but when it is referred to those things which it moves and disposes, it was called *Fate* by the ancients.

[[vocabulary: liberum arbitrium (freedom of the will), mens divina (Divine mind), providentia (providence)]]

--- Page 223 ---
->*Good and Ill Fortune.*<- ->201<-

That these two are different will easily be clear if one considers the efficacy of each. For Providence is the Divine Reason itself, seated in the Supreme Ruler of all, which disposes all things; but Fate is the inherent disposition in moving things, through which Providence binds all things together in their orders. For Providence embraces all things together, however different, however infinite; but Fate sets in motion individual things distributed by places, forms, and times. 

Therefore, this unfolding of temporal order, unified in the foresight of the Divine Mind, is Providence; but that same unity, distributed and unfolded in time, is called Fate. Although these are different, one depends upon the other; for the order of Fate proceeds from the simplicity of Providence. For just as a craftsman, perceiving the form of the thing to be made in his mind, carries out the effect of his work and leads through temporal moments that which he held simply and instantly in his mind—so God disposes all things to be done through Providence, simply and stably, but administers those very things he has disposed through Fate in a manifold and temporal way.

[[vocabulary: fatum (fate), providentia (providence), fati series (order of destiny), artifex (artificer/craftsman)]]

--- Page 224 ---
->202<- ->*The Consolation of Philosophy.*<-

Whether, therefore, Fate is carried out by certain Divine spirits as the ministers of Providence, or by some soul, or by the service of all nature, or by the celestial motions of the stars, or by the power of angels, or by the various cunning of demons, or by any or all of these—the series of Fate is woven. This, at least, is certain: that Providence is the immovable and simple form of things to be done, while Fate is the shifting bond and temporal order of those things which the Divine Simplicity has disposed to be done. 

Thus it is that all things which are subject to Fate are also subject to Providence, to which Fate itself is subordinate. But some things, which are established under Providence, transcend the chain of Fate. These are the things which, fixed stably in proximity to the primary Divinity, are beyond the order of the mobility of Fate. For as among several circles revolving around the same center, the innermost approaches the simplicity of the middle point and acts as a sort of pivot for the others which revolve around it; but the outermost, whirled in a wider circuit, the further it departs from the indivisible center of the point, the more it is spread out through broader spaces—

[[vocabulary: spiritus divini (Divine spirits), angeli (angels), daemones (demons), prima divinitas (primal Divinity)]]

--- Page 225 ---
->*Good and Ill Fortune.*<- ->203<-

—while, on the other hand, if anything joins and unites itself to that center, it is gathered into simplicity and ceases to be scattered or to flow away. In the same way, that which departs further from the Primary Mind is bound by the greater entanglements of Fate, and a thing is free from Fate to the degree that it seeks more closely that center of things. And if it cleaves to the stability of the Supreme Mind, being beyond movement, it also transcends the necessity of Fate. Therefore, as reasoning is to the intellect, as that which is generated is to that which is, as time is to eternity, as the circle is to the center—so is the moving sequence of Fate to the stable simplicity of Providence. 

That sequence moves the heaven and the stars, tempers the elements among themselves in turn, and transforms them by alternate change. It renews all things that are born and die through the similar progressions of offspring and seeds. This same sequence binds the actions and fortunes of men by an indissoluble bond of causes; and since it proceeds from the origins of immovable Providence, it is necessary that these causes also be immutable.

[[vocabulary: mens prima (primal mind), fati necessitas (fate's necessity), fati series (causal series)]]

--- Page 226 ---
->204<- ->*The Consolation of Philosophy.*<-

For in this way, the world is best ruled, if the simplicity abiding in the Divine Mind produces an inflexible order of causes. This order, by its own immutability, restrains those mutable things which would otherwise flow about at random. Hence it is that, although to you who are not capable of considering this order, all things seem confused and disordered, nevertheless the proper measure of all things directs them toward the good. 

For there is nothing which is done for the sake of evil, not even by the wicked themselves; as has been abundantly demonstrated, they seek the good but are led astray by perverse error. Much less does the order proceeding from the center of the Supreme Good turn aside from its own beginning anywhere. 

'But,' you will say, 'what confusion could be more unjust than that both prosperity and adversity should happen to the good, and that the wicked should possess what they desire and suffer what they hate?' 

[[vocabulary: nexus causarum (nexus of causality), providentia immobilis (unalterable providence), immutabilis (immutable)]]

--- Page 227 ---
->*Good and Ill Fortune.*<- ->201<-

Do men possess such great health of mind that those whom they judge to be righteous or wicked must necessarily be as they think? For on this point the judgments of men disagree; those whom some judge worthy of reward, others judge worthy of punishment. But even granted that someone could discern the good and the bad, would he be able to look into the internal temperament of the soul, so to speak—borrowing a metaphor from the body? 

For to one who does not know, it is a marvel why sweet things are suitable for some healthy bodies and bitter things for others; and why some sick people are helped by mild remedies and others by sharp ones. But the physician, who knows the measure and temperament of health and sickness, does not wonder. Now, what is the health of souls but righteousness? And what is their sickness but vice? Who else is the preserver of the good and the driver-away of the evil but God, the guide and physician of minds?

[[vocabulary: prospera/adversa (prosperity/adversity), iustitia (righteousness), animae salus (soul's health)]]

--- Page 228 ---
->206<- ->*The Consolation of Philosophy.*<-

He, looking forth from the high watch-tower of His Providence, sees what is suitable for each person and applies what He knows to be appropriate. 

From this arises that notable miracle of the order of Fate: that something is done by the Knower at which the ignorant wonder. For, to touch upon a few points of the Divine depth which human reason is able to grasp: here is one whom you judge to be most just and most observant of equity, yet to the all-knowing Providence, the opposite seems true. Our Lucan reminded us that the winning cause pleased the gods, but the conquered cause pleased Cato. 

Therefore, whatever you see happen here contrary to your expectation—it is a right order in the things themselves, but a perverse confusion in your own opinion. But let us grant that there is someone so well-constituted that God and men agree in their judgment of him. 

[[vocabulary: providentiae specula (watch-tower of providence), fati ordinis miraculum (mystery of destiny), Lucan, Cato]]

--- Page 229 ---
->*Good and Ill Fortune.*<- ->207<-

Yet he is perhaps weak in strength of mind; so that if any adversity should befall him, he might cease to cultivate that innocence through which he was unable to retain his fortune. Therefore, the wise Dispensation spares him whom adversity might make worse, lest He permit him to suffer who is not fit for labor. Another is perfect in all virtue, holy and close to God; Providence judges it a crime that any adversity should befall him, to the point that He does not even allow him to be afflicted by bodily diseases. For as one more excellent [than I]* has said: 

> 'The body of the holy man is built of ether.' [1]

It often happens that the government is given to the good so that the overflowing wickedness of men may be restrained. To others He distributes a mixture of fortunes according to the quality of their souls; He troubles some lest they grow arrogant through long prosperity; others He allows to be exercised by hardships so that they may strengthen the virtues of the mind by the use and exercise of patience.

[[notes: * Philosophy refers to Parmenides.]]
[[vocabulary: innocentia (innocency), dispensatio (dispensation), sanctus vir (holy saint), aether (ether), Parmenides]]

--- Page 230 ---
->208<- ->*The Consolation of Philosophy.*<-

Some fear more than they can bear what they are able to carry; others despise more than they can bear what they are unable to carry; these He leads to the knowledge of themselves through affliction. Some have purchased a great name for the ages at the price of a glorious death; some, remaining undefeated by punishments, have given an example to others that virtue cannot be conquered by evils. All of which things, there is no doubt, happen rightly and in order, and for the benefit of those to whom they are seen to happen. 

For that other side—that the wicked sometimes suffer hardships and sometimes obtain what they desire—also proceeds from the same causes. No one wonders that the wicked are afflicted, for all think they deserve ill. Their punishments indeed both deter others from crimes and amend those upon whom they are inflicted. But the prosperity of the wicked provides a great argument to the good: what they ought to judge of this kind of happiness, which they see often serves the wicked.

[[vocabulary: patientia (patience), infortunia (misfortune), invicti (invincible constancy), prosperitas (prosperity)]]

***
**Translator's Notes:**
1. **Unclear Reading/Allusion:** Philosophy quotes Parmenides here: *"Ανδρος ιερον δεμας αιθερος οικοδομηται"* (The holy body of a man is built of ether). Boethius uses this to illustrate the soul's proximity to the divine.
2. **Proper Nouns:** Lucan and Cato are preserved as requested; they refer to the Roman poet and the Stoic statesman respectively, specifically regarding their conflicting views on the Roman Civil War.
3. **Scholarly Rigor:** The term "Simplicity" (*simplicitas*) is used in the technical Neoplatonic sense, meaning "indivisibility" or "oneness," rather than "easiness."

--- Page 231 ---
[[language: English]]
[[original page: 209]]

-> *Good and Ill Fortune.* <-

[There is another cause for] fortune of this kind, which often attends the wicked so assiduously. 

There is another object which may, I believe, be attained in such cases: there is one, perhaps, whose nature is so reckless and violent that poverty would drive him more desperately into crime. *His* disorder providence [Lat. *providentia*] relieves by allowing him to amass money. Such a one, in the uneasiness of a conscience stained with guilt, while he contrasts his character with his fortune, perchance grows alarmed lest he should come to mourn the loss of that whose possession is so pleasant to him. He will, then, reform his ways, and through the fear of losing his fortune he forsakes his iniquity. Some, through a prosperity unworthily borne, have been hurled headlong to ruin; to some the power of the sword has been committed, to the end that the good may be tried by discipline, and the bad punished. For while there can be no peace between the righteous and the wicked, neither can the wicked agree among themselves. How should they, when each is at variance with himself, because his vices rend his 

-> 14 <-

[[vocabulary: providence, iniquity, prosperity]]

--- Page 232 ---
[[language: English]]
[[original page: 210]]

-> 210 *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

conscience, and ofttimes they do things which, when they are done, they judge ought not to have been done. Hence it is that this supreme providence [Lat. *summa providentia*] brings to pass this notable marvel—that the bad make the bad good. For some, when they see the injustice which they themselves suffer at the hands of evil-doers, are inflamed with detestation of the offenders, and, in the endeavour to be unlike those whom they hate, return to the ways of virtue. It is the Divine power [Lat. *divina vis*] alone to which things evil are also good, in that, by putting them to suitable use, it bringeth them in the end to some good issue. For order in some way or other embraceth all things, so that even that which has departed from the appointed laws of the order, nevertheless falleth within *an* order, though *another* order, that nothing in the realm of providence may be left to haphazard. But 

> '"Hard were the task, as a god, to recount all, 
> nothing omitting."' [Note: Boethius quotes Homer, *Iliad* xii. 176]

Nor, truly, is it lawful for man to compass in thought all the mechanism of the Divine 

[[vocabulary: providence, virtue, Divine power]]

--- Page 233 ---
[[language: English]]
[[original page: 211]]

-> *Good and Ill Fortune.* <-

work, or set it forth in speech. Let us be content to have apprehended this only—that God, the creator of universal nature, likewise disposeth all things, and guides them to good; and while He studies to preserve in likeness to Himself all that He has created, He banishes all evil from the borders of His commonweal [Lat. *reipublicae*] through the links of fatal necessity. Whereby it comes to pass that, if thou look to disposing providence, thou wilt nowhere find the evils which are believed so to abound on earth. 

'But I see thou hast long been burdened with the weight of the subject, and fatigued with the prolixity of the argument, and now lookest for some refreshment of sweet poesy. Listen, then, and may the draught so restore thee that thou wilt bend thy mind more resolutely to what remains.'

[[vocabulary: commonweal, fatal necessity, prolixity]]

-> SONG VI. <-

-> THE UNIVERSAL AIM. <-

Wouldst thou with unclouded mind 
View the laws by God designed, 
Lift thy steadfast gaze on high 
To the starry canopy; 
See in rightful league of love 
All the constellations move. 
Fiery Sol, in full career, 
Ne'er obstructs cold Phœbe's sphere; 
When the Bear [Lat. *Arctos*], at heaven's height, 
Wheels his coursers' rapid flight, 
Though he sees the starry train 
Sinking in the western main, 
He repines not, nor desires 
In the flood to quench his fires. 
In true sequence, as decreed, 
Daily morn and eve succeed; 
Vesper brings the shades of night, 
Lucifer the morning light. 
Love, in alternation due, 
Still the cycle doth renew, 
And discordant strife is driven 
From the starry realm of heaven.

[[vocabulary: Phœbe, the Bear, Vesper, Lucifer]]

--- Page 235 ---
[[language: English]]
[[original page: 213]]

-> *Good and Ill Fortune.* <-

Thus, in wondrous amity, 
Warring elements agree; 
Hot and cold, and moist and dry, 
Lay their ancient quarrel by; 
High the flickering flame ascends, 
Downward earth for ever tends. 
So the year in spring's mild hours 
Loads the air with scent of flowers; 
Summer paints the golden grain; 
Then, when autumn comes again, 
Bright with fruit the orchards glow; 
Winter brings the rain and snow. 
Thus the seasons' fixed progression, 
Tempered in a due succession, 
Nourishes and brings to birth 
All that lives and breathes on earth. 
Then, soon run life's little day, 
All it brought it takes away. 
But One sits and guides the reins, 
He who made and all sustains; 
King and Lord and Fountain-head, 
Judge most holy, Law most dread; 
Now impels and now keeps back, 
Holds each waverer in the track. 
Else, were once the power withheld 
That the circling spheres compelled

In their orbits to revolve, 
This world's order would dissolve, 
And th' harmonious whole would all 
In one hideous ruin fall. 
But through this connected frame 
Runs one universal aim; 
Towards the Good do all things tend, 
Many paths, but one the end. 
For naught lasts, unless it turns 
Backward in its course, and yearns 
To that Source [Lat. *exordium*] to flow again 
Whence its being first was ta'en.

-> VII. <-

'Dost thou, then, see the consequence of all that we have said?' 

'Nay; what consequence?' 

'That absolutely every fortune is good fortune.' 

'And how can that be?' said I. 

'Attend,' said she. 'Since every fortune, welcome and unwelcome alike, has for its object the reward or trial of the good, and the punishing or amending of the bad, every fortune must be good, since it is either just or useful.' 

'The reasoning is exceeding true,' said I, 'the conclusion, so long as I reflect upon the providence and fate of which thou hast taught me, based on a strong foundation. Yet, with thy leave, we will count it among those which just now thou didst set down as paradoxical [Lat. *paradoxa*].' 

'And why so?' said she. 

'Because ordinary speech is apt to

[[vocabulary: fortune, paradoxical, providence]]

--- Page 238 ---
[[language: English]]
[[original page: 216]]

-> 216 *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

assert, and that frequently, that some men's fortune is bad.' 

'Shall we, then, for awhile approach more nearly to the language of the vulgar [Lat. *vulgarem sermonem*], that we may not seem to have departed too far from the usages of men?' 

'At thy good pleasure,' said I. 

'That which advantageth thou callest good, dost thou not?' 

'Certainly.' 

'And that which either tries or amends advantageth?' 

'Granted.' 

'Is good, then?' 

'Of course.' 

'Well, this is *their* case who have attained virtue and wage war with adversity, or turn from vice and lay hold on the path of virtue.' 

'I cannot deny it.' 

'What of the good fortune which is given as reward of the good—do the vulgar adjudge it bad?' 

'Anything but that; they deem it to be the best, as indeed it is.' 

'What, then, of that which remains, which, though it is harsh, puts the restraint 

[[vocabulary: vulgar, adversity, virtue]]

--- Page 239 ---
[[language: English]]
[[original page: 217]]

-> *Good and Ill Fortune.* <-

of just punishment on the bad—does popular opinion deem it good?' 

'Nay; of all that can be imagined, it is accounted the most miserable.' 

'Observe, then, if, in following popular opinion, we have not ended in a conclusion quite paradoxical.' 

'How so?' said I. 

'Why, it results from our admissions that of all who have attained, or are advancing in, or are aiming at virtue, the fortune is in every case good, while for those who remain in their wickedness fortune is always utterly bad.' 

'It is true,' said I; 'yet no one dare acknowledge it.' 

'Wherefore,' said she, 'the wise man ought not to take it ill, if ever he is involved in one of fortune's conflicts, any more than it becomes a brave soldier to be offended when at any time the trumpet sounds for battle. The time of trial is the express opportunity for the one to win glory, for the other to perfect his wisdom. Hence, indeed, virtue [Lat. *virtus*] gets its name, because, relying on its own efficacy [Lat. *viribus*], it yieldeth not to adversity. And ye who 

[[vocabulary: paradoxical, virtue, efficacy]]

--- Page 240 ---
[[language: English]]
[[original page: 218]]

-> 218 *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

have taken your stand on virtue's steep ascent, it is not for you to be dissolved in delights or enfeebled by pleasure; ye close in conflict—yea, in conflict most sharp—with all fortune's vicissitudes, lest ye suffer foul fortune to overwhelm or fair fortune to corrupt you. Hold the mean with all your strength. Whatever falls short of this, or goes beyond, is fraught with scorn of happiness, and misses the reward of toil. It rests with you to make your fortune what you will. Verily, every harsh-seeming fortune, unless it either disciplines or amends, is punishment [Lat. *poena*].'

[[vocabulary: vicissitudes, disciplines, amends]]

--- Page 241 ---
[[original page: 219]]

-> *On Fortune, Prosperous and Adverse.* <-

#### SONG VII.
#### THE ASCENT OF THE HERO.

For twice five years the war was waged, 
    While the harsh son of Atreus [1] 
    Sought to purge his brother’s 
    Stolen bed with Ilium’s ruins.

Yet when he sought to give the Grecian fleet 
    The winds it lacked, he bought the gale with blood; 
    The king cast off the father’s heart 
    And stained his hands with his own daughter’s gore. [2]

Ithaca’s lord [3] wept for his lost 
    Companions, whom the savage Cyclops, 
    Immured within his cavernous hall, 
    Gorged into his monstrous belly.

Yet the giant, frantic with his sightless wound, 
    Paid for that grim and bloody joy 
    With tears of pain—his vision quenched 
    By the hand of the wily Odysseus.

Next, the labors of the mighty Hercules: 
    He quelled the Centaurs' haughty pride; 
    He stripped the Lion of its golden pelt 
    To clothe himself in noble spoils.

[1] *Atrides*: Agamemnon.
[2] Referring to the sacrifice of Iphigenia at Aulis.
[3] *Odysseus/Ulysses*.

[[vocabulary: Ilium, Atrides, Odysseus, Cyclops, Polyphemus, Alcides (Hercules), Centaur]]

-> *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

With unerring arrows he reached the birds [1] 
    In their flight; he stole the apples 
    From the dragon’s sleepless watch; 
    With triple chain he dragged the Hound of Hell. [2]

He gave the flesh of the cruel master 
    To be the fodder for his own fierce steeds; [3] 
    He scorched the heads of the many-necked Hydra; 
    Beneath his own waters, the river-god 
        Achelous 
    Hid his brow, shorn of its strength. [4]

On Libya’s burning sands he laid 
    The giant Antæus low; he slew 
    The thief Cacus to appease the master's wrath; 
    And the bristles of the great Boar 
    Were stained with its own foaming gore.

For his final toil, his shoulders bore 
    The weight of the celestial sphere; 
    Nor did his neck bend beneath the load; 
    By this last labor, he merited 
    The heights of the heaven he sustained.

Go forth then, brave ones, where the path 
    Of high example leads! Why falter? 
    Why turn your backs in cowardly flight? 
    Earth conquered gives the stars as your reward.

--- Page 243 ---
[[original page: [unnumbered]]]

### BOOK V.

### ON THE COEXISTENCE OF CHANCE, 
### FREE WILL, AND DIVINE 
### FOREKNOWLEDGE.

#### SUMMARY.

[[meta: This summary corresponds to the transition from Book IV to Book V.]]

**CH. I.** Boethius inquires whether anything may be truly attributed to chance (*casus*). Philosophy, following the Peripatetic definition of Aristotle (*Physics* II, 4), explains that chance is not the absence of cause, but the unexpected convergence of independent causes in actions undertaken for a specific purpose. This convergence is governed by an inescapable chain of order proceeding from Providence.—**CH. II.** The question of whether human freedom (*liberum arbitrium*) can exist within this causal chain. Philosophy asserts that every rational nature possesses free choice, for without judgment there is no reason. However, this freedom is greater in divine minds and diminished when entangled in earthly desires.—**CH. III.** The conflict between God’s certain foreknowledge (*praescientia*) and human freedom. Boethius argues that if God sees all things eternally, then all things must happen of necessity, rendering rewards and punishments unjust.

[[vocabulary: Boethius, Aristotle, casus (chance), providentia (providence), liberum arbitrium (free will)]]

--- Page 244 ---
[[original page: 222]]

-> *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

If human actions are necessitated by divine foresight, then the soul’s merit is void, and the very act of prayer becomes a futility. God would then appear as the author of both virtue and vice.—**CH. IV.** Philosophy explains that the difficulty lies in the limitation of human reason (*ratio*), which struggles to grasp the nature of divine simplicity. She notes that knowledge of a thing depends not on the nature of the object itself, but on the capacity of the one who knows. Sense, imagination, and reason all perceive the same man differently; intelligence (*intelligentia*) transcends them all.—**CH. V.** A discussion on the hierarchy of cognition. As human reason is superior to the imagination and senses, it should not submit to their limitations. We must strive to rise toward the heights of the supreme intelligence, where the eye of God perceives all things as a present reality.—**CH. VI.** The definition of Eternity (*Aeternitas*): the total, simultaneous, and perfect possession of unending life. God, being eternal, views all temporal changes as if in an ever-present "now." This foreknowledge does not impose necessity upon future events any more than our seeing an event in the present imposes necessity upon its occurrence. A distinction is drawn between *absolute necessity* (e.g., that the sun must rise) and *conditional necessity* (e.g., that a man must be walking if he is perceived to be walking).

[[vocabulary: praescientia, ratio, intelligentia, aeternitas, necessitas (necessity)]]

--- Page 245 ---
[[original page: 223]]

-> *Summary.* <-

Events which occur through the freedom of the will are known to God as necessary in a conditional sense—because they are present to His sight—but remain free in their own nature. Thus, the integrity of the will remains, and the moral order of rewards and punishments is preserved. Man is subject to a profound responsibility, for all things are done before the eyes of a Judge who sees all.

[[vocabulary: voluntas (will), providentia]]

[[meta: Blank page.]]

### BOOK V.

#### I.

[[image: decorative drop-cap letter S]] HE had spoken, and was now 
directing the course of her 
discourse to treat and resolve 
certain other matters, when I 
interrupted: ‘Thy exhortation is indeed 
rightful, and carries the full weight of 
thine authority. But that which thou 
once calledst a most profound question 
regarding Providence, I now find to be 
beset by many other difficulties. I ask 
of thee whether thou thinkest there is 
aught at all that we may call "chance," 
and if so, what its nature might be.’
    Then she replied: ‘I hasten to fulfill 
my debt and to open to thee the path 
by which thou mayest be restored to thy 
true homeland. And though these 
matters are indeed profitable to know, 
-> 15 <-

[[vocabulary: providentia, casus]]

--- Page 248 ---
[[original page: 226]]

-> *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

they are somewhat removed from the 
direct path of our inquiry. I fear lest, 
diverted by side-paths, thou shouldst be 
too weary to reach the end of the 
primary journey.’
    ‘Have no such fear,’ I said. ‘For me, 
it is a form of rest to understand those 
things in which I take the greatest 
delight. Furthermore, when every part 
of thy argument has stood firm with 
unshakable credit, I can have no doubt 
concerning what remains.’
    She answered: ‘I will grant thy 
request,’ and she began thus: ‘If 
"chance" be defined as an event 
produced by a random motion without 
any chain of causes, I affirm that chance 
is nothing at all. I judge it to be an 
empty word, signifyng nothing in 
reality. For what place can be left for 
randomness where God has constrained 
all things into a system of order? For 
that "nothing comes from nothing" 
is a truth that none of the ancients 
denied, though they applied it not to 
the efficient cause, but to the material 
subject—this being the very foundation 
of all their laws of nature.

[[vocabulary: "ex nihilo nihil", causalis series (causal chain)]]

--- Page 249 ---
[[original page: 227]]

-> *Free Will and Foreknowledge.* <-

But if something were to arise without 
causes, it would appear to have 
emerged from nothing; and if this is 
impossible, then it is likewise 
impossible for there to be "chance" 
of the kind we have just defined.’
    ‘What then?’ I asked. ‘Is there 
nothing that may be rightly called 
chance or accident? Or is there something, 
hidden from the common sort of men, 
to which these terms apply?’
    ‘My friend Aristotle,’ she said, 
‘defined it in his *Physics* with reasons 
both brief and near to the truth.’
    ‘In what manner?’ I asked.
    ‘Whenever,’ she said, ‘something is 
done for the sake of a particular 
purpose, and some result other than 
the one intended occurs for certain 
reasons, that is called "chance." As, 
for example, if a man should dig the 
earth for the purpose of cultivation 
and find a weight of buried gold. Such 
a find is indeed believed to have 
happened by chance, yet it does not 
proceed from nothing (*ex nihilo*), for it 
has its own causes, the unforeseen 
and unexpected confluence of which 
produced this "accident." For if the 
cultivator had not been digging, 
-> 15—2 <-

[[vocabulary: accidens (accident), Aristoteles, Physica]]

--- Page 250 ---
[[original page: 228]]

-> *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

and if he who hid the money had not 
buried it in that precise spot, the gold 
would not have been found. These, then, 
are the causes of the fortuitous 
gain: it proceeds from the meeting and 
concurrence of causes, and not from 
the intention of the actor. For neither 
the one who buried the gold nor the 
one who worked the field intended 
that the money should be found; but, 
as I said, it coincided that the one 
dug where the other had buried it. We 
may therefore define chance as an 
unexpected result of concurring causes 
in those things which are done for the 
sake of some purpose. This 
concurrence and meeting of causes is 
brought about by that inevitable 
order which, proceeding from the 
source of Providence, disposes all 
things in their proper places and 
times.’

[[vocabulary: concursus causarum (concurrence of causes), fatalis series (inevitable order)]]
