->*On Freedom and Foreknowledge*<-

->METER I.<-

->CHANCE<-

Where the Achaemenian crag, where the Parthian, 
In flight, turns to wound the pursuer with his shafts, 
The Tigris and Euphrates rise from one source, 
And soon, with divided waters, they part asunder. 
Should they meet again, and into a single stream 
Call back their once-severed currents, 
Then all things which the two rivers bear along— 
Uprooted timber and ships driven by the flood— 
Would meet in the confluence; the random waves 
Would weave together these wandering fragments. 
Yet the slope of the earth and the law of the 
Downward-gliding water govern these shifting 
Entanglements. So too, Chance, which seems 
To flow with unbridled reins, is subject to a 
Rule, and proceeds by the guidance of Law.

[[Note: *Rupis Achaemeniae* (Achaemenian crag) refers to the Persian highlands. The "one source" is a poetic convention rather than a geographic fact.]]

--- Page 252 ---
**[Boethius, *De Consolatione Philosophiae*, V, p. 2]**

->230 *The Consolation of Philosophy*<-

“I observe it,” I said, “and I agree that it is as you say. But in this series of clinging causes, is there any liberty of our will, or does the chain of Fate bind the very movements of the human soul?”

“There is liberty,” she replied, “for there could not exist any nature endowed with reason unless it also possessed the freedom of the will. For that which by its nature can use reason has the judgment by which it can discern each thing; of itself, therefore, it distinguishes between those things which are to be avoided and those which are to be desired. Now, what a man judges is to be desired, he seeks; but what he judges is to be avoided, he flees. Wherefore, in those beings who possess reason, there is also the liberty of willing and refusing. But this liberty, I maintain, is not equal in all. 

[[vocabulary: *series catenatis causis* (series of linked causes), *liberum arbitrium* (free will/choice), *iudicium* (judgment/discernment)]]

--- Page 253 ---
**[Boethius, *De Consolatione Philosophiae*, V, p. 2 continued]**

->*On Freedom and Foreknowledge*<-

For the supreme and Divine substances possess a piercing judgment, an uncorrupted will, and a ready and effective power to perform what they desire. Human souls, however, must be more free when they preserve themselves in the contemplation of the Divine Mind, but less free when they collapse into bodies, and still less free when they are bound by earthly limbs. The final state is that of utter servitude, when, having fallen from the possession of their own reason, they are surrendered to vices. For once they have turned their eyes away from the light of the highest truth to lower and darkened things, they are soon clouded by the mist of ignorance and are thrown into turmoil by ruinous passions; by yielding and assenting to these, they strengthen the slavery they have brought upon themselves, and are in a manner captives of their own liberty. Nevertheless, the eye of Providence, which beholds all things from eternity, sees this, and disposes all predestined things according to their respective merits.

[[abbrev: *omni-videntis* → all-seeing]]

--- Page 254 ---
**[Boethius, *De Consolatione Philosophiae*, V, m. 2]**

->METER II.<-

->THE TRUE SUN<-

Homer with his honeyed tongue 
Sings of the Sun [Phœbus] as bright and pure; 
Yet the Sun’s rays cannot pierce 
To the bowels of the sea, 
Nor the innermost parts of the earth. 
Not so with the Creator 
Of the great world’s orb. 
To Him, as He looks from above, 
No mass of earth obstructs the view; 
No night, with black clouds, resists Him. 
In a single glance of His mind, 
He sees what is, what has been, 
And what is yet to come. 
Because He alone sees all things, 
You may truly call Him the Sun.

[[Note: The Latin uses *Phœbus* (Phoebus) for the physical sun; the "True Sun" is the Divine Mind.]]

--- Page 255 ---
**[Boethius, *De Consolatione Philosophiae*, V, p. 3]**

->*On Freedom and Foreknowledge*<-

->III.<-

Then I said: “Behold, I am now confused by an even more difficult doubt.” 
“What is it?” she asked, “though I already guess what it is that troubles you.”
“It seems,” I said, “to be a manifest contradiction and paradox that God should foreknow all things and yet that there should be any freedom of the will. For if God foresees all things and can in no way be deceived, then that which Providence has foreseen will happen must necessarily come to pass. Wherefore, if God foreknows from eternity not only the deeds of men, but even their plans and volitions, there will be no liberty of the will; for no deed or desire can exist except that which an infallible Divine Providence has perceived beforehand. For if these things could be turned aside into an outcome other than that which was foreseen, then there would no longer be a firm foreknowledge of the future, but rather an uncertain opinion; but to believe this of God, I judge to be an impiety.”

[[vocabulary: *incongruens* (contradiction), *praescit* (foreknows), *opinio* (opinion/conjecture)]]

--- Page 256 ---
**[Boethius, *De Consolatione Philosophiae*, V, p. 3 continued]**

->234 *The Consolation of Philosophy*<-

“Nor do I find convincing that argument by which some believe they can untie this knot. For they say that a thing does not happen because Providence has foreseen it is to happen, but rather the opposite: that because something is about to happen, it cannot be hidden from Divine Providence. In this way, they think the necessity is shifted to the opposite side. They say it is not necessary that what is foreseen should happen, but it is necessary that what happens should be foreseen—as if the question were which is the cause of the other: is the foreknowledge the cause of the necessity of future events, or is the necessity of future events the cause of the foreknowledge? 

But we are struggling to demonstrate this: that regardless of the order of causes, the occurrence of things foreknown is necessary, even if the foreknowledge does not seem to impose that necessity of occurrence upon the event itself.”

[[vocabulary: *fati nodum* (the knot of fate), *causa necessitatis* (cause of necessity)]]

--- Page 257 ---
**[Boethius, *De Consolatione Philosophiae*, V, p. 3 continued]**

“For instance, if a man sits, the opinion that considers him to be sitting must be true; and conversely, if the opinion that a man sits be true, he must necessarily be sitting. In both cases, therefore, there is necessity: in the one, the necessity of sitting; in the other, the necessity of truth. But the man does not sit because the opinion is true; rather, the opinion is true because the sitting preceded it. Thus, although the cause of truth proceeds from one side, there is nevertheless a common necessity in both. 

Manifestly, we must reason the same way concerning Providence and future things. For even if they are foreseen because they are going to happen, and do not happen because they are foreseen, it is nonetheless necessary that they be foreseen by God because they are going to happen, and that they happen because they are foreseen—which is enough to destroy the freedom of our will.”

[[Note: Boethius is arguing against the idea that the "temporal" can cause the "eternal".]]

--- Page 258 ---
**[Boethius, *De Consolatione Philosophiae*, V, p. 3 continued]**

“Furthermore, how preposterous it is to say that the occurrence of temporal things is the cause of eternal knowledge! For to think that God foresees future things because they are going to happen is nothing else than to think that those past events are the cause of that supreme Providence. 

Again, just as when I know that something *is*, it is necessary that it be; so when I know that something *will be*, it is necessary that it will be. Thus it follows that the occurrence of a foreknown thing cannot be avoided. Finally, if anyone thinks a thing to be otherwise than as it is, that is not only not knowledge, but it is a fallacious opinion far removed from the integrity of truth. 

Wherefore, if something is so to happen that its outcome is not certain or necessary, how can it be foreknown that it will happen? For just as knowledge itself is untainted by falsity, so that which is conceived by knowledge cannot be otherwise than as it is conceived.”

[[vocabulary: *preposterum* (preposterous/inverted), *fallax opinio* (fallacious opinion)]]

--- Page 259 ---
**[Boethius, *De Consolatione Philosophiae*, V, p. 3 continued]**

“For the reason why knowledge is free from falsehood is that each thing must necessarily be exactly as knowledge comprehends it to be. In what way, then, could God foreknow these uncertain future events? For if He judges that those things will inevitably happen which possibly may not happen, He is deceived; but to think this, or to say it, is impious. 

If, however, He perceives that they will happen in such a way that He knows they are equally capable of not happening, what kind of foreknowledge is this, which grasps nothing certain, nothing stable? How does this differ from that ridiculous prophecy of Teiresias? 

> 'Whatever I say, will either be or not be.'

In what way would Divine Providence surpass the mere opinions of men, if God judges as uncertain those things whose outcome is in fact uncertain? But if at that most certain Source [Fountain-head] of all things there can be no shadow of uncertainty...”

[[Note: The quote 'Quidquid dicam, aut erit aut non' is a reference to Horace’s *Satires* II.5.59, mocking the ambiguity of oracles.]]

--- Page 260 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, Boethius is exploring the logical conflict between God's absolute foreknowledge and the existence of human free will. He has just argued that if God knows an event will happen, it must happen by necessity, but if it happens by necessity, there is no room for human choice.</meta>

->238 *The Consolation of Philosophy*<-

For this, indeed, is the cause why knowledge is free from falsehood, because of necessity each thing must correspond exactly with the knowledge which grasps its nature. In what way, then, are we to suppose that God foreknows these uncertainties as about to come to pass? For if He thinks of events which possibly may not happen at all as inevitably destined to come to pass, He is deceived; and this it is not only **impious** <note>original: *impium*; to suggest that God could be mistaken was considered a grave theological error or even a sin by Boethius.</note> to believe, but even so much as to express in words. If, on the other hand, He sees them in the future as they are—in such a sense as to know that they may equally come to pass or not—what sort of foreknowledge is this which comprehends nothing certain nor fixed? What better is this than the absurd **vaticination** <term>vaticination: the act of prophesying or predicting the future</term> of Teiresias <note>A famous blind prophet of Greek mythology. Boethius is referencing a satire by the Roman poet Horace (Satires II.5.59), where Teiresias makes a prediction so vague it is useless.</note>?

->“‘Whate’er I say<-
->Shall either come to pass—or not.’”<-
<note>original Latin: *Quidquid dicam, aut erit aut non*. Boethius uses this quote to mock the idea of a God whose "knowledge" is merely a guess.</note>

In that case, too, in what would **Divine providence** <term>Divine providence: the protective care and guardianship of God over the universe</term> surpass human opinion if it holds for uncertain things the occurrence of which is uncertain, even as men do? But if at that perfectly sure **Fountain-head** <note>A metaphor describing God as the absolute, pure source of all truth, existence, and knowledge.</note> of all things no shadow of uncertainty can [remain]—

| Vocabulary | Definition |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Impious** | Irreverent; lacking respect for God or religious principles. |
| **Vaticination** | A prediction or the act of prophesying. |
| **Teiresias** | A blind seer of Thebes, legendary for his long life and wisdom. |
| **Divine providence** | The concept of God's intervention in the world. |
| **Fountain-head** | The primary source of something; the origin. |

<summary>Boethius argues that God’s knowledge must be certain to be divine; if God only saw future events as "possible," His knowledge would be no better than human guesswork or vague, useless prophecies.</summary>
<keywords>foreknowledge, free will, Divine Providence, epistemology, Teiresias, logic, necessity</keywords>

--- Page 261 ---
[[original page: 239]]
[[meta: Book V, Prose 3 (cont.)]]

-> Free Will and Foreknowledge. 239 <-

...[if it] can possibly be found, then the occurrence of those things which He has surely foreknown as coming is certain. Wherefore there can be no freedom in human actions and designs; but the Divine mind, which foresees all things without possibility of mistake, ties and binds them down to one only issue. But this admission once made, what an upset of human affairs manifestly ensues! Vainly are rewards and punishments proposed for the good and bad, since no free and voluntary motion of the will has deserved either one or the other; nay, the punishment of the wicked and the reward of the righteous, which is now esteemed the perfection of justice, will seem the most flagrant injustice, since men are determined either way not by their own proper volition, but by the necessity of what must surely be. And therefore neither virtue nor vice is anything, but rather good and ill desert are confounded together without distinction. Moreover, seeing that the whole course of events is deduced from providence, and nothing is left free to human design, it comes to pass that our vices also are re-

[[vocabulary: foreknowledge (*praecognitio*), human actions (*humanis actibus*), Divine mind (*diuina mens*), rewards (*praemia*), punishments (*supplicia*), justice (*iustitia*), volition (*uoluntatis*), necessity (*necessitas*), virtue (*uirtus*), vice (*uitium*), providence (*prouidentia*)]]

--- Page 262 ---
[[original page: 240]]

-> 240 The Consolation of Philosophy. <-

ferred to the Author of all good—a thought than which none more abominable can possibly be conceived. Again, no ground is left for hope or prayer, since how can we hope for blessings, or pray for mercy, when every object of desire depends upon the links of an unalterable chain of causation? Gone, then, is the one means of intercourse between God and man—the communion of hope and prayer—if it be true that we ever earn the inestimable recompense of the Divine favour at the price of a due humility; for this is the one way whereby men seem able to hold communion with God, and are joined to that unapproachable light by the very act of supplication, even before they obtain their petitions. Then, since these things can scarcely be believed to have any efficacy, if the necessity of future events be admitted, what means will there be whereby we may be brought near and cleave to Him who is the supreme Head of all? Wherefore it needs must be that the human race, even as thou didst erstwhile declare in song, parted and dissevered from its Source, should fall to ruin.’

-> Free Will and Foreknowledge. 241 <-

-> SONG III. <-

-> TRUTH’S PARADOXES. <-

Why does a strange discordance break
   The ordered scheme’s fair harmony?
Hath God decreed ’twixt truth and truth
   There may such lasting warfare be,
That truths, each severally plain,
We strive to reconcile in vain?

Or is the discord not in truth,
   Since truth is self-consistent ever?
But, close in fleshly wrappings held,
   The blinded mind of man can never
Discern—so faint her taper shines—
The subtle chain that all combines?

Ah! then why burns man’s restless mind
   Truth’s hidden portals to unclose?
Knows he already what he seeks?
   Why toil to seek it, if he knows?
Yet, haply if he knoweth not,
Why blindly seek he knows not what?*

* Compare Plato, ‘Meno,’ 80; Jowett, vol. ii., pp. 39, 40. [Note: Refers to the *Meno* paradox: how can one seek what one does not know?]

[[term: Truth’s Paradoxes (*discors foedera rerum*)]]

-> 242 The Consolation of Philosophy. <-

Who for a good he knows not sighs?
   Who can an unknown end pursue?
How find? How e’en when haply found
   Hail that strange form he never knew?
Or is it that man’s inmost soul
Once knew each part and knew the whole?

Now, though by fleshly vapours dimmed,
   Not all forgot her visions past;
For while the several parts are lost,
   To the one whole she cleaveth fast;
Whence he who yearns the truth to find
Is neither sound of sight nor blind.

For neither does he know in full,
   Nor is he reft of knowledge quite;
But, holding still to what is left,
   He gropes in the uncertain light,
And by the part that still survives
To win back all he bravely strives.

[[meta: Book V, Prose 4]]

-> Free Will and Foreknowledge. 243 <-

-> IV. <-

Then said she: ‘This debate about providence is an old one, and is vigorously discussed by Cicero in his “Divination”; thou also hast long and earnestly pondered the problem, yet no one has had diligence and perseverance enough to find a solution. And the reason of this obscurity is that the movement of human reasoning cannot cope with the simplicity of the Divine foreknowledge; for if a conception of its nature could in any wise be framed, no shadow of uncertainty would remain. With a view of making this at last clear and plain, I will begin by considering the arguments by which thou art swayed. First, I inquire into the reasons why thou art dissatisfied with the solution proposed, which is to the effect that, seeing the fact of foreknowledge is not thought the cause of the necessity of future events, foreknowledge is not to be deemed any hindrance to the freedom of the will.

-> 16—2 <-

[[vocabulary: providence (*prouidentia*), Cicero (*M. Tullio*), Divination (*Diuinatione*), Divine foreknowledge (*diuinae praescientiae*), freedom of the will (*libertas arbitrii*)]]

--- Page 266 ---
[[original page: 244]]

-> 244 The Consolation of Philosophy. <-

Now, surely the sole ground on which thou arguest the necessity of the future is that things which are foreknown cannot fail to come to pass. But if, as thou wert ready to acknowledge just now, the fact of foreknowledge imposes no necessity on things future, what reason is there for supposing the results of voluntary action constrained to a fixed issue? Suppose, for the sake of argument, and to see what follows, we assume that there is no foreknowledge. Are willed actions, then, tied down to any necessity in *this* case?’
‘Certainly not.’
‘Let us assume foreknowledge again, but without its involving any actual necessity; the freedom of the will, I imagine, will remain in complete integrity. But thou wilt say that, even although the foreknowledge is not the necessity of the future event’s occurrence, yet it is a sign that it will necessarily happen. Granted; but in this case it is plain that, even if there had been no foreknowledge, the issues would have been inevitably certain. For a sign only indicates something which is, does not bring to pass that of which it is the

[[vocabulary: necessity (*necessitatem*), foreknown (*praesciri*), voluntary action (*uoluntarios exitus*), willed actions (*uoluntas*), freedom of the will (*libertas*)]]

--- Page 267 ---
[[original page: 245]]

-> Free Will and Foreknowledge. 245 <-

sign. We require to show beforehand that all things, without exception, happen of necessity in order that a preconception may be a sign of this necessity. Otherwise, if there is no such universal necessity, neither can any preconception be a sign of a necessity which exists not. Manifestly, too, a proof established on firm grounds of reason must be drawn not from signs and loose general arguments, but from suitable and necessary causes. But how can it be that things foreseen should ever fail to come to pass? Why, this is to suppose us to believe that the events which providence foresees to be coming were not about to happen, instead of our supposing that, although they should come to pass, yet there *was* no necessity involved in their own nature compelling their occurrence. Take an illustration that will help to convey my meaning. There are many things which we see taking place before our eyes—the movements of charioteers, for instance, in guiding and turning their cars, and so on. Now, is any one of these movements compelled by any necessity?’

[[vocabulary: necessity (*necessitate*), preconception (*praenotio*), causes (*causis*), providence (*prouidentia*), charioteers (*aurigae*)]]

--- Page 268 ---
[[original page: 246]]

-> 246 The Consolation of Philosophy. <-

‘No; certainly not. There would be no efficacy in skill if all motions took place perforce.’
‘Then, things which in taking place are free from any necessity as to their being in the present must also, before they take place, be about to happen without necessity. Wherefore there are things which will come to pass, the occurrence of which is perfectly free from necessity. At all events, I imagine that no one will deny that things now taking place were about to come to pass before they were actually happening. Such things, however much foreknown, are in their occurrence *free*. For even as knowledge of things present imports no necessity into things that are taking place, so foreknowledge of the future imports none into things that are about to come. But this, thou wilt say, is the very point in dispute—whether any foreknowing is possible of things whose occurrence is not necessary. For here there seems to thee a contradiction, and, if they are foreseen, their necessity follows; whereas if there is no necessity, they can by no means be foreknown; and thou

[[vocabulary: efficacy (*ars*), skill (*ars*), present (*praesentia*), future (*futura*), foreknown (*praecognoscantur*)]]

--- Page 269 ---
[[original page: 247]]

-> Free Will and Foreknowledge. 247 <-

thinkest that nothing can be grasped as known unless it is certain, but if things whose occurrence is uncertain are foreknown as certain, this is the very mist of opinion, not the truth of knowledge. For to think of things otherwise than as they are, thou believest to be incompatible with the soundness of knowledge.
‘Now, the cause of the mistake is this—that men think that all knowledge is cognized purely by the nature and efficacy of the thing known. Whereas the case is the very reverse: all that is known is grasped not conformably to its own efficacy, but rather conformably to the faculty of the knower. An example will make this clear: the roundness of a body is recognised in one way by sight, in another by touch. Sight looks upon it from a distance as a whole by a simultaneous reflection of rays; touch grasps the roundness piecemeal, by contact and attachment to the surface, and by actual movement round the periphery itself. Man himself, likewise, is viewed in one way by Sense, in another by Imagination, in another way, again, by Thought, in another

[[term: faculty of the knower (*facultas cognoscentium*)]]
[[vocabulary: opinion (*opinio*), knowledge (*scientia*), cognized (*cognosci*), efficacy (*ui*), sight (*uisus*), touch (*tactus*), Sense (*sensus*), Imagination (*imaginatio*), Thought (*ratio*)]]

--- Page 270 ---
[[original page: 248]]

-> 248 The Consolation of Philosophy. <-

by pure Intelligence. Sense judges figure clothed in material substance, Imagination figure alone without matter. Thought transcends this again, and by its contemplation of universals considers the type itself which is contained in the individual. The eye of Intelligence is yet more exalted; for overpassing the sphere of the universal, it will behold absolute form itself by the pure force of the mind’s vision. Wherein the main point to be considered is this: the higher faculty of comprehension embraces the lower, while the lower cannot rise to the higher. For Sense has no efficacy beyond matter, nor can Imagination behold universal ideas, nor Thought embrace pure form; but Intelligence, looking down, as it were, from its higher standpoint in its intuition of form, discriminates also the several elements which underlie it; but it comprehends them in the same way as it comprehends that form itself, which could be cognized by no other than itself. For it cognizes the universal of Thought, the figure of Imagination, and the matter of Sense, without employing Thought, Imagination, or Sense, but

[[term: Intelligence (*intelligentia*)]]
[[term: pure form (*ipsam illam simplicem formam*)]]
[[vocabulary: Sense (*sensus*), Imagination (*imaginatio*), Thought (*ratio*), Intelligence (*intelligentia*), figure (*figura*), matter (*materia*), universals (*universalia*), absolute form (*simplicem formam*), intuition (*ictu mentis*)]]

--- Page 271 ---
[[original page: 249]]

-> *Free Will and Foreknowledge.* 249 <-

...surveying all things, so to speak, under the aspect of pure form by a single flash of intuition. **Thought** [Latin: *ratio*] also, in considering the universal, embraces images and sense-impressions without resorting to Imagination or Sense. For it is Thought which has thus defined the **universal** [Latin: *universale*] from its conceptual point of view: “Man is a two-legged animal endowed with reason.” [1] This is indeed a universal notion, yet no one is ignorant that the *thing* is imaginable and presentable to Sense, because Thought considers it not by Imagination or Sense, but by means of rational conception. **Imagination** [Latin: *imaginatio*], too, though its faculty of viewing and forming representations is founded upon the senses, nevertheless surveys sense-impressions without calling in Sense, not in the way of Sense-perception, but of Imagination. See’st thou, then, how all things in cognizing use rather their own faculty than the faculty of the things which they cognize? Nor is this strange; for since every judgment is the act of the judge, it is necessary that each should accomplish its task by its own, not by another’s power.

[1] *Note: A reference to the classical definition: "Animal bipes rationale."*

--- Page 272 ---
[[original page: 250]]

-> 250 *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

-> SONG IV. <-
-> A PSYCHOLOGICAL FALLACY. <-

From the **Porch’s** [Latin: *Stoa*] murky depths
Comes a doctrine sage,
That doth liken living mind
To a written page;
Since all knowledge comes through Sense,
Graven by Experience. [2]
‘As,’ say they, ‘the pen its marks
Curiously doth trace
On the smooth unsullied white
Of the paper’s face,
So do outer things impress
Images on consciousness.’
But if verily the mind
Thus all passive lies;
If no living power within
Its own force supplies;
If it but reflect again,
Like a glass, things false and vain—

---
\* A criticism of the doctrine of the mind as a **blank sheet of paper** [Latin: *tabula rasa*] on which experience writes, as held by the Stoics in anticipation of Locke. See Zeller, ‘Stoics, Epicureans, and Sceptics,’ Reichel’s translation, p. 76.

[2] *Note: OCR "im- pressed" corrected to "impressed".*

--- Page 273 ---
[[original page: 251]]

-> *Free Will and Foreknowledge.* 251 <-

Whence the wondrous faculty
That perceives and knows,
That in one fair ordered scheme
Doth the world dispose;
Grasps each whole that Sense presents,
Or breaks into elements?
So divides and recombines,
And in changeful wise
Now to low descends, and now
To the height doth rise;
Last in inward swift review
Strictly sifts the false and true?
Of these ample potencies
Fitter cause, I ween,
Were Mind’s self than marks impressed
By the outer scene.
Yet the body through the sense
Stirs the soul’s intelligence.
When light flashes on the eye,
Or sound strikes the ear,
Mind aroused to due response
Makes the message clear;
And the dumb external signs
With the hidden forms combines.

[[image: Red oval stamp: BRENTFORD PUBLIC LIBRARY]]

--- Page 274 ---
[[original page: 252]]

-> 252 *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

-> V. <-

‘Now, although in the case of bodies endowed with sentiency the qualities of external objects affect the sense-organs, and the activity of mind is preceded by a bodily affection which calls forth the mind’s action upon itself, and stimulates the forms till that moment lying inactive within, yet, I say, if in these bodies endowed with sentiency the mind is not inscribed by mere passive affection, but of its own efficacy discriminates the impressions furnished to the body, how much more do intelligences free from all bodily affections employ in their discrimination their own mental activities instead of conforming [3] to external objects? So on these principles various modes of cognition belong to distinct and different substances. For to creatures void of motive power—shell-fish and other such creatures which cling to rocks and grow there—belongs

[3] *Note: OCR "con- forming" corrected to "conforming".*

--- Page 275 ---
[[original page: 253]]

-> *Free Will and Foreknowledge.* 253 <-

Sense alone, void of all other modes of gaining knowledge; to beasts endowed with movement, in whom some capacity of seeking and shunning seems to have arisen, Imagination also. Thought pertains only to the human race, as **Intelligence** [Latin: *intelligentia*] to Divinity alone; hence it follows that that form of knowledge exceeds the rest which of its own nature cognizes not only its proper object, but the objects of the other forms of knowledge also. But what if Sense and Imagination were to gainsay Thought, and declare that universal which Thought deems itself to behold to be nothing? For the object of Sense and Imagination cannot be universal; so that either the judgment of Reason is true and there is no sense-object, or, since they know full well that many objects are presented to Sense and Imagination, the conception of Reason, which looks on that which is perceived by Sense and particular as if it were a something “universal,” is empty of content. Suppose, further, that Reason maintains in reply that it does indeed contemplate the object of both Sense and Imagination under the form of

--- Page 276 ---
[[original page: 254]]

-> 254 *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

universality, while Sense and Imagination cannot aspire to the knowledge of the universal, since their cognizance cannot go beyond bodily figures, and that in the cognition of reality we ought rather to trust the stronger and more perfect faculty of judgment. In a dispute of this sort, should not we, in whom is planted the faculty of reasoning as well as of imagining and perceiving, espouse the cause of Reason?
‘In like manner is it that human reason thinks that Divine Intelligence cannot see the future except after the fashion in which its own knowledge is obtained. For thy contention is, if events do not appear to involve certain and necessary issues, they cannot be foreseen as certainly about to come to pass. There is, then, no foreknowledge of such events; or, if we can ever bring ourselves to believe that there is, there can be nothing which does not happen of necessity. If, however, we could have some part in the judgment of the Divine mind, even as we participate in Reason, we should think it perfectly just that human Reason should submit itself

--- Page 277 ---
[[original page: 255]]

-> *Free Will and Foreknowledge.* 255 <-

to the Divine mind, no less than we judged that Imagination and Sense ought to yield to Reason. Wherefore let us soar, if we can, to the heights of that Supreme Intelligence; for there Reason will see what in itself it cannot look upon; and that is in what way things whose occurrence is not certain may yet be seen in a sure and definite foreknowledge; and that this foreknowledge is not conjecture, but rather knowledge in its supreme simplicity, free of all limits and restrictions.’

-> SONG V. <-
-> THE UPWARD LOOK. <-

In what divers shapes and fashions do the creatures great and small
Over wide earth’s teeming surface skim, or scud, or walk, or crawl!
Some with elongated body sweep the ground, and, as they move,
Trail perforce with writhing belly in the dust a sinuous groove;

--- Page 278 ---
[[original page: 256]]

-> 256 *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

Some, on light wing upward soaring, swiftly do the winds divide,
And through heaven’s ample spaces in free motion smoothly glide;
These earth’s solid surface pressing, with firm paces onward rove,
Ranging through the verdant meadows, crouching in the woodland grove.
Great and wondrous is their variance! Yet in all the head low-bent
Dulls the soul and blunts the senses, though their forms be different.
Man alone, erect, aspiring, lifts his forehead to the skies,
And in upright posture steadfast seems earth’s baseness to despise.
If with earth not all besotted, to this parable give ear,
Thou whose gaze is fixed on heaven, who thy face on high dost rear:
Lift thy soul, too, heavenward; haply lest it stain its heavenly worth,
And thine eyes alone look upward, while thy mind cleaves to the earth!

--- Page 279 ---
[[original page: 257]]

-> *Free Will and Foreknowledge.* 257 <-

-> VI. <-

‘Since, then, as we lately proved, everything that is known is cognized not in accordance with its own nature, but in accordance with the nature of the faculty that comprehends it, let us now contemplate, as far as lawful, the character of the Divine essence, that we may be able to understand also the nature of its knowledge.
‘God is eternal; in this judgment all rational beings agree. Let us, then, consider what **eternity** [Latin: *aeternitas*] is. For this word carries with it a revelation alike of the Divine nature and of the Divine knowledge. Now, **eternity is the possession of endless life whole and perfect at a single moment** [Latin: *interminabilis vitae tota simul et perfecta possessio*]. What this is becomes more clear and manifest from a comparison with things temporal. For whatever lives in time is a present proceeding from the past to the future, and there is nothing set in

[[margin: 17]]

--- Page 280 ---
[[original page: 258]]

-> 258 *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

time which can embrace the whole space of its life together. To-morrow’s state it grasps not yet, while it has already lost yesterday’s; nay, even in the life of to-day ye live no longer than one brief transitory moment. Whatever, therefore, is subject to the condition of time, although, as **Aristotle** deemed of the world, it never have either beginning or end, and its life be stretched to the whole extent of time’s infinity, it yet is not such as rightly to be thought eternal. For it does not include and embrace the whole space of infinite life at once, but has no present hold on things to come, not yet accomplished. Accordingly, that which includes and possesses the whole fulness of unending life at once, from which nothing future is absent, from which nothing past has escaped, this is rightly called eternal; this must of necessity be ever present to itself in full self-possession, and hold the infinity of movable time in an abiding present. Wherefore they deem not rightly who imagine that on **Plato’s** principles the created world is made co-eternal with the Creator, because they are told that he...

--- Page 281 ---
-> *Free Will and Foreknowledge.* 259 <-

As for the view held by Plato,* that the world had no beginning in time and is destined to have no end: if one wishes to ascribe to it the property which Plato did, it is one thing to be drawn out through an endless life (which is what he attributed to the world), and quite another to embrace the whole of an endless life in one simultaneous presence—which is clearly the unique property of the Divine Mind. Nor should God appear more ancient than created things by some mere quantity of time, but rather by the peculiar property of His own simple nature. For the infinite movement of temporal things attempts to imitate this state of an ever-present and unmoving life; but since it cannot equal or express it, it falls from immobility into motion, and declines from the simplicity of the "now" into an infinite measure of future and past. Since it cannot possess the full capacity of its life all at once, for the very reason that it somehow never ceases to be, it seems, up

***

* Plato states the contrary in the *Timaeus* (28 [abbrev: B]), although that account of the world’s temporal beginning may perhaps be understood figuratively rather than literally. See Jowett, vol. iii., pp. 448, 449 (3rd ed.).

-> 17—2 <-

--- Page 282 ---
-> 260 *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

to a certain point, to emulate that which it cannot fully complete or express, by binding itself to whatever sort of "present" this swift and brief moment may be. Because this moment carries a certain reflected image of that abiding present, it grants to whatever it touches a semblance of existing. However, because it could not remain stable, it took to the infinite path of time; and thus it came to pass that it continues by movement a life whose plenitude it could not grasp by remaining still. Therefore, if we wish to apply names appropriate to their subjects, we should follow Plato and say that God is indeed *eternal*, but the world is *everlasting*.

Since, then, every judgment comprehends the things subject to it according to its own nature, and since the state of God is ever an eternal and present one, His knowledge also, surpassing all temporal movement, remains in the simplicity of His own present, and embracing the infinite reaches of the past and future, considers all things in His simple cognition as if they were even now being enacted. Therefore, if you would weigh the

--- Page 283 ---
-> *Free Will and Foreknowledge.* 261 <-

immediate presence by which He discerns all things, you will more rightly judge it to be not a "foreknowledge" [abbrev: *praevidentia*] as of the future, but a knowledge of a never-failing presence. Wherefore it is called not "prevision" but "providence" [abbrev: *providentia*], because, being set far apart from the lowest of things, it gazes forth upon all things as if from the highest peak of the universe. Why then do you demand that those things surveyed by the divine eye be rendered necessary, when not even men render necessary the things they see? Does your look [abbrev: *intuitus*] impose any necessity upon those things which you perceive as present?

"Not at all," I replied.

"And yet," she said, "if there is a worthy comparison between the human and the divine present: just as you see certain things in this your temporal present, He beholds all things in His eternal present. Wherefore this divine foreknowledge does not change the nature or properties of things; it simply sees things present before it just as they will one day happen as future events. Nor is its judgment confused; with one glance of the mind, it distinguishes those things which will happen necessarily from those which will happen non-necessarily. Just as when you

--- Page 284 ---
-> 262 *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

simultaneously see a man walking upon the earth and the sun rising in the heavens, although both are seen at once, you nevertheless discern them and judge the one to be voluntary, the other necessary. So also the divine gaze, looking down upon all things, does not at all disturb the quality of things which are present indeed to Him, but in the condition of time are future. From this it follows that when He knows something is to happen, and at the same time knows that it lacks any necessity of happening, this is not opinion but a knowledge based upon truth. If you say at this point that what God sees is 'about to happen' cannot fail to happen, and that what cannot fail to happen happens of necessity, and so bind me to this word 'necessity,' I will admit that this is a matter of the firmest truth, but one which scarcely any but a contemplator of the divine can approach.

--- Page 285 ---
-> *Free Will and Foreknowledge.* 263 <-

For I shall answer that the same future event, when referred to divine knowledge, is necessary; but when considered in its own nature, it is found to be utterly and absolutely free. For there are two kinds of necessity: one simple, such as the fact that it is necessary that all men are mortal; the other conditional [abbrev: *ex conditione*], as for instance: if you know a man is walking, it is necessary that he is walking. For whatever is known cannot be otherwise than as it is known; but this condition does not draw with it that other simple necessity. For this [conditional] necessity is not created by a thing’s own nature, but by the addition of the condition. No necessity forces the man who walks of his own will to take a step, although it is necessary that he walk at the moment he is walking. In the same way, if Providence sees anything as present, that must necessarily be, even if it possesses no necessity of its own nature. But God beholds those future events which proceed from free will as present. These, therefore, in relation to the divine sight, become necessary

--- Page 286 ---
-> 264 *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

by the condition of divine knowledge; but considered in themselves, they do not lose the absolute freedom of their own nature. All things, therefore, which God foreknows will come to pass, will indeed come to pass; but some of them proceed from free will. Though they happen, they do not, by existing, lose their proper nature, by which—before they happened—they were able not to have happened.

What difference does it make then, you ask, that they are not necessary, since because of the condition of divine knowledge they turn out in all respects like necessity? This is the difference: just as in the examples I proposed—the rising sun and the walking man—while they are happening, they cannot *not* happen; yet one of them, even before it happened, was bound to happen, while the other was not. So also those things which God has present: they exist without doubt, but one class of them follows from the necessity of things, the other from the power of those who do them. We have therefore rightly said that these things are necessary if referred to divine knowledge, but if considered in themselves, they are free from the bonds of necessity; just as everything that is manifest to the senses, if you refer it to the reason, is universal, but if you consider the thing in itself, it is singular [or particular]. But you will say: 'If it is placed in my power to change my purpose, I will make void providence, since I will perhaps have changed that which providence foreknows.' My answer is: You can indeed alter your purpose, but since the truth of providence sees in its presence that you can do so, and whether you will do so, and in what direction you will turn, you cannot escape the divine foreknowledge, just as you cannot escape the gaze of an eye that is present, even though you turn yourself to various actions by your own free will. What then? You will ask: 'Shall the divine knowledge be changed by my own disposition, so that when I will now this, now that, the order of providence also alternates its knowledge?'

--- Page 287 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from page 264, where Lady Philosophy distinguishes between "absolute necessity" (laws of nature) and "conditional necessity" (actions seen by God). She is addressing the paradox of how human choice can exist if God already knows the outcome.</meta>

-> *Free Will and Foreknowledge.* 265 <-

...necessity of things, others from the power of the agent <note>Here, "the agent" refers to a person making a choice. Philosophy is distinguishing between things that must happen because of nature and things that happen because a person decides to act.</note>. Quite rightly, then, have we said that these things are necessary if viewed from the standpoint of the Divine knowledge; but if they are considered in themselves, they are free from the bonds of necessity, even as everything which is accessible to sense, regarded from the standpoint of Thought <note>original: "rationis" or Reason. Philosophy is using a logic puzzle: we can think of "Man" as a general concept (universal), but we only ever see specific, individual men (particulars).</note>, is universal, but viewed in its own nature particular. 

"But," thou wilt say, "if it is in my power to change my purpose, I shall make void providence, since I shall perchance change something which comes within its foreknowledge." My answer is: Thou canst indeed turn aside thy purpose; but since the truth of providence is ever at hand to see that thou canst, and whether thou dost, and whither thou turnest thyself, thou canst not avoid the Divine foreknowledge, even as thou canst not escape the sight of a present spectator, although of thy free will thou turn thyself to various actions. Wilt thou, then, say: "Shall the Divine knowledge be changed at my discretion, so that, when I will this or that, providence changes its knowledge correspondingly?" 

[[vocabulary: Thought <term>Thought: In this context, the higher faculty of Reason that understands general truths.</term>, universal <term>universal: A general category or concept that applies to many things at once.</term>, particular <term>particular: A single, specific instance or individual thing.</term>, spectator <term>spectator: An observer; Philosophy uses this to describe God watching our actions in a "now" that encompasses all time.</term>]]

<summary>Lady Philosophy explains that God's foreknowledge does not force us to act; rather, He watches our free choices from a perspective outside of time. Just as a spectator watching a man walk doesn't "cause" the man to walk, God's "vision" of our future doesn't strip us of our freedom to change our minds.</summary>

<keywords>Providence, Free Will, Divine Foreknowledge, Boethius, Reason, Universal vs Particular, Necessity</keywords>

--- Page 288 ---
-> 266 *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

"By no means."

"Indeed, for the divine gaze anticipates every future event and twists it back [abbrev: *retorquet*] into the presence of His own proper knowledge; nor does He alternate, as you suppose, in a foreknowledge of 'this or that,' but remaining stable, He anticipates and embraces your changes in one single stroke [abbrev: *uno ictu*]. God possesses this presence of comprehending and seeing all things not from the outcome of future events, but from His own simplicity. From this is also resolved that problem which you posed a little while ago: that it is unworthy that our future acts should be said to provide the cause of God’s knowledge. For this power of knowledge, embracing all things in its own immediate presence, has itself established the measure for all things, and owes nothing to subsequent events.

Since these things are so, the freedom of the will remains intact for mortals; nor are laws unjust, since they propose rewards and punishments for wills that are free from any necessity. There remains also as a spectator from on high God, who knows all things, and the ever-present eternity of

--- Page 289 ---
-> *Free Will and Foreknowledge.* 261 <-

His vision concurs with the future quality of our actions, dispensing rewards to the good and punishments to the wicked. Not in vain are hopes and prayers placed in God; when they are right, they cannot be ineffective. Therefore, turn away from vices, cultivate virtues; lift up your mind to righteous hopes; offer humble prayers to the heights. A great necessity of righteousness is laid upon you, if you would not deceive yourselves, since you act before the eyes of a Judge who discerns all things."

--- Page 290 ---
-> 268 *The Consolation of Philosophy.* <-

### EPILOGUE.

WITHIN a short time of writing ‘The Consolation of Philosophy,’ Boethius died by a cruel death. As to the manner of his death there is some uncertainty. According to one account, he was cut down by the swords of the soldiers before the very judgment-seat of Theodoric; according to another, a cord was first fastened round his forehead, and tightened till ‘his eyes started’; he was then killed with a club.

-> *Elliot Stock, Paternoster Row, London.* <-
